The 7th Bersaglieri Regiment had only two battalions, the 10th and 11th. The 8th was disbanded in September due to losses.
For the Battleaxe scenario ONLY can this 8th battalion be used. It's worth noting the battalion did attempt to reform, but was destroyed again at Gialo (technically part of Crusader, but very far off.)
On the other hand, the 9th Bersaglieri Battalion had four battalions. It likely had three back in Italy (which appears on most orders of battle) but received a fourth in Africa.
Two of them were disbanded or destroyed due to the severe losses suffered by this regiment during Crusader.

I've since changed my mind PARTIALLY on this. Sources indicate that while the 8th was disbanded, during Crusader, an attempt was made to reform it, that failed.
So while this unit certainly didn't exist by Gazala or later, back during Crusader, it's fair to have this battalion (now of mixed support form) in the order of battle.

Hollmann commanded the 3rd Battalion, 155th Infantry Regiment (not in game) that was near the 3rd Battalions of the 255th and 347th Regiments.
Actually, this is very likely not true. His rank was only that of Oberleutnant (First Lieutenant). It would be insane to have him coxmmanding a battalion.
It's now been confirmed that the commander was a Major Meythaler/Maythaler (actual spelling likely the second, but ultimately unclear).
The commanders of the regiment's first 2 battalions haven't been researched much by me but can definitely be found out.

Many sources indicate an Oberst Zintel as commander of the 115th Motorized Regiment. This may have been the case.
But... I can't find a single mention of this guy literally anywhere else. So I don't intend to trust this.
In the unlikely world where he did exist, Zintel took control of the 115th Infantry Regiment not long before crusader began.
I was unable to find any evidence that Marcks commanded it during this time period. I've later found evidence that he took command only in December.
NOTE: This has since been retconned. Zintel is even less documented than Marcks.
In fact, I was wrong. Marcks was in command during at least a brief period of Operation Crusader. But either way neither he nor Zintel were the commander at the start.
Instead, I've put Zincke in command. My guess is that his name was misheard as Zintel and that's where it all began.
And Marcks was just caught up in this. I guess that makes sense given he commanded not one, not two, but three different regiments within a few monhts.

Finke and Warrelmann have both been claimed as the commander of 2nd Machine Gun Battalion. Based on my sources I believe Warrelmann was the commander at this time and Finke commanded 2nd Battalion, 115th Infantry Regiment instead.
This has now become relevant in light of recent events, I've had to reintroduce both battalions.
I believe that sources that indicate the non-existence of 2nd Machine Gun Battalion confused these two units. That, or they didn't realize the 2/115th Battalion had been temporarily detached.
This battalion would be converted to a regular infantry battalion in April 1942 with its heavy companies split among the other battalions.
I don't know what happened to Warrelmann after that, but I believe he stayed with the battalion since his name does get mentioned later in 1942.
In fact, his name gets mentioned as a the commander of a Kampfgruppe centered around 3/115, which was the former 2nd Machine Gun Battalion, which confirms he stayed.

I've found later evidence that Warrelmann (first name Hinrich) lasted at least as long as Second El Alamein, confirming the above.
He also commanded a battle group during the capture of Tobruk in 1942, although it seems like this group only formed near the very end of the battle to hold against a potential counterattack (that never materialized).
And for my game, since Paul Schütte is unavailable to lead his battle group in Operation Sommernachtstraum, that's another time for Warrelmann to take command of a force.

Sonderverband 288 had not fully arrived in Africa by November 1941.
Only one group, Sperrverband (blocking detachment) Daumiller had actually arrived, but didn't participate in Crusader. I think the unit is cool though, so I made it pickable.
That detachment basically just spent the entire battle as a guard in Benghazi and consisted of a few infantry-adjacent companies. It withdrew after Benghazi was being given up.

I don't intend to add Mickl's 155th Infantry Regiment (Semi-Motorized) as I don't know all of its battalion commanders.
[
    Only the third battalion's commander, Hollmann, is currently known.
    There's actually a lot of confusion about this, since other sources say that Meythaler was acutally the commander of 3/155 and 3/255's commander was somebody else.
    I'm inclined to think this was the case now, since the claim that Meythaler was in command of 3/255 wasn't well substantiated and could be a typo.
    It's also possible that his name was spelled Maythaler. I don't know either way, maybe somebody who knows German can help me with that.
    There's also a Captain Kolbeck who became commander of some kind of composite battalion at some point. Whether he held a command before that is unclear.
    Hartz commanded what appears to be just a single platoon from the 3/255 battaion. I wanted to put him as temp battalion commander but it didn't become necessary.
    I'm also aware that von Barby previously commanded the battalion, but it looks as if he's busy at the moment.

    I've made him the commander, but I really can't confirm this.

Final Evidence: I have determined from new sources (dating to 2018, nearly 10 years later) that von Barby DID NOT command the 361st regiment at this time.
He only took command... on January 4, 1942. Before then, it was Oberstleutnant Heinz Grund, who appears to have been wounded. And before him, it was Kirsten.
So yes. Von Barby was available to command 3rd Battalion, 255th Infantry Regiment at this time. It's decided.
Also, I found out who Kolbeck was. He was the adjutant in the divisional staff. So no, not a battalion commander until later.

Having reflected some, this really was a major error of judgement on my part. I did find significant evidence that it might've been him, but I disregarded it all because I thought my previous evidence was infallible.
Even when that previous evidence proved (and has proven again) to be on the unreliable side of things.

Sources are murky on this but here's the path of the infantry of the three divisions:
Rommel arrives in Africa with IR zbV 200 with 2 machine gun battalions.
He is then joined by 15th Panzer with 15th Infantry Brigade with 2 Regiments, 104 and 115, each with 2 motorized infantry battalions (NOT machine gun).
These 6 battalions were then evenly distributed between 15th and 21st Panzer (renamed from 5th Light), with each receving 2 motorized infantry and 1 machine gun battalion in their regiments (115 and 104 respectively).
The loose 200th Regimental Staff would command random units during crusader.
At this time, 5 random 3rd Battalions were transferred to Africa to form 90th Light Division. They were joined by the 361st Light Regiment. 3 of the random battalions later formed 155th Infantry Regiment.
After Operation Crusader, the 3/255 Battalion was destroyed and disbanded. The remaining loose 3/347 Battalion and 1 from IR 155 became part of the new IR 200 which became a permanent part of 90th Light Division.
The panzer divisions still had 115th and 104th regiments, though in a greatly weakened state, with 1/104 lost with Major Bach at the frontier.
It appears the remaining machine gun battalions became regular infantry and the 15th Motorcycle Battalion was also amalgamated.

The XX corps was called the Corpo d' Armata di' Manovra until 1942. But just calling it CAM would be pretty confusing and indistinct.

It appears that Italian Mixed Support (a.a. e a.c.) (Support Weapons and Anti-Tank in Italian) Battalions varied heavily in number of companies and design.
I've made them all the same for abstraction reasons, and because the extra companies don't matter much. Either way, quality sources are hard to find.

One source I have found indicates the 66th Infantry Regiment "Sicilia" had three infantry battalions and lacked a mixed support battalion.
This feels like a mistake to me, though, since I can't find support anywhere else and it just... doesn't make any sense? So I'll ignore it.

If you thought the structure of German Kampfgruppen could be confusing, they at least make a decent amount of sense in structure.
British order of battle could often be worse, with loose artillery battalions lying around in divisional units, random companies supporting brigades, and loose reconaissance everywhere.
I've tried my best to minimize these loose units since all they would do is greatly frustrate the British commander. Only loose units that can be called "Infantry" are kept.
This is problematic in the case of Machine-Gun Battalions. Both 70th Division and 1st South African Division had divisional battalions of that type.
I've elected to not deploy them, however, since these units are never mentioned in the sources and don't appear to fight as one.
On the other hand, 27th Machine Gun Battalion from New Zealand does seem to have fought as a single operational unit, so I've included it.
Ultimately, though, this'll operate on a case-by-case basis. Sometimes they'll be included, sometimes they won't.
Here's a couple such examples:
70th Division: 1st RHA, 107th RHA, 17th Coast RA, 149th Anti-Tank RA, 1st Royal Northumberland Fusiliers (MG)
7th Armoured: 4th SA Armoured Cars, 4th RHA (sent from support group to 7th Armoured Brigade, probably by Gott)
1st South African Division: "President Steyn" Machine Gun Battalion

One source I have calls Daumiller Daumüller. I can't say anything about this.

I researched for some time about this guy called Alfred Köster who commanded 200th Panzergrenadier Regiment after Geissler left. I was unable to find much of value. Where he originally came from I'll never know.
In contrast, we know a lot about Albert Panzenhagen of 3/347, even though some earlier histories don't acknowledge his battalion's apparent existence.
In fact, some are in a bit of denial about it. Panzenhagen even commanded a Kampfgruppe during Operation Sommernachtstraum centered around his battalion and still they don't list it.
Similarly, we know a lot about the Panzer commanders, including how Fenski was killed during the battle and replaced with Kümmel.

2nd Battalion, 115th Infantry Regiment appears to have been commanded by Göttman at this time.
We later hear during Gazala that this man was no longer in charge and that the new commander was called Roske. He was killed or captured soon after anyway.
Some sources indicate that after Roske's loss, the battalion was disbanded, whle others cliam that a Rocholl replaced him. Either way the battalion was gone by the end of the battle.
Alternatively, maybe both of these are true. Rocholl could've taken command only for the battalion to end up disbanded a few days later anyway.

I can confirm that Moubray still commanded the 3rd Battalion, Coldstream Guards by the end of Operation Crusader. Only in 1942 was he replaced by Sainthill.
I can't be sure that he was still around when Rommel launched his second offensive, but given the timeline it seems unlikely that he would have lost command (given his battalion's decent performance.)
Lucky time to leave command for him, I guess, since otherwise he would've been captured at Tobruk. Or maybe he would've managed to break out like Sainthill did.

Some sources indicate the Giovani Fascisti Regiment and the MILMART battalion as part of the Areite Division. I believe they were corps assets or part of RECAM but were fluidly redeployed within CAM.
Bortolo Zambon certainly was not in command of the Brescia division, having left at the end of October. Instead Gioda acted as temporary commander until Lombardi could take command.

If the 46th Artillery Regiment (Italian) ever does get added to the game, make sure to give its command to a certain Colonel Grati.
Actually maybe not, he might've actually been in command of the 2nd Celere Artillery Regiment.
This has now happened as part of the updated sets. I've ended up picking Grati even though he's in a disputed position.

The commander of the machine gun (4th) company of 15th Motorcycle Battalion was a Leutnant Paulewicz.
The commander of the third company of the 33rd Engineer Battalion was an Oberleutnant Cirener.
This is the same company that was briefly given to the 2nd Machine Gun Battalion, I believe (wrong).
I was wrong about this ^. It was acutally the 2nd company of the battalion, of unknown commander, that was given to the 2nd Machine Gun Battalion temporarily.

A Major Teetz commanded the 300th Oasis Battalion in some way but was no longer in command by the time of Crusader. He's only mentioned in once place but is higher ranking than Ennecerus.
The 300th Oasis Battalion is an interesting unit. For the simplified Crusader map I decided to use an earlier form of it (still commanded by Teetz) with only 2 oasis companies.
I believe its other companies weren't assigned yet despite having arrived in Africa, as the battalion staff was still very much a training unit and not for combat.
Those three companies are: 6th Oasis (Hauptmann Ennecerus, possibly), 12th Oasis (Leutnant Schön) (some claim has name lacked an accent but I find that unlikely), 580th Reconnaissance (Hohmeyer)

Despite a very short lifespan, the 90th Light Divison had a ton of commanders. Before Sümmermann there was Hans von Ziegler und Klipphausen as acting commander.
The 2nd Machine Gun Battalion was commanded by Voigtsberger back in July 1941. It's unclear when Warrelmann replaced him.

The transfer of engineer units is very hazy and unclear. However, from what I can tell, this is what happened:
33rd Panzer Pioneer Battalion had 3 companies. One was lost to join 2nd Machine Gun Battalion, along with another from the 39th Panzer Pioneer Battalion of 3rd Panzer Division (which never entered Africa).
The first company eventually returned to its parent unit while the other one was sent to the 200th z.b.V. Panzer Pioneer Battalion. Since that unit doesn't exist in, my world, we'll let 2nd MG Battalion keep its backup.
Because the precedent has been set that even a single company of engineers is enough, this battalion has now been rated as engineer-capable in-game.
Either way, it looks as if both were destroyed during Crusader. The 33rd Engineer Battalion was utterly ruined and down to company strength by the end, and the 200th had surrendered in Bardia.
After having done a lot more research, though, this isn't as unclear as before. It'd be better to describe it as "obscure" instead.

I originally used this to make 2nd Machine Gun larger than a normal unit, but I was actually wrong. It appears all german motorized infantry at this time had 5 companies.
This 5th company was a heavy company and wasn't present in non-motorized battalions. They were disbanded in 1942 with all companies becoming somewhat "heavy".
It appears this additional company also applied to the 15th Motorcycle Battalion. And the 4th battalion for these units was a machine-gun battalion. It was kind of a mess at the time.
I've decided not to use this as a reason to change around the sizes and specifications of the other units, however. Designing Crusader has never been about obsessing over companies.
Only the special engineer company of 2nd Machine Gun Battalion is being counted, because this was a company that certainly wasn't meant to part of its structure.
On later versions of my orders of battle, though, I've removed this extra company. In real life, it joined the 21st Panzer Division's 200th z.b.V. engineer battalion.
Since that unit doesn't exist in my world, I gave it to the Afrika z.b.V. Division instead, as a component of the Oasis Battalion (as both were on the frontier).

Actually, on some later versions, I'm gonna take this company away. It'll probably just be a loose company given to, say, the 300th z.b.V. Oasis Battalion Staff.
After all, even though the 200th z.b.V. Engineer Battalion doesn't exist in this world, doesn't mean it can't go somewhere.

The 115th Artillery Regiment was distinct from the 115th Rifle Regiment, and in fact both had a second battalion that was sent to Africa.
In the case of II./115 AR, because only 1 of the three batteries actually crossed the Mediterranean safely and another was reconstituted with Italian artillery, I've nicknamed it "Half and Half" or "Halb und Halb".
It was a also designated both as motorized and as heavy, though when it arrived in Africa these statuses became more debatable.

German sources indicate the existence of 255th Anti-Tank Battalion as part of the Savona Division. I couldn't find a single mention of this anywhere else, so its likely an abberration.
If I had to guess, the German who wrote this heard of the 155th Machine Gun Battalion from the Savona and somehow managed to get both its number and its type wrong. What a bozo.
Alternatively, this could be the Anti-Tank coermpany of the same division, which was a smaller unit but had an authorized strength of 8 guns (the Germans list 12 guns but some were of different caliber).

To an outside observer, Italian artillery would seem quite obsolete. And I have marked them as obsolete in most orders of battle, because they were. Many guns dated back to the 19th century or to Austria-Hungary.
Furthermore, Italian artillery units were the same size (12 guns per battalion) as the equivalent German units. Meanwhile British field regiments of artillery had 24 guns.
That said, while weak, massed Italian artillery could still pack a real punch. For that reason I've kept them in on the updated orders of battle.
But their stats were certainly be worse than those of any other nation's artillery ,and by a significant margin. Especially in the British Cpature of Tobruk where they did little to nothing.

Some of the British tank battalion commanders are disputed among my sources. I do, however, know for certain that 6th Royal Tank Regiment was led by Lister.
Streitz was almost certainly not the commander of 200th z.b.V. Engineer Battalion since I've seen him mentioned as company commander in the 900th z.b.V. Engineer Battalion. But this isn't that important for this game.

[
    For Crusader`, I've decided to use the August 20, 1941 organization of the 90th Light Division (then called the Special Purpose Divisional Command "Afrika").
    Its only actual combat units were the 300th Oasis Battalion (just 2 companies) and the 3rd Battalion, 347th Infantry Regiment.
    I may also end up adding attached units to it from the Corps Troops or from 15th Panzer Division.

    It's not fully clear, but I believe this division must have been at the frontier, since III./I.R. 347 was guarding Halfaya in the interim (replaced by Bach/Ennecerus on October 26).
    Two of the oasis companies were there with it, the 6th and 12th, two were at the Tobruk salient, and the fifth was who knows where. All five would later end up at the frontier.
    The 10th Oasis Company was known to be at the frontier before Battleaxe, but afterward is unclear. The 6th Oasis Company is known to have been at Qalala for its defense.

    It took some time to build this division's order of battle in-game, since we're operating with some constraints here.
    Here's the original form from Lexikon der Wehrmacht:



    Divisionskommando z.b.V. Afrika 20. August 1941:

    Stab mit Divisions-Kartenstelle 259

    Bataillonsstab z.b.V. 300 mit 6. und 12. Oasen-Kompanie

    III. / Infanterie-Regiment 347

    1 Panzer-Divisions-Aufklärungs-Abteilung (im Wechsel)

    Nachrichten-Kompanie z.b.V. Afrika



    Translation:

    Special Purpose Divisional Command "Afrika" on August 20, 1941

    Staff with Divisional Mapping Detachment 259

    Special Purpose Battalion Staff 300 "Oasis" with 6th and 12th Oasis Companies

    3rd Battalion, 347th Infantry Regiment

    1 Panzer Division Reconnaissance Battalion (in rotation)

    Special Purpose Signals Company "Afrika"




    So how do I intend to implement this in my version?
    Well, the staff, mapping detachment, and signals troops aren't typically recorded as they aren't combat units, so we'll ignore them.
    The Oasis Battalion and 3rd Battalion, 347th Infantry Regiemnt will be in as normal.
    But what about the reconaissance? Here's my answer:
    Historically, there were 2 recon battalions for 3 divisions. So, 2/3 of a battalion per division. Now there's two divisions, to there should be 4/3 recon battalions.
    Therefore, the 33rd Reconaissance Battalion should be in as normal, and the Afrika Division should recieve 1/3 of a battalion, or a company - its own 580th Reconaissance Company!
    Because we don't do companies here, I ended up attaching that one to the Oasis Battalion.
    It also has a battalion attached from the 15th Panzer Division, but that's not in its normal organization so we can ignore it.
]

Meanwhile, Crusader`'s 15th Panzer Division is based on its September 20, 1941 organization. It's about what you'd expect, with two panzer battalions, four infantry in a brigade, and some support units.
To add a little flavor, though, I've given the 2nd Battalion, 33rd Artillery Regiment the "Half and Half" designation since it distinguishes the unit (as the designation's actual holder isn't present.)

Sonderverband 288, mid-reorganization but prior to becoming the Afrika-Grenadier-Regiment, had two mixed battalions, each with three companies, and a 7th company that acted as reconnaissance.
However, my sources indicating this are a little difficult to believe. I don't really see how this would've actually been accomplished given the mixed nature of the original Sonderverband companies.
Instead, I think it was basically in limbo as a pile of random companies until a single major reorganization took place that resulted in it somehow expanding to twelve companies strong.
Four made up each of two battalions, plus their were four additional support companies, making this Afrika Panzergrenadier Regiment quite powerful.
I don't think this happened until after First El Alamein or even into 1943, but I've had it happen just before the battle so the unit can reach its final form for the showdown.

Regio Esercito indicates that many of the Army Corps Artillery Regiments of the Italian army were actually formed from each other's depots. Here are some of them:
8th CAR (8,113,114): 22nd CAR (1,42,43)
10th CAR (7,17,35): 16th CAR (40,44,49), 25th CAR (105,130,148,150)
Niehorster, however, indicates that the 1st group of the 22nd CAR stayed in Italy. When the regiment arrived in Africa, it gained the 42nd and 43rd groups, plus some anti-air, but left the 1st group behind.
The 42nd group and 43rd group were likely not mobilized by the same depot and came from elsewhere, maybe even the G.a.F. There are later formations of these recorded but not in Africa and of different caliber.
The 10th CAR also appears reduced in Niehorster's order of battle, with its 7th group gone. I assume it was left in Italy as well or was sunk on transport. Niehorster also shows its 35th group as the 25th.
I'm inclined to believe this, since another source backs it up. I think this 7th group was lost and the 35th group was a typo on Regio Esercito.
After having found another mistake on Niehorster's artillery, this is now once again in contention. Regio Esercito has yet to be wrong so I'm going to side with it.
25th CAR was armed with 149/13 Austro-Hungarian howitzers built in 1914. They were obsolete for sure, but heavier than the rest.
Some time later, I've seen ever more mentions of XXV and none of XXXV. I'm starting to think Regio Esercito and its source book were the ones responsible for this mistake.

I've found an updated version of Montanari's order of battle for the above. In it, the 25th CAR is confirmed but I can also say with certainty what the 10th CAR had.
It had the 7th and 17th groups. So it was the 35th group that in fact got lost.
He also confirms that the 22nd CAR was greatly reduced and only two batteries were left by the time it got stuck in Tobruk.
But he does confirm that one is of 100/17 and the other is of 105/28. This 100/17 battery must be from the I group, the 105/28 could be 42 or 43.
He also indicates that the 26th artillery regiment sent its 1st group to the fortress where it was presumably destroyed.

The 20th (1,2,3,4,20) and 21st (1,2,3,4,21) were destroyed in North Africa and didn't have subsidiary elements.
10th, 22nd, and 25th CAR were all destroyed when they surrendered in Tobruk.
Navarini's XXI Corps would have the 16th and 24th CAR. Both seem to have survived to be destroyed in Tunisia.

I had a hard time finding any indication of where the 283rd and 284th artillery groups of the "Sabratha" artillery Raggruppamento came from, but I did it!
Both of them came from the 14th Frontier Guards Artillery Regiment's 28th Raggruppamento, which contained groups 281-284.

Wikipedia indicates that the Sirte Division at Tobruk had under its command a 51st Bersaglieri Motorcycle Company.
Given that the Sirte is the 61st Division and all of its other support subunits are numbered as the 61st, I'm gonna say this is a typo.
This is supported by the fact that the Sabratha Division, the 60th, happens to also have a Bersaglieri Motorcycle Company, and it's numbered as the 60th just like the division.
However, I've found evidence of a 53rd Company that was at Sicily despite the rest of the 53rd Division being in Yugoslavia.
This swings the needle for me on this one. I think it's reasonable with the 53rd in existence to say the 51st had done something similar previously (3 years previously in fact).

Some time later I've finally come to my independent conclusion on this. I have the following evidence:
I have no idea what happened to the 201st, 202nd, and 203rd companies. I can only assume the 202nd became the 22nd, while the 201st and 203rd were destroyed somewhere.
The 61st likely did exist. The 60th and 63rd did for sure, and were likely independently formed units sometime between June and December. I think it's reasonable for the 61st to be in the same boat.
On the other hand, I've lost trust that the 61st ever existed. The 51st Division "Siena" never went anywhere near Africa and there was no reason for it, a regular line division, to have a motorcycle company attached.
I think this is just a typo by Montanari. Other sources mention the 22nd and "various divisional companies" (maybe including the 61st) but no 51st.
Furthermore, Montanari himself mentions that Pitassi Mannella formed two reserve groups, each with one such motorcycle company. If the 51st was also there, where did it vanish off to?

Later evidence from Montanari makes me think the 22nd had nothing to do with the 202nd. He lists the 22nd as destroyed in Tobruk, but the 202nd as part of the Sabratha Division in Mid-January (p. 340).
The 201st was also there with the 202nd, and both were apparently near Giovani Berta at this time. This was on 10 January, but Tobruk had already been cut off at this time so they couldn't've been the same.
Strangely, though, the 55th Artillery Regiment is also there and we know that this unit did end up being destroyed in Tobruk, which doesn't make a whole lot of sense. Maybe its headquarters was out but the rest was in?
Another page mentions that the 60th company was missing 2 platoons but that the 201st company's 2 platoons were in the command reserve. This suggests a renaming took place.
With this one, I don't think it matters all that much, but either way, it's undecidable with the meager information I have now. Too bad!

Note to self: the 14th and 15th groups of the Raggruppamento Batterie Volanti were renamed and somewhat reformed from the old 1st and 3rd groups. They weren't completely new formations.
These batteries were mostly mediocre guns but had been mounted on captured British trucks. They didn't come from any other artillery unit so can't be amalgamated.

I am fairly certain that the Savona Division never had any tanks. Some tanks (13?) were alloted but they did not make it to Africa, being sunk on transport.
Some tanks do appear in Bardia by the end of Operation Crusader. I suspect that these were probably left behind by the Ariete Division during the Dash to the Wire.
They definitely didn't have a full tank battalion. I couldn't find a reference to one anywhere which suggests their set didn't have time to deploy.

The battery commanders of the 3rd South African Field Regiment were Harris, Berry, and Greenwood (7th-9th).

About the Italian Mixed Engineer battalions, I originally though had each division had its own battalion, giving the Italians a lot of engineer support.
However, I changed my mind about this when I realized that the "Mixed" part included signals units. Each battalion only actually represented a single company of "combat" engineers.
For a time, I combined all such engineers into a single company, but that wasn't right either. It didn't represent any kind of real-life unit and the companies were non-homogenous.
It didn't help that little information exists about most of these engineer companies, down to their numbers (only the battalions numbers are known).
With so little to go off of, indicating Italian divisional engineers (specialized ones notwithstanding) didn't do much of anything, I've finally made the decision to remove them altogether.

So I was entirely off on this. There was a single company of construction engineers "genio artieri" in each mixed engineer battalion, but these men were not for combat.
They were not armed with any kind of heavy weapons. The men who fulfilled the pioneer role that other nations' engineers complete were considered part of the infantry.

The Italian mixed support battalions had either three or four companies. However, it doesn't matter for this game, because the 4th company was an anti-air company and thus not relevant.
Thus, they will always be represented in-game with three companies: anti-tank, mortar, and machine gun.
The motorized divisions were distinct from others in that they never had any infantry guns. They were replaced with anti-tank guns in each regiment (probably the right decision).
German units did have infantry guns, even the motorized ones, but in the case of the 115th regiment its company was lost in transport and not replaced until 1942.

This does bother me, though, because later charts I've found tend to indicate that the organization was in fact meaningfully different, in that the divisional battalion didn't have a machine gun company.
I find this kinda hard to believe, given that the machine gun company had previously had 3 companies. Where did the 3rd one go? Maybe it became part of the bersaglieri regiments, but that seems weird to me.
A more reasonable guess is that the extra MGs were split up to provide replacements to lost infantry MGs or even to other divisions. A ton of machine guns were lost with the Bignami Column, after all.

The air crash that killed Vyvyan Pope prior to Operation Crusader is often discussed as doing significant damage to the operation. What is not often discussed is that it was a double disaster.
Russell, commander of 7th Armoured Brigade who had just been made Pope's chief of staff, also met his end. As we know, his replacements both in the brigade and in the staff position were not up to scratch.

The 4th "Genova Cavalleria" Machine Gunners Group was originally from the 36th Southeast Tripoli Border Guard sector and was already in Africa when the war began.
The unit was certainly not actually cavalry, had no armored cars, and wasn't some kind of special redeployment from the mainland. It was simply driven to join the Afrika and Savona divisions.
I believe the cavalry regiment depots had been responsible for the formation of numerous units, including its representative regiment, but also even dismounted units like this machine gun battalion.

Guardia Alla Fronteria artillery regiments weren't all the same. They had varying numbers of groups, each of which typically had 3 batteries but could have 2 (and possibly 4).
There were also some independent groups lying around, not subordinated to a specific regiment. And some GaF districts just didn't have any artillery.
In this game, the only GaF units present are the ones of the 28th "Zuara" district with the 280th regiment, and it does seem that all four of its groups had 3 batteries.
Of those four groups, two of these groups made up Raggruppamento Artiglieria "Sabratha" (the 283rd and 284th).

(Later Note)
Heh. Many, many hours later, I probably know a thousand times more about the G.a.F. then when I wrote the above^. None of it is wrong, but it's quite useless in retrospect.
I've also switched my focus onto another battle, which has a ton of GaF units involved, so that part is certainly now untrue.

King's Cross (Tobruk) is sometimes referred to as Sidi Mahmud. Just to add some confusion.
It doesn't help that Bir Lunes might actually be Bir Junes. And Ras el Madauar has a million spellings.

For some godforsaken reason, the two mortar companies, the 141st and 142nd, of the 64th "Catanzaro" Infantry Division, made it to Tobruk despite the division being destroyed at the camps and Bardia.
Regardless of how they got there, I'm going to attach them to the Blackshirt battalions as a sort of "ad-hoc" remnant of earlier destroyed units, but pretend they're independent rather than regimental.

The 140th Blackshirt Battalion was a part of the 238th Blackshirt Legion of the 2nd "28 Ottobre" Blackshirt Division.
It was formed using cadres from Salerno, which its nickname as a battalion. Its source territorial unit was nicknamed "Aquilia".
This territorial legion had formed two other battalions. The 240th was sent to Ethiopia but wasn't reformed in WW2. The 540th was kept in Italy as a militia unit.
The 238th regiment/legion had the nickname "Aurelio Padovani". This man was a previous fascist leader. It included 3 battalions and a machine gun company (but no regimental mortar company)
As a legion it was of the "Deposito" or depot variety, but as a battalion it was the "d'Assalto" or assault variety.
It appears that this battalion was the only survivor of the division which was otherwise destroyed in Bardia. It would end up in Tobruk where it, too, was destroyed.
There's a different blackshirt unit, the 130th, with the similar-sounding nickname of "L'Aquila".

Actually, I was wrong. The battalion was never at Bardia in the first place and was attached to the Sirte division from the start.
All the way back when the "28 Ottobre" Division moved to Bardia from its earlier position on the Tunisian border, it left this battalion (with half of its companies) in the Tobruk fortress or nearby.

CC.NN. battalions were called coorte (cohort) and companies were called centuria (century) but this applied only to the militia battalions in the territorial army.
Field-formed battalions were given normal designations in line with the army EXCEPT that regiments were still called legions.

III./I.R. 347 was relieved from its defensive position at Halfaya by I./S.R. 104 and Oasis elements on October 26, about a month before crusader.
I can't be sure, but it must have been reinforced by significant flak and artillery units or some Italian elements since this battalion was slightly short of weaponry (likely sunk on transport).
Interestingly, III./I.R. 155, formerly III./I.R. 268, was given instruction by the command based on lessons learned by the Schützen Regiments. Maythaler apparently didn't learn his lesson very well.

Something I've done out of convenience is to repeatedly ignore the Ras el Medauar salient in the southwest of the Tobruk defenses, held by German troops for much of the siege.
It was decided that the salient was too stalemated and not worth attacking in again, while the Australian and British units constantly harassed it with small-scale attacks.
If I did choose to model it, I would put I./S.R. 115 there to back up the various Italian units, for the Battleaxe campaign only.

Wikipedia doesn't list this, but the 18th Indian Infantry Brigade had the 121st Field Regiment, Royal Artillery when it was destroyed.
For this game it's gonna be replaced with the 3rd to reduce overhead. But this regiment/battalion certainly was there.

The Operation Compass Order of Battle on Wikipedia (from Montanari) lists a XXI Libyan Coastal Battalion as part of the Tobruk garrison at the start of the operation.
However, according to Niehorster, the battalion was part of the GaF and thus would be part of the 2300 that Montanari lists there.
But since Niehorster's OOB of the 32nd GaF sector's subsectors is incomplete, I have no way to verify this using the numbers.
Because of this lack of information, I'm just going to ignore this unit until I can find more on it.
Worse, I've later come accross some forum discussion that claims this unit was actually numbered as the XXXI and Montanari made a mistake.
At this point, I believe the unit isn't worth the ambiguity to include in my orders of battle. So, it won't be getting added.
Another source claims that only a single battalion of border guards was actually there. My guess is that the Libyan battalion was still in formation or not official, and thus wasn't counted.

Little to no damage was sustained by the Trento division during Operation Brevity, as its elements were either in Bardia (which wasn't attacked) or were late reinforcements.
The Italian units that were lost seem to all have been from the 8th Bersaglieri Regiment, though they fought hard and surrendered much less than the previous year. About one company was captured.

I considered giving the Germans access to a company of the "Diana" tank destroyer because I think the whole idea of it is cool: a captured soviet gun on a random truck.
The tank destroyer was a bit of a janky mess, had completely useless 4.5mm armor, and was generally not great. Nine were produced, all given to the 605th Anti-Tank Battalion.
Unluckily, I don't think the "Diana" nickname was ever actually given to the unit during this time. A later production run did use it but I don't think they made it to Africa.
Also, the nickname may have been given by the British and later adopted by the Germans, which complicates matters.
So, I think I'm not gonna end up adding this unit after all. It's for a battalion that doesn't exist and is historically quite unclear.
They also were not present for Crusader, arriving only in January 1942, though they did manage to participate in the battles after that.

For future reference, a standard Italian tank battalion has a much simpler structure than that of any other nation. There are 46 tanks total, in three companies of 15 each, and one command tank.
Each company had 3 platoons, with 5 tanks each. Later in the war, many companies would receive an additional radio-equipped tank to improve communications, but this wasn't active in 1940.

Niehorster lists an AS1940 autotransportable division as having 232 HMGs which I think is a frankly insane number.
It has 166 extra compared to the metropolitan type, but has only one extra machine gun battalion with companies that have just 12 HMGs each.
It's not fully clear, since some claims indicate that the machine gun battalions could have anywhere between 3-5 companies. The 155th for sure had at least 3, I don't know about the rest.

To reduce the confusion listed above about the state of the Guardia alla Frontiera at Tobruk, I'll compile what I know here:
1. The sectors responsible for the town were the 31st and 32nd. A few stragglers from elsewhere might've been but aren't recorded.
The sectors were intended to contain 1800 men each. However it appears only 2300 men actually showed up for the defense.
The XXI or XXXI Libyan Battalions may have been there, but it is entirely unclear which one or if either was actually there at all. These also might not be part of the GaF.
The 31st had 10 artillery groups and the 32nd had 12. This disagrees with Niehorster but I belive the mistake is over there.
The 31st had an engineer company that was surely real but might not have been in Tobruk when the battle began. The 32nd also had an engineer company but I cannot locate it.

If I had to guess, the 1800 men number was entirely theoretical. No sources indicate that anywhere near this number of infantry ever showed up (but maybe it includes artillerymen?)
Montanari gives this number on page 47 of "Operations in North Africa" but this dates back to the first half of 1940 and is no longer relevant by 1941.
The 2300 men seems not entirely unreasonable. If you include the 31st GaF Infantry Battalion, 21st/31st Libyan Coastal Battalion, both engineer companies, and lesser support, it about adds up.
It's probably also the 31st and not the 21st Battalion. Montanari is far from infallible and this type of mistake isn't uncommon, plus 31 is the number of the sector it's in.
The eastern sector was split into sub-sectors A and B. As we know from the western sector, 1 battalion manned each sub-sector, so there should've been two battalions worth of troops there.
In this case, I/69 or II/69 (the other was probably destroyed or depleted) defended one while the 31st Frontier Guard Infantry Battalion defended the other.
The Libyan battalion is supposedly "coastal" so I don't think it would've ended up deployed on the outer ring; it would've been on, well, the coast. I also don't know its structure or even its number.

Later sources dispute the above ^. In fact it appears both of the battaslions were on the frontline. And it WAS the 31st, not the 21st.
He indicates the following frontier guard artillery structure as well:
XXXI:
    Infantry battalion with 3 static machine gun companies and one gun company
    5th Group: 232bis, 242, 243
    6th Group: 230bis, 231bis
    7th Group: 237, 239, 244, 245 (all heavy)
XXXII:
    1st Group: 248, 249, 258
    2nd Group: 246, 246bis, 258bis
    3rd Group: 240bis, 247, 247bis
    4th Group: 236bis, 240, 241 (had personnel only at in june 1940 but received 77/28 by 1941)
5th Artillery Regrouping (idk)
6th Artillery Regrouping (31st Group)
7th Artillery Regrouping (30th Group)
31st Libyan Coastal Battalion

(The above was provided by J. Leser. I don't think his reading was very thorough as I strongly disagree with the above.)

Regio Esercito claims that the 31st GaF sector had an infantry battalion of the following structure:
XXXI Battaglione Fanteria Guardia alla Frontiera
    3 compagnie mitraglieri da posizione
    1 compagnia cannoni da 37/10
(3 static machine gun companies, 1 cannon (probably anti-tank gun) company)
As stated previously, there was probably a Libyan battalion there. A typical Libyan battalion had 4 infantry companies, but this battalion seems to be of special design, so I can't confirm.

Apparently a lot of Italian divisions in North Africa actually had two mortar companies per regiment rather than one.
It is difficult for me to verify this, however, and only Regio Esercito's order of battle for 10 June actually reflects this.
Some of these regiments also show up with four infantry battalions which I don't think was ever actually implemented, which further leads me to believe this didn't take place.
Based on later information, my best explanation for this is that this was the planned organization for an upgraded Type 1940 AS division, but with manpower and mortar shortages, this was never implemented.

Don't be an idiot like me. Compagnia in Italian is the singular, not the plural, form of company.

The Trento division's 2 anti-airctaft batteries were numbered the 412th and 414th. I have no information regarding the batteries of any other division.

I thought Umberto Barberis was the commander of the 69th Infantry Regiment. This was wrong. He was actually commander of the GaF of XXI Corps.
This confuses me a bit since I don't understand why the GaF would be subordinated to a corps, especially one that wasn't there.
I think that this position (commander of GaF XXI Corps) was transferred to the XXII Corps at some point but this wasn't recorded.
Anyway, he was captured with Pitassi Mannella, Della Mura, and Vietina when the fortress fell.

I struggle to find much information on the individual batteries and regiments of the Royal Artillery and Royal Horse Artillery, and of imperial artillery forces.
It appears that RHA regiments began the war with only 16 guns each and RA regiments had 24 but at times RHA regiments had as much as 36.

One battalion of replacements, the 64th "Catanzaro", ended in Tobruk during its fall, according to at least one source that I trust.
However, we don't do field replacement units here, so that fact is staying in this notes page forever.
We also don't do anti-air units here, and there were a couple: 15th Anti-Air Group with 2 batteries, and 1 battery of machine anti-air assigned to the 4th Tank Infantry Regiment.

All the replacement units of the 60th-64th divisions were placed in assorted locations in Cyrenaica.
The 60th and 61st were with their divisions, the 64th was in Tobruk, the 63rd was around Benghazi, and the 62nd was somewhere around Tobruk.

The only source regarding the numbering of the bersaglieri motorcycle companies is the historically quite unreliable Montanari.
I don't have any other choice so I'll take his word on the 51st and 22nd not being the 61st (bis) and 202nd, but this is still quite suspicious to me.
But since there's a solid 6 months for these reorganizations to take place during which I have no other evidence on the matter, I can't really do much about it.
I do have one other source that claims the existence of a 22nd but lacks any kind of date. It helps but not by much.

Montanari lists a 26th Artillery Raggruppamento at Tobruk, but this couldn't've been the 26th CAR because that unit never formed a 100/17 group, contradicting him.
It might have been the 26th "Pavia" Artillery Regiment, but without further evidence I'm gonna have to discard this unit out of hand.

The 30th and 31st artillery groups of the GaF appear to have been formed by the depot of the 11th Artillery Regiment GaF but were parcelled out to other regiments/raggruppamenti during the war.
Both were in Tobruk under what seem like random regiments (the 6th and 7th, haha).

Somehow he is never mentioned a single time, but the commander of the 2nd Support Group during Sonnenblume was a Brigadier Henry Latham.

In its original form, 33rd Panzer Pioneer Battalion had 3 companies, with the 2nd detached, and the 3rd with some tanks attached to it.
These tanks disappear by September. I believe they were used to replace losses in the panzer regiments. This change will be reflected in some orders of battle.

That's a total of 20 groups, plus 2 spare batteries, totaling 248 guns. He later gives a number of 340, which probably includes anti-tank, anti-air, naval, etc.

Painfully, Wikipedia refers to the 2 raggruppamenti (east and west) as simply "2 groups" which greatly undersells the actual strength (29 batteries, not 6).
This is a translation issue but it really is a problem around this kind of thing. Translating the Italian sources isn't done very well, to be fair most sources were only opened long after the war itself.

Montanari and Regio Esercito, however, disagree about the exact structure of the GaF batteries and groups. Montanari's OOB is above but Regio Esercito claims that all the groups were renumbered prior to destruction.
I'm inclined to trust Regio Esercito much more on this one since it's based on war diaries. Montanari's one for the 31st and 32nd regroupings appears to be copied from June 10 and doesn't reflect wartime changes.
Welp, time to make a million more changes. For the big one, of course, I've lost most interest in the small one.

I'm going to hazard a guess for the confusing setup of some of Montanari's artillery. The XXX and XXXI gruppi don't appear anywhere else with the same guns (the 120/35 is very rare, the 149/35 less so).
There is a mention of a different XXX and XXXI gruppo, however these were formed in 1939, and the 1940 order of battle lists neither as having the 120/25 gun.
This makes me think that the XXXI and XXX gruppi he speaks of are actually the 6th and 7th gruppi from the 31st sector, renamed (the XXX might be from elsewhere, I'm not sure).
This makes more sense, given these are labelled under the 6th and 7th "Raggruppamenti" Artiglieria G.a.F., units I couldn't find anywhere else.
There are mentions of such units in Regio Esercito, labelled under the 6th and 7th Army Artillery Regiments, but neither of them actually had an XXX or XXXI group sent to Africa.
The 2 groups left sector 31 and were scheduled to leave but didn't get the chance to. At the same time, the 31st received 3 replacement battalions from the 32nd.
The 32nd then received replacements of its own that are of mixed caliber but their exact nature is not recorded. This amounted to 2 groups of mixed caliber.
Since the details of these units is pretty much unknowable, there is little I can do.

I probably could've guessed this, but the position of Zaafran's name in Arabic translates to "Saffron".

Apparently this isn't common knowledge, but all former "cavalry" units in the British Army had a standard establishment of 3 squadrons.
This includes both tank (RTR) units and the less regular "Hussar" units.

The Libyan Parachute Battalion "Black Devils" and National Libyan Parachute Battalion are different units. They were, respectively, the first and second battalions of a regiment-sized force.

According to one source, the XXXI Frontier Guard Sector had a total of 29 batteries:
16 77/28

For some reason, while the average Italian anti-tank company had 8 guns, the anti-tank company of the 31st GaF Infantry Battalion had 23!
This unit was just generally oversized. Its 3 machine gun companies, which logically would have several hundred men, actually had 611 machine guns.
They were of the Schwarzlose type, meaning each one needed a three-man crew. I have no idea how a regular battalion managed to do this.

The "Volontari della Libia" blackshirt battalion was formed a few days after the war began, and as its name suggests was formed from various volunteers from Libya.
It was numbered the 2nd and was formed from one company from each of the existing CC.NN. Libyan legions. Designations:
1st Company: Tripoli, 2nd Company: Misurata, 3rd Company: Bengasi, 4th Company: Derna
Based on this, I don't think there was any kind of heavy company in the unit, rather all four companies were identical (and not very large).
It probably had some kind of machine guns or mortars, but given the lack of a heavy company and its ad-hoc establishment, any weapons can't be known.
The idea to form the unit was from Alberto Venturelli, but its commander in combat was Giovanni Rocca.
Also, apparently this mess: "1a 2'Y4°" translates to "1st, 2nd, 3rd, and 4th" from Italian.
By the way, I don't believe the personnel came from the existing legions, the existing legions just served as formation places. There are records of new recruits forming this unit.
So, it's training and experience were probably even more lackluster than a typical blackshirt unit, and there were few heavy weapons. Truly an impressive fighting force.

Important - these were formed from Italian men, not Libyans. They just happened to be living in Libya and thus could only amass the men to form 4 legions.

The companies that formed it were from the 4 existing colonial legions. These were formed in 1935 and were named after the locations they were formed in.
Prior to this, there were only 3 legions, one each for Tripolitania, Cyrenaica, and Fezzan. Their designations were "Oea" and "Berenice" (3rd unknown).

Guardia alla Frontiera engineers were mostly responsible for the maintenance of fortifications. They seem to have some combat ability but they were by all means "mixed".
Some sources indicate they consisted exclusively of communications and operators, and lacked any real combat element.
I think for this reason they'll be removed from my orders of battle, just like the rest of the mixed engineer battalions.
Or maybe not. I'll have to think about this over the next few days, since I don't have a replacement company-sized unit ready.
After having learned about the utterly uneven sizes of GaF units, I think I'll keep it in. Even if only part of it (it's "mixed" after all) was combat engineers, it's still a roughly company-sized portion.

I later have turned around on this idea. The engineers are actually separate from the other GaF numbers which are only for infantry.
I was certainly trolling when I went through this the first time anyway. These engineers were entirely for support tasks and had no combat ability.
The same also goes for the regular engineer units, as while some did have partial combat ability, the only specialized assault engineers, the Guastatori, were never divisional in nature.
There were also sometimes artillery engineers, which I hadn't noticed until now. These guys were certainly not meant to be anywhere near a frontline.
(Note: this is not true, there were no artillery engineers. This is due to the translation on Bing being terrible, as the term "artieri" actually translates to "builders" or "artisans".)

I have finally obtained the original text of Montanari's writing on the defense of Tobruk. This has been a long time coming.

General Berti had pretty high demands for Pitassi Mannella's defense, believing he had access to much more firepower than he actually did.
He ordered, for example, 100 anti-tank guns to be deployed on the front, with 4 more in reserve and 16 or more mobile. There were probably actually only 80 or less total.
But at the very least, Berti had the right idea about how the battle should be fought. He wasn't completely incompetent in this regard, though he probably should've been little more realistic.

Also, he claims there were only 198 artillery pieces in the fortress, not 343.
This lines up with the British claim of 208 pieces captured. Maybe the extra 10 were stragglers or inoperable so Berti didn't count them.
Strangely, his number of 198 states it doesn't include 120mm or 149mm guns but does include 20mm guns. So for these to line up the number of 120mm and 149mm guns must be 145.
According to my data, the GaF had 12 batteries of these calibers and the regular army the same number, totaling 96 guns. Not far off, but something is clearly wrong.

Later findings on GaF artillery below:
I think Montanari was trolling with two of his labelled units. The 31st/6th and 30th/7th units were probably actually the 6th/31st and 7th/31st.
This makes things a lot simpler. The 6th/31st had 2 batteries of 120/25 (230th bis, 231st bis, both static) and the 7th/31st we've already seen with 4 batteries of 149/35 (238, 239, 244, 245, all static).
This typo is honestly pretty catastrophic compared to the rest. So I guess these two units really were just aberrations.

We know for certain that some actual infantry of Italian origin were there since they were captured and weren't from the Libyan Battalion.
The existence of a battalion is also mentioned here: https://italianiinguerra.wordpress.com/2024/01/22/africa-settentrionale-22-gennaio-1941-gli-australiani-prendono-tobruch/.
Obviously, this may be the Libyan battalion, but since it's mentioned as part of the Frontier Guard and not as Libyan, this seems quite unlikely to me.
A different man who was killed there is mentioned in another source which refers to him as an "infantryman", not an artilleryman. And of course he wasn't Libyan.
This is missing from the above data, from Montanari suggesting its at least somewhat incomplete. It also doesn't mention many artillery units.
My guess is that at the very least, the XXXI GaF Infantry Battalion was probably there. Otherwise, there would barely even be a frontline in the eastern sector.

However if or if not any artillery was there is hard to say. Outside of the 6 batteries above (242, 244, 245, 246, 247, 249), none are directly mentioned.
He later also mentions the existence of 2 "raggruppamenti" but no sizes for them are described. He also doesn't include anything about their numbering.
And to be more unhelpful, his map is wrong and only actually shows the locations of two groups, which lines up with the size of 6 batteries.

It's happened! I've obtained exact artillery numbers. Once again my tertiary sources have drained the detail Montanari provides.

Other Notes:

2/69 was in the frontline, not 1/69
2/69 had 1 company of 1/69 with it

Extra anti-tank guns were training units
The 64th replacements battalion with 2 companies (the third detached?) was sent to occupy the Beiad position
Naval 76/40 gun and mortars (probably 141st and 142nd) were assigned to reinforce 3/69 in the reserve line
It also had some machine gunners and a platoon of the 140th Blackshirt Battalion
A bunch (4) of training SQUADS formed a reserve at the Piave point. This is so small that it's negligible.
I'll probably end up replacing this messed-up garrison with a random spare company I have lying around from the 69th Infantry.
Pt. 144 (manned by 140th CC.NN.) had a battery of artillery (75/27) and some light (heavy support infantry company, not mortar company) mortars
Pilastrino had one company of the 61st MG battalion, and 12/70, with some bonus light weapons
The other 2 (with some extra MGs) were on the western sector's frontline or in reserve close to it
Gabr el Abd had the 61st replacement battalion with one regular company and one heavy company (oh yeah replacement battalions have those now)
q. 71 (near Airente I think) had 2 companies of the XXXI Libyan Coastal Battalion and a couple light machine and anti-tank guns
It also had 2 77/28 guns attached to it. This was probably half of the 246th or 247th battery, GaF
The other company of XXXI Libyan and the third company of the 64th replacements battalion were on coastal defense duty
But some of them were also deployed if necessary to defend the line between the Airente q.71 position and the sea
It appears that the support companies of the rifle battalions were typically spread out among the toher three companies
The 141st and 142nd mortar companies were attached to Barberis's frontline but since they had no mortars with them they were probably used as infantry
The defense of the port was conducted with the depots of the GaF, 116th "Marmarica" Infantry Regiment, some carabinieri, and the 3rd Company of the 62nd Replacement Battalion

I can't find the locations of any of the supposed new naval batteries "Vittoria", "Nembo", "Buffolotto" (section, probably the 149/35 guns)
Actually these guns have been located based on his map. The Buffolotto section is also confirmed to consist of 2 149/35 guns, originally G.a.F. but crewed by naval personnel.

A section of 149/35 guns also appears on some maps. I believe this had 2 guns and was deployed at Hagfet Umm Haleiga.

It's clear to me that the V, VI, and VII groups were present. Two of them are directly mentioned by Montanari and while the V group isn't, its calibers match exactly with his "mixed" group.
I can't say the same for the front support batteries, they aren't diretly shown. However, the gun numbers strongly imply that they were there (maybe disbanded to provide front reinforcement).

I watched a video on the North African campaign here:
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Odk0Cp2D_fU&t=448s

I didn't think the video was all that good, and thus left the following corrections:

6:42 The 1st South African Brigade is shown taking Bir el Gubi (point marked south of Tobruk) from the Italian 132nd Division. This didn't happen, and Bir el Gubi would only fall to a different Commonwealth force.
8:14 The 1st South African Brigade was also not here. It had been swapped with the 22nd Guards Brigade and was only responsible for defending British supply depots.
8:26 The 7th Armoured Brigade wasn't in is operation. The brigade had been nearly destroyed previously and had given up its remaining tanks to the other two brigades.
10:51 The Free French Brigade at Bir Hacheim wasn't destroyed until much later in the battle. Only the 150th Brigade had been destroyed at this point and some of the minefields cleared.
11:02 The 9th Indian Infantry Brigade (shown in Tobruk) was part of the force used for the attack (Operation Aberdeen). It also took high losses during this operation and retreated afterward, so it wasn't captured in Tobruk as shown in the video.
11:38 The South African troops on the coast did not retreat in the same direction as the British 50th Division to the south. They escaped along the coast, through Tobruk, instead.
13:43 Only 50% of the Italian 60th "Sabratha" Division was overrun and the rest of it was able to counterattack and restore the situation. The division was disbanded by Rommel after the battle, not destroyed by the attack.
14:57 The 185th "Folgore" Division had started off as a parachute division, but the symbol on the map is wrong as it had been converted to a regular infantry division by this point.

The research could've been better and I also dislike the perpetuation of some tropes, namely the obsession with tanks and the refusal to admit the times when Italian troops did show promise.

At Bardia, there was a XVII group of 75/27 guns. I have no idea where this came from, but it wasn't from the 10th CAR since that one was of 105/28 guns.

Strangely, Montanari's maps only show the existence of two companies of the 2nd Battalion, 70th Infantry Regiment.
I want to assume those are the 5th and 6th companies, since the Italians didn't consider the support company of each battalion to be a position-holding unit (rather it was broken up to support the others).
Perhaps the other company had been transferred to the 3rd/69th battalion, since Montanari refers to that one as having been "reinforced".

Some claims give Pitassi Mannella control over 62 light tanks but only 25 medium tanks (of 11/40?). I don't think this tank even exists and this claim is quite frankly absurd.

The frontier guard infantry battalions confuse me because there's only a single one in existence in Africa. There are also six in Albania (numbered 1-6), but none in the Italian mainland.
Since the 31st was the only one in the entirety of Africa, I think we can be a bit less assumptive about its structure since so there's no comparable units. I honestly think it's possible that this single battalion contained all 2300 men Montanari mentions.
Which is starting to make more sense, as it's said that this force had 600 machine guns and 23 anti-tank guns. A normal battalion couldn't handle that much firepower, even if most of it was dug into the defenses and non-maneuverable.
In fact, a different kind of Italian machine gun company at this time had 10 Schwarzlose and 13 Breda 30s, from 161 troops. So 3 such companies would have 70 total machine guns. How did 600 get there, then?

However, I do have reason to believe that the GaF had an abnormally large density of such weapons since it was large immobile.
THe initial strength of the GaF in Europe was 21,000 men from 27 sectors, with 6000 machine guns and several thousand regular guns.
So if 21,000 men, many of whom were artillerists had 6000 machine guns, I guess its reasonable for a single battalion structure to have so many guns (many of which probably were never fired).
The GaF later had 50,000 men by June 1940, but this rapid increase was accomplished primarily by adding 22 CC.NN. battalions and didn't reflect much increase in actual organic GaF strength.

Based on how the coverage sectors were organized, artillery would've been split up. Some would've been placed under the command of each fortification and others under a sectoral command.
I think in Tobruk's case the larger guns were in sectoral positions while the smaller guns were placed inside each fortification and weren't mobile.
However, no maps I have show these guns in place, and in fact Montanari only mentions 2! 77/28 guns in the entire fortress.

With the number 2300 as the only thing of value in my possession, I'm gonna have to make even more assumptions. No quality book on this topic exists.
The 31st Infantry Battalion GaF was probably actually a battalion in size. It had an insane amount of firepower, but was highly static and thus could accept such establishment.
Adding the two engineer units and the 31st Libyan Coastal Battalion, we get pretty close to 2300, which makes me think the 1800 x 2 number also includes artillery.
This implies that the difference of 1300 was the number of artillerymen. A standard Italian artillery regiment of 9 batteries, 36 guns had 82 officers and 2687 men.
So that means that the 1300 difference was probably 4-6 batteries, the same ones that Montanari mentions in his late order of battle. But then how did more guns just show up in his map?
Disregard the above. I've re-read some things and I was certainly not on the mark with those statements.

Montanari actually puts a total of 3300 men in Tobruk in June, 1500 in the XXXI sector and 1800 in the XXXII sector (infantry). By december only 2300 men remain total.
I assume this was the full XXXI sector (in its infantry battalion) and 800 men in the XXXII sector (location unknown). We could give each sector 2 subsector companies to represent this.
But the issue with this is that the exactual structure of the subsectors is something I can't reasonable figure out. They also varied from a single machine gun company to several plus fortress units.
Since the town was one big fortress, there's no reason for the GaF to have additional sub-fortress units. But how many machine gun companies were there is something I can't calculate.

I thought this made sense, but then I read that the XXXII sector, under the Barberis command, had its own machine gun battalion. Maybe this was the Libyan battalion?
Since I don't have anything else to go off of, and the battalion was after all part of the XXXII sector, I guess it had machine guns now. But this wasn't part of its name, so I wouldn't execute on this.
Apparently there was also a 32nd Libyan Battalion in Tobruk according to some sources? But these claims are relatively unreliable so I wouldn't lend too much credence to that claim.
Also not helping is that Montanari never references a single unit as being part of this subsector other than the few artillery batteries. So maybe it just didn't exist by this point or was in the process of dissolution.

A forum post says the 12th battery of the 42nd "Sabratha" Artillery Regiment was supposed to be one of its 20/65 batteries, but Regio Esercito says it had the 60th and 260th batteries
There was also supposedly a 4th battery from the Bologna Division, which also doesn't make a lot of sense since this was before the division was partially reconstituted with a ton of random units.

Carlo Rostagno was the chief of staff of the 22nd Corps for most of December, but at some point he must have been replaced. Montanari refers to him as "former".
He is the ultimate source for most of my Tobruk order of battle information. I haven't found much that indicates him to be unreliable, at least not as badly as Montanari for sure.
Instead, it is Adolfo de Leone, Brigadier General, who is listed as having held this position, starting sometime in January, that was captured.
It does appear that Rostagno was also captured, but not listed elsewhere as he was a colonel, not a general. Meanwhile Brigadier Virginio Villanis, 10th Army Chief of Artillery, might have also been there.
Rostagno, just like Di Giorgis, would return to Italy after the war and enjoy a fairly successful end to his career leading a branch of the Italian military, in this case the Guardia alla Finanza.
Other than the order of battle, Rostagno's reports also included details about the security of the fortress, and how the British had undertook significant aerial reconnaissance against it.

However Montanari lists Leone as commander of the artillery, not Virginio Villanis. Since I only have two sources about this, they are in disagreement, and one is notoriously unreliable, not much I can do.
I disagree though. Villanis is mentioned later as leading some kind of column during the Mechili-Beda Fomm campaign. He didn't have a reason to be in Tobruk anyway, that place was hopeless.
Just like Bergonzoli, Villanis would lead one of several columns ordered to conduct the retreat of the remainder of 10th Army. Both were captured.
Barberis was captured too, shortly after the fall of the El Adem / Sidi Mahmud position, which his headquarters, along with two regimental headquarters, had been located at.

Notes on total numbers:
22,000 men given by most Italian sources
2,000 sailors (not counted by above)
25,000 British claim total
The 1,000 difference is probably negligible or a rounding error.

The British 3rd Armoured Brigade has no insignia. It just doesn't. I guess if the unit didn't survive to a specific point in the war, it just never receives such identification.

Also found some more notes on what RECAM was doing. It started the battle in the rear in Cyrenaica, and had to spend a week to move forward.
It then patrolled the area between Bir el Gubi and El Adem providing some support to the Ariete and Pavia divisions. After Ariete moved, RECAM took its place.
It also made some minor attacks against the New Zealanders but was attacked by German aircraft. After holding off the Indians at Bir el Gubi again, it became combat incapable and had to retreat.
Other than the 52nd Tank Battalion, Romolo Gessi PAI Battalion (2 MG and 1 Armored Car Company), and Flying Batteries, RECAM had a random motorized machine gun company. There was also an independent experimental L6 tank platoon.
The Flying Batteries Group had this organization:
    1st Group (3 65/17)
        Batteries were numbered 1-3
    2nd Group (3 65/17, 1 20/35)
        65/17 Batteries were numbered 4-6
    11th Independent Battery (65/17)
    13th Independent Battery (100/17)
    14th Independent Battery (20/35)
In May 1942, it was reformed into the 136th Artillery Regiment, with the following organization:
    14th Group
        1st 65/17 Battery
        2nd 65/17 Battery
        3rd 65/17 Battery
        20/65 Section
    15th Group
        4th 65/17 Battery
        5th 65/17 Battery
        6th 65/17 Battery
        20/65 Section
    16th Group
        7th 75/27 Battery
        8th 75/27 Battery
        9th 75/27 Battery
    17th Group
        10th 100/17 Battery
        11th 100/17 Battery
        12th 100/17 Battery
    13th 20/65 Battery
Some sources disagree that the 13th Independent Battery had 100/17s, instead claiming that it was the 14th that had this armament.
Either way, this battery's four guns were lost by friendly fire during Crusader. Some claim a single gun survived but was later lost within a few months.
The 13th 20/65 battery was renamed the 88th by the time this unit was fully attached to the Giovani Fascisti Armored Divison in January 1943.

The Giovani Fascisti Battalion Group had the following OOB:
HQ Company
MG Company
Transport Company
81/14 Platoon
47/32 Platoon
1st Battalion (3 Companies)
2nd Battalion (3 Companies)
1 Light Tank Company (From the 3rd Light Tank Battalion, Bir el Gubi Only)
Elements of the 8th and 9th Bersaglieri Regiments (Bir el Gubi Only)

So, um, I screwed thsi up. While the RBV order of battle is mostly correct, I made some misplays with the structure of the rest of RECAM. Let's give this another attempt.
There is no random motorzied machine gun battalion. The Romolo Gessi Battalion was a machine gun unit, as it was possible to mount the MGs on the handlbars of the motorcycles.
It had a command platoon rather than company, and it appears the armored car company was numbered as the first rather than any of the motorcycle machine gun companies.
There was also extra artillery not part of the RBV (so not motorized very well) of the following composition. All are listed as "missing" for some reason however, I don't know what that exactly means.
1 20/35 AA Group with 2 batteries
1 88/56 Battery (German gun, Italian crews)
1 105/28 Group with 2 batteries
1 Engineer Company from the CAM Engineer Raggruppamento
1 Mixed Truck Group

We can't know the exact structure of XXXI Libyan Coastal Battalion but its sister XXXII battalion had 25 officers and 752 men, with 15 MGs (3 heavy).
A Libyan MG battalion at this time had 18 officers and 518 men from its 4 companies, so it's not really comparable.
Meanwhile, a standard Libyan battalion had 3 companies with 3 rifle platoons each and a command company, totalling 19 officers and 672 men.
After later reorganizations, September 1941 battalion had 4 companies (varied types) with 27 officers and 725 men. I think this is the most comparable unit (though they differ heavily in MGs).
The coastal battalion had a comparable number of men all the way back in 1940 prior to these changes, so I might see this as a reason to give it 4 companies.
Or maybe it was just a 1940 battalion and the extra 80 men were made up with some random spare platoon. This I also consider quite reasonable.

At the same time, the XXX battalion was at Zuara. I don't think it did anything, but somehow had only 459 men by the end of Compass, implying some of its men had been lost.
The remnants of the XXXII battalion were doing even worse, with just 250 men left. Other battalions had between 400-600 each.
According to some very shaky data, the XXXIV battalion had 4 companies, or at least 4 company-sized units deployed in 4 towns.
But, this was a special battalion formed from previously destroyed Maletti units, so it ain't great. It had previously been destroyed and thus its reconstitution was probably abnormal.
From this, even some non-engaged battalions managed to lose a ton of men and be down to few troops, which makes me think that the organizations were utterly arbitrary and I should use my best judgment.

The XXXIV Battalion that was to be reformed may be the one that Montanari mentions on page 205 of "The First British Offensive". There, it's referred to as "battalion of 600 men to be constituted".
The book also includes the name of its probably first commander, a Colonel Colpani. He could be a survivor from Nibeiwa or a random staff officer found in Cyrenaica.

Other than the two motorized divisions, some claim that the 60th-63rd divisions named after African towns were also given the red shield insignia.
I really can't be bothered to make this change though, and I think the blue one looks better anyway, so I probably won't do anything about this.

In case I need a name handy, Vito de Caro, a lieutenant, was captured as part of the 69th Regiment of the Sirte Division.

After this month-long journey, I think it's appropriate to make a final order of battle so this never happens to anyone else again, and post it to AHF. Here it is!

Changes:

The 51st Bersaglieri Motorcycle Company is a typo, it's the 61st
Elements of the G.a.F. had been moved elsewhere, leaving only part of the 32nd sector
Montanari - "After 15 September the gradual displacement of many units of the border guard to reconstitute the garrisons of the border area and in the area of Halfaya and Sidi Barrani" (p. 292)
"Border guard personnel from Tobruk were used, for the most part, to garrison the strongholds along the grid from Giarabub to the sea and other strongholds around Sidi el Barrani and Buq Buq" (p. 171)
"Deployed in the advanced works with for anti-tank purposes most of the small caliber pieces (65, 75, and 77 mm)"
The 31st/6th and 30th/7th groups are likely a mistake and were the 6th/31st and 7th/31st groups

I've been looking at various other bridging phases of the campaign in North Africa. One of them is the sort of "lull" while Rommel is arriving in Tripoli.
During this time, the Italians began to transport elements to defend Sirte. One such unit was the column "Santamaria", named after Major Camillon Santamaria Niccolini.
It consisted of one tank company, one machine gun company, and two batteries of 75/27. It shrunk by April to barely a company of strength.
A mention of a "Reparti da Posizione Presidio Sirte" responsible for defending the town can also be found, but this unit might be a collection of admin units and not even really exist.

I know that the frontline defense positions had anti-tank guns of 47/32 and 75/27 as well as Breda 20/65s, but you have to count manually to get an exact idea.
There were 8 47/32 guns of the 61st Anti-Tank battery, as well as 6 guns of the 25th Anti-Tank Battery. I have a decent idea of where these were.
4 guns, likely from the 61st, were placed in the reserve. 2, which could be from either battery, were with the 31st Libyan Coastal Battalion.
A total of 43 47/32 guns were registered, while 42 were recorded as anti-tank guns in the eastern sector. Given the above 6 removed, 37 47/32s were part of this anti-tank grouping.
There were thus just 5 more anti-tank guns, which isn't a whole lot and is hard for me to believe. Something easier for me to believe is that this 42 number ignores the 23 GaF ones, leaving instead 28.
But then some of them were training too, which might be the "Derna" battery. That could account for maybe 4 of the 28 guns left. We're gonna have to count 20/65s.

Of the 20/65s, there were 42, but of course not all were used here. I can only find information about where 4 were, so I think the information might just be incomplete.
