With 14 47/32s assumed in those positions, we'll add 8-12 20/65s, with 16-20 spots left for 77/28 and 75/27. I think my original assumption of 3 batteries plus the training one as reasonable.
Nothing conclusive can be decided, though. I'm gonna have to give up on this one and just accept that we know nothing here. An unlucky situation and a waste of time.
But at the very least, my setup is something that isn't plain false or insanely unacceptable. And it does agree with the other Montanari claims. Unless I can somehow find more information, that's that for now.

Good news, though, is that there are some calibers we can count. I think I have enough information to find all the 77/28 guns, of which 12 total could be found.
Two were deployed as artillery in a training battery, leaving 10 used as anti-tank guns. Of those, 2 were used to support the 31st Libyan Coastal Battalion.
This is one place where the text and the images of Montanari disagree. I think the images are generally more accurate since they were written later so I'll take their word for it.
That leaves 8 77/28s, which were probably in two G.a.F. batteries assigned to support the fortifications at the outer ring.

What about 75/27s? 11 are listed in an anti-tank role, with 48 used in a firing role. The 48 number makes sense, as there were four groups, 2 each from the 55th and 43rd regiments.
Of the anti-tank ones, 3 were deployed with the 140th Blackshirt Battalion, leaving 8 which I assume were 249th and "Derna" batteries. I think we're finally making some progress here, these numbers add up well.
So with 8 75/27s and 8 77/28s, that leaving 26 other calibers, presumably 14 47/32s and 12 20/65s, or maybe some other counting. But since I think Montanari was right about the 47/32s deployed back, I actually disagree.
I think the actual number is 16 47/32s, 10 20/65s, and 8 of every other caliber. The GaF 23 47/32s aren't counted for somse reason, and an additional 4 47/32s were given to the mobile reserve.
Finally, I can be confident that my counting is correct. It just took several hours. I'm not to displeased by this since we got results, but this could've been so much smoother.

The base 231st and 232nd batteries were also in a distinct sector in Albania though it's unclear if they saw combat in Greece. Again, they never made it to Africa.

1. On page 182, he mentions the "1 batteria mista del I gruppo del 22o raggruppamento" as part of the artillery complement of one sector for the defense. No elements of the 22o raggruppamento are described in the other sectors. No further mentions of this unit are made in this chapter.

There's some disagreeing points here:

1. In various sources (other than Montanari) about the order of battle half a year previously in June 1940, the following is said about these batteries:
1st Group: 248th, 249th, and 258th batteries
2nd Group: 246th, 246th Bis, and 258th Bis batteries
3rd Group: 240th, 245th, 247th, and 247th Bis batteries
4th Group: (Irrelevant)
5th Group: 232nd Bis, 242nd, and 243rd batteries
6th Group: 230th Bis and 231st Bis batteries
7th Group: 238th, 239th, 244th, and 245th batteries
It's important to note the distinction between the 1st-3rd groups and 5th-7th groups. The first three were all equipped with lighter guns, while the latter three were equipped with heavier guns. Thus it is not difficult to distinguish between them.
I haven't found any original source for this, but it comes from multiple places and most of them I trust and have few mistakes (compared to Montanari). But it is outdated by January 1941.

2. On page 182, he mentions the 5th, 6th, and 7th Guardia alla Frontiera groups as deployed, but gives no information about their composition.
These descriptions were dated to January 20 by Montanari, but I don't know where he got the information from.

3. On page 332, a report from XXII Corps Chief of Staff Carlo Rostagno mentions only 6 batteries: 242/5, 244/7, 245/7, 246/2, 247/3, and 249/1.
This document's exclusive purpose is to be an order of battle and is highly exhaustive. I haven't found any abnormalities with it other than this.
Three of these are from the 5th-7th groups, while the other 3 are from the 1st-3rd groups.
Notably, none of these batteries have the "bis" designation. I do not know whether these were renamed, removed, or if he just forgot them.
This document has a definitive name attached to it but is also oudated from December 10.

4. In sketch 18 of the companion sketches book, the 5th, 6th, and 7th groups are all drawn, though the 5th is shown under the name "mixed group".
Each group is shown with 2 batteries. The calibers match up with the June 1940 5th-7th groups, except that all have been reduced to the size of 2 batteries (rather than 3, 2, and 4 respectively).
No numbers or names are shown for the individual batteries, but the calibers match up with some of both the regular and bis batteries.
This sketch is dated to January 23 by Montanari but I don't know where he got the information from.

I've come up with the following two possibilities, but keep in mind I might have missed another:

1. There were 9 total batteries. The 5th, 6th, and 7th groups had 2 each, and there were 3 more from the 1st-3rd groups.
Rostagno only mentioned 6 of the batteries because the rest were "bis", and he decided for some reason not to include the "bis" ones.
Between December and January, the latter 3 batteries were disbanded and thus don't show up any in January reports.
It's also possible that all of the bis batteries had left the garrison and returned in January for some reason. But they couldn't have gone far, otherwise they would've been trapped outside by the faster British troops, which makes me wonder where they went at all.

2. There were 6 total batteries, 2 each in the 5th-7th groups. Between December and January the 3 in the 1st-3rd groups were rearmed with heavy guns and subordinated to the 5th-7th groups.
In this possibility, all of the "bis" batteries were already gone by December.

There might not appear to be a significant difference between these options, but what's important is that in one of them, the latter batteries are numbered 230bis-232bis, while in the other, they are numbered 246, 247, and 249.

The key difference is whether you think it was more likely that the Italians "forgot" batteries or "renamed and reequipped" batteries.

It's worth noting that I have also counted up the total artillery gun numbers that Montanari provides, and they are consistent with the 9 initial batteries worth of guns.
But this doesn't rule out 6 batteries, since the guns could have existed as independent single guns rather than batteries, deployed in the defensive posts.
In the 2nd possibility, while the batteries were rearmed, their old guns could still be lying around. The difference is the time at which they were disbanded.

This is irrelevant to the above discussion, but I have no idea how Niehorster got the idea of the mixed engineer detachment in XXXII sector in addition to the one in the XXXI sector.
Not anywhere have I seen this mentioned. He might actually just be extrapolating and making a guess or maybe he just copy-pasted the entire structure of the XXXI sector an called it a day.

I've found more about the reconstitution efforts. The three positions reformed were those of Bir Sheferzen, Fort Maddalena, and some place called Garn um Grein, each with one company.
If 1000 infantrymen were removed to reconstitute 3 companies of defense, that does imply the companies are much larger than I initially believed. They really were pretty oversized, and backed up with machine guns and anti-tank guns besides.
So that means that the XXXI Battalion, if it still existed that is, had a similar strength of 1000 men. So where did the other 1300 men go? Where they in a series of strongpoint-type defensive units? I'll see if I can find out.
But at the very least, I am again considering the 5-company or 5-strongpoint possibility of how the GaF was organized back in Tobruk again. This could use some more thought.

Montanari's "Operations" also mentions a 105/25 gun. This is probably a typo for 105/28 or for 120/25 and I don't know which.
There was a 105/25 gun later, actually an extended 105/23, but this gun wasn't developed until 1942. It was a rare model that was actually a decently modern gun, but of course few ever made it into combat due to building delays. They ended up used by Semoventi.

Apparently G.a.F. guns of 47/32 and 65/17 had been transferred to the Libyan Divisions Group. I am not aware of the origins of these guns and they could have been from any of the sectors that had these guns.
At the same time, the Sirte Division's 65/17 batteries appear to have been transferred to the Cirene Division. Based on other information, though, I'm gonna say that these two batteries were reconstituted by the next battle.

Except we know there were only 12 77/28s when the battle was actually joined. So this chart must not be very accurate, since Rostagno agrees that only 8 were left in batteries by then.
If we take it to be true, the only reasonable series of events is this: By December, most batteries had been disbanded, leaving behind guns. By January, those guns had left... for some reason.

This is dated to December 12, and is 72 guns in total. But what is most noticeable is that this implies there were 18 batteries, much more than Rostagno, or anyone, admits.
We know that the amount of 149/35 fell, as did the amount of 77//28, and by a large margin too. These were likely moved in late December. But the number of 120/25 remains constant.
Thus we can confirm that at this stage, the G.a.F. mustered 12 120/25 and 4 105/28, while the XXII corps later brought 12 more 105/28. This confirms that Rostagno was inaccurate - these must be the 230th-232nd bis batteries.

On second thoughts, I absolutely have no idea how the subsectors even work. The XXX "Bardia" G.a.F. coverage sector has the fortress of Bardia subordinated to it, and we know that that single fortress unit contained 2500 men, despite being just a portion of a subsector.
At the same time, the two secotrs at Tobruk had less men total, and for them, the fortress unit contained the subsectors, not the other way around. Again, this is a place of utter ambiguity, and I am certain that deciphering this situation is beyond my capabilities.

To get a better idea, I went and examined on Niehorster's website a ton of the other GaF sectors. Here's what I've learned that gives me a little more confidence:
What I found out is that the subsectors were functionally administrative units that were responsible for land areas. They didn't have a military organization role.
Their mobile units varied greatly. Some had machine gun companies, alpini battalions, or artillery groups. Others straight up have nothing, like those of the XXXI and XXXII sectors I've been worrying about.
That implies that the issue isn't a lack of information. These sectors legitimately just didn't have any units subordinated to them other than individual strongpoints which contained individual fortifications. There were no companies, platoons, etc.
And since I ignore all units of such small size as part of my game, I think it's safe to ignore them. They had 2300 men, but outside of the XXXI Infantry Battalion, the rest weren't organized in a way that matters for the game.

It might also be the case that the subsectors were merged with the elements of the Sirte Division that took over most of their positions, making their already unclear impact reduced even further.

So basically, the above like 200 lines are just me questioning myself when I already had everything pretty much right. Good job myself!

https://www.arsmilitaris.org/pubblicazioni/arremba.pdf (I lost it once, may that never occur again)
I read a document about the "San Giorgio", honestly this was a very impressive find! Here's the takeaways.
1. The ship was nicknamed the "Lioness of Tobruk" or "Leonessa di Tobruk".
2. The naval battery "Nembo" used guns taken from the sunken destroyer of the same name. Wikipedia says they were sent to Bardia instead but I don't see how that happened. The calibers match up too.
3. Somehow little damage was sustained by the ship, in fact only one of its guns was lost and that was due to wearing out by firing too much.
4. Not only was the ship ordered to fight to the end rather than try to escape, it was also given some contradictory orders that contributed to the lack of action. This was caused by its subordination both to naval command Libia and the Tobruk Fortress.
5. This article claims the XXII Army Corps' engineer raggruppamento contained a 55th Engineer Company.
6. The scuttling attempt was apparently unsuccessful to the point that none of the guns were damaged and the ship could be rapidly refloated. Two sailors were killed in the process, though.
7. Its name references the patron saint and flag of the republic of Genoa.
8. After Pugliese, the second-in-command was a Lieutenant Commander Pane.
(I don't know where the 55th Engineer Company could have come from, since the 55th artieri company was with the "Bari" Division. I can only assume this was actually a signalmen unit)

Just to be prudent once again, let's give another consideration to the nonexistance of the three batteries Rostagno missed.
We have a shortage of 8 120/25, as well as 3 100/27. The latter can be considered relatively inconsequential, but I don't think the same can be said for the former.
There really is no solution to this. The 120/25 gun was a naval gun that was used only by a few units. Maybe some appeared from Tripoli but I have absolutely zero evidence in that direction.
Also, these batteries were definitely not disbanded or reorganized in any way, since we know they were used as direct fire artillery in batteries rather than as anti-tank guns.

The question can also now be reframed in a different way. Rather than 9-battery vs 6-battery, the real debate is over the identities of these batteries.

I have been reading Mario Montanari's "La Prima Offensiva Britannica in Africa Settentrionale" published in 1979. I couldn't find a version in English, but I've gotten through it fine.
However, there is something that is seriously confusing me about his description of the loss of Tobruk in January 1941, and that is the numbers of the Guardia alla Frontiera's artillery batteries.

The sources say the following:

1. There were originally many batteries responsible for Tobruk's defense. However many of them were removed between June 1940 and January 1941. Here's the order as of June 1940:
1st Group: 249 and 2 others
2nd Group: 246 and 2 others
3rd Group: 247 and 3 others
4th Group: 3 others
5th Group: 232 bis, 242, and 1 other
6th Group: 230 bis and 231 bis
7th Group: 244, 245, and 2 others
The others are not unknown; I do know their numbers. However, I am certain they had left the garrison by January so they aren't relevant to this question.
It's important to note the distinction between the 1st-3rd groups and 5th-7th groups. The first three were all equipped with lighter guns, while the latter three were equipped with heavier guns.

2. On page 182, Montanari mentions the 5th, 6th, and 7th Guardia alla Frontiera groups as deployed, but gives no information about their composition.
These descriptions were dated to January 20 by Montanari, but I don't know where he got the information from.

3. On page 332, a report from XXII Corps Chief of Staff Carlo Rostagno mentions only 6 batteries: 242/5, 244/7, 245/7, 246/2, 247/3, and 249/1.
This document's exclusive purpose is to be an order of battle and is highly exhaustive. I haven't found any major abnormalities with it other than this.
This document has a definitive name attached to it but is also oudated from December 10.

4. In sketch 18 of the companion sketches book, the 5th, 6th, and 7th groups are all drawn, though the 5th is shown under the name "mixed group".
Each group is shown with 2 batteries. The calibers match up with the June 1940 5th-7th groups, except that all have been reduced to the size of 2 batteries (rather than 3, 2, and 4 respectively).
No numbers or names are shown for the individual batteries, but the calibers match up with the original 5th-7th groups shown above.
This sketch is dated to January 23 by Montanari but I don't know where he got the information from.

One more thing is known: the guns of all 9 batteries were in the fortress. However, the guns of the three lighter ones were sent to the forward defensive positions rather than being used in batteries behind the lines.
The question is not of what guns were there, but rather, what the numbers of the remaining 6 batteries were. The first three were almost certainly the 242nd, 244th, and 245th, but what about the other three?
I've come up with the following two possibilities, but keep in mind I might have missed another:

1. The batteries shown in the sketches were numbered the 230th-232nd bis.
This requires Rostagno to have missed them in his document for some reason. Maybe he made an honest mistake, maybe he was confused about the "bis" designation, or maybe he was unfamiliar with the G.a.F.

2. The batteries shown in the sketches were numbered the 246th, 247th, and 249th.
This requires the Italian Army or G.a.F. to rename, rearm, and reorganize these three batteries.

The XXII Corps (Pitassi Manella) and Tobruk Military Fortress (Umberto Barberis) were originally separate commands, but on December 10, the date of this document, the commands were merged under Pitassi Manella, with Barberis becoming his subordinate.
I don't know how this merger worked, and it might not have been complete in just one day. Barberis also held the concurrent commands of the Tobruk Military Fortress and the XXI Corps G.a.F. command, which doesn't help.
Ultimately, both me and the AIs I asked came to the conclusion that possibility 1 is more likely. The whole renaming and rearming shebang is quite frankly insane and much less likely than just three batteries slipping through.
Rearming is also unreasonable given the active external threat faced. If the bis batteries left, why didn't they take their guns with them? And if not, how did some guns show up out of nowhere? It's unanswerable.
And no such rearmament is mentioned by any of Montanari's books. I could search harder but at this point I really don't think this insane possibility was real.

And anyway, what could I replace these 3 batteries with? Maybe these three? I guess this is a reserve possibility to keep around in case a huge turnabout occurs.

2. Training Company "Piave"
3. Naval Battery "Nembo"

I briefly had some more questions about the source of the XLII and XLIII groups of the 22nd CAR. THe good news is that I've solved this conundrum.
It does seem the 43rd came from the depot of the 3rd CAR with 105/28, but I can't confirm if this unit stuck around or was eventually reformed using a different HQ. And I later found that the 42nd came from the depot of the 6th CAR with 105/28.
These two units were probably both formed at about the same time and met up with the 22nd CAR on June 1. Neither of these units seem to have G.a.F. origins so that's another theory shot dead.

The Maneuver Army Corps had a CAR, the Raggruppamento Artiglieria di Manovra. Sort of. The unit never actually fought with its corps was only formed out of four groups of 100/17 taken from the Bologna, Pavia, Sabratha, and Brescia divisions.
Half of these ended up in Tobruk where they destroyed. About the other half, I don't know, but I don't think they made it back to Tripolitania safely, that's for sure.
Also, there just never was a 23rd CAR. I'm assuming the reason for this was that the XXIII Corps had been rather newly formed and thus didn't receive its own artillery unit; there just wasn't time to form one.
Carlo Montù's "History of the Artillery" is quite blunt about this. Almost all of the Italian corps had these groups but the one for the 23rd just didn't materialize at this time.

149/13 guns are apparently medium howitzers. I guess caliber is less important than total length in the case of howitzers compared to field guns.
I've seen a claim stating that the "100 series" MG battalions of Italy were all motorized I really don't know if this refers only to the 100-109 battalions or if it includes units with numbers up to 199 (such as the 155th).

The above changes are courtesy of La Guardia alla Frontiera, a book by Massimo Ascoli from the same official publishing office as Montanari's books.
I've started reading through this. Obviously a lot of the book is about the European and Albanian GaF which I don't consider all that interesting, but there's still plenty to see.

Some more information on the units that the Tobruk GaF were withdrawn to replace, based  on Salvatore Castagna's (commander of Giarabub) book:
150th CC.NN. Battalion (of the 250th CC.NN. Legion, of the 4th CC.NN. Division "3 Gennaio"), 75/27 and 47/32 batteries from Maddalena and Garn ul Grein (75/27 were replaced with 77/28)
Prior to this, the 150th CC.NN. Battalion had been split up into its four companies, with the positions of Giarabub, Garn ul Grein, Maddalena, and and Sheferzen each receiving one. 
There were also other elements of the 4th CC.NN. Division in the form of 75/27 batteries. These were presumably also replaced with 77/28 guns from Tobruk.
So, if he is to be believed, four companies, or company-sized units, were withdrawn from Tobruk to defend this position until surrendering.
They were also joined by Castagna's original Libyan machine gun companies, but it seems many of them took the chance to desert since they disappear later in his book.

Salvatore Castagna claims that the GaF companies transferred to Giarabub's defense from Tobruk were the 1st, 3rd, 7th, and 10th GaF companies, as well as some 47/32 and 20/65 guns.
However I don't have any information on what was left behind. Maybe the 2nd, 4th, 5th, 6th, 8th, and 9th companies were kept around but I can't know for sure.
It's also possible that some of these companies corresponded with those from the 31st GaF Infantry Battalion. But again, I can't know for sure because of the inclarity of it all.
And other companies were transferred to Maddalena and random Egyptian rear areas, which I also can't track. So unfortunately this is useful information but not enough.

About the 20/65 guns, they might have been from the DICAT, or the militia for territorial anti-air defense. 2 groups of this force armed with 20/65s are said to be in Tobruk in June.
Montanari mentions this but fails to specifically point out that they were part of the DICAT, so when I read this the first few times I thought it referred to army units. Thanks for the confusion pal.
After that, they are never mentioned again, so I will presume they are among the units that were transferred out to replenish other garrisons.
12 20/65 guns are mentioned as joining the Giarabub garrison, though some such guns may have already been present. I think it's fair to say these were likely the DICAT groups. Let's see what Castagna says.
In his book, he confirms that there were 12 20/65 guns and one loose 77/28 for some reason. He does not mention their place of origin.
The batteries of the DICAT groups were the 201st-203rd for the 1st group and 204th-206th for the 2nd group, all batteries having 8 20/65 guns.

There's also an OOB for the 340th GaF Artillery Raggruppamento:
    342nd Group (818, 819, 837)
    344th Group (842, 843, 844)
    352nd Group (12, 16, 163)
    351st Group (852, 853, 856)
On pages, 202 and 203, the same is stored for the 290th, 330th, and 350th, but I can't be bothered to transcribe them.

An order on 1 June 1940 was meant to disband numerous GaF units, including the 14th Artillery Regiment, 1st Infantry Regiment, and the 31st and 32nd Infantry Battalions.
The 1st Infantry Regiment had been formed in Tripoli and effectively carried out all of tasks that the GaF had accross the entirely of Libya, or at least Tripolitania. Its depot was maintained for infantry and engineers.
I don't know when this was carried out, and I think it wasn't fully carried out at all, with many of these units remaining around until later, including the 31st Infantry Battalion.
At the same time, the depot of the 14th Artillery Regiment was kept around and used to deploy the other Raggruppamenti. So, for sure, this unit was disbanded successfully.
More information about the 14th Artillery Regiment: It is not to be confused with the 14th Artillery Raggruppamento, which was a distinct unit that remained exclusively on the Italian mainland's coast.

At formation, in June, the 31st GaF Artillery Raggruppamento was designated medium, or "medio calibro", while the 32nd was designated light, or "piccoli calibro".

If the 31st Infantry Battalion was disbanded, that does explain a lot, but also leaves just as many fresh questions. Perhaps this unit really was a temporary move like the 1st Infantry Regiment and would be returned back to sector control.
That said, because we know nearly nothing about the organization of these sectors, I'd like to keep it as it is.
It's possible to say that 4 infantry companies => 4 capisaldi and just create these new companies, but given I don't know which capisaldi they were, it's more trouble than it's worth.

The GaF artillery in Europe had a similar structure. Artillery regiments existed to serve as depots but in combat the artillery would be subordinate to the sector/strongpoint/fortification commands.
It appears that some fortifications were classified based on the types and number of guns contained within, but this was discontinued or didn't apply to Africa because those units do not fit into this schema.
This was later changed when the GaF's artillery was merged with existing static artillery. Now medium and small guns could be added with numbers varying from 3-8 for small and 3-6 for medium, though this still didn't apply to African units.
Some batteries were denoted as SP, "sempre pronte", or "always ready". This wasn't really the case though, as these "always ready" batteries took several hours to activate. Still, they were better than regular batteries that could take days.
The average artillery regiment prior to their dissolution down to depots included around 10 or 20 such sempre pronte batteries. None have been found in Africa.

GaF engineer units were truly mixed in nature, including both the men responsible for maintaining field fortifications, as well as those responsible for maintianing electricity, lights, and communication lines.
It appears these units were intended to be roughly company-sized, in fact they had originally been named companies. There were also some loose platoons but these were mostly absorbed. Each subsector would have one platoon from these companies.
Most were trained and equipped at GaF depots, but I've found some evidence indicating that some companies had the privilege of forming at regular army engineer regiment depots.

It seems only the XXI Corps in Cyrenaica had its own GaF command, or any GaF units at all. The others received some attached units, but that was it.
The chronology on this is a bit messed up. According to some, the Tobruk Fortress command was subordinate to this XXI Corps GaF Command role, while according to others it remained independent and later reached Pitassi Manella. Both could be true.
As for Tripolitania, the former Tripoli Fortress command was decisively converted to the XX Corps GaF Command. I don't think this "Tripoli Fortress" formation was ever reformed.

I think only A squadrons of the 6th Australian Division Reconaissance Regiment actually fought at Tobruk. The other 2 squadrons were responsible for the attack on Giarabub.
But like... who cares? The actions of the Australians, ultimately, are not all too relevant to me. They were given one of the easiest victories of the entire war and are still gloating about it.
No such sympathy for the Italians, they can't even get a decent book written about their part in this battle. The best we have is Montanari from last century.

I've also decided to look over some of Montanari's sketches from his second book, "the Second British Offensive in North Africa", which covers the Crusader period. Here's some takeaways:

It appears that the frontier area was not entirely under the command of Di Giorgis. Rather, the frontier was split into three sectors, with regular sectors for Halfaya and Sidi Omar and a special "Fortress Bardia".
"Fortress Bardia" was originally controlled by the Division z.b.V. "Afrika" but after it was relieved by elements of the Savona Division, Di Giorgis was effectively in control of all three.
It is interesting to know, though, that Major Bach really did have his own privileged command separate from the Savona Division that also included the 300th Oasis Battalion.

Not all of RECAM was available to support the Giovani Fascisti Regiment at Bir el Gubi. Most of it had moved north to hold the flank against the 7th Armored Division and was confused in its return.
To be fair, the mediocre amount of support elements and the confused and ad-hoc structure of RECAM itself likely greatly restricted its movement. I'm not surprised that its actions are far from impressive.
During the retreat, both Antelat and Sceleidima received detachments from the 90th Light Africa Division to guard the flank of the retreat. They were presumably pushed aside by the stronger detachments of 7th Support Group.

I also looked at the sketches for El Alamein and the retreat ot Tunisia but there wasn't too much to see. The one thing I did discover is detail about Menton's Kampfgruppe, which stayed out of El Alamein and screened the retreat.
In terms of the GaF, I think finding the exact names of the companies is a fool's task. But there is something we can do. We could consider that maybe the companies were numbered in order.

The GaF unit that was sent to support the Libyan Divisions was a provisional company with 10 47/32 guns. They were somewhere around the Tummar positions but were mixed in with some "regular" anti-tank companies.
While most Autotransportable Type 1940 AS divisions have a single company of 8 47/32 guns, at least for a time the Pavia and Brescia Divisions had a miserable little platoon of 2 47/32s and nothing else.
According to a random guy on the internet's tests, the 100/17 howitzer is much worse than the 100/28 gun, which has a shell with 12% more mass and 45% more range.

Based on a tentative mapping, I would assume the GaF companies were numbered from sea to sea, with 1 at Umm Cuheil and 12 at Es Zeitun.
Moving 1, 3, 7, and 10, and moving the remaining XXXI sectors in taken spots to the former XXXII sector positions, we get the following tentative report:
2nd Company at Bir Junes, 4th Company at Ras el Meduauar, 5th Company at Sidi Daud, 6th Company at Hagfet Umm Haleiga (half).
This is entirely conjecturous, though, which doesn't sit well with me. But it might be the best we can do given we know the XXXI Infantry Battalion had been disbanded.
Alternately I could simply refer to the subunits as capisaldi and establish 4 of them, with 1 at each of the remaining points. However I don't know if they would keep their original names or change when they moved.
I'm gonna need more time to think about this. Perhaps keeping a sort of "infantry battalion" but under a new name is the solution?

Apparently I was wrong that all GaF artillery was static. In fact, despite the lack of SP batteries, most Libyan aritllery that wasn't deployed in the fortified works was motorized.
It was kind of a necessity at this point. I think regular artillery units and corps artillery were also motorized in theory (but often not in practice due to deficiencies in transport).
The best solution to this is not to motorize them, but to at least not condemn them to death as hopelessly static units. And in fact giving them mobile artillery might make their actions at least a little more interesting.

Information on the 22nd CAR beyond its original order of battle is very hard to find. But at the very least I did find a first world war image of one of its siege batteries, the 238th.
There's also a picture of the 239th battery of the GaF online, but nothing else about the GaF batteries can really be found.

Upon its reformation, the Sabratha Division had managed to find about 5000 men from various areas of Libya and replacements coming from Italy. Despite this, it was still highly depleted.
Its 86th "Verona" (both had this designation) Regiment was designated as a "addestramento volontari" or training volunteers regiment due to its chaotic reformation using fresh troops.
It appears to have been formed to a relatively normal template and I can't find anything weird about it other than that it was understrength (but they all were).
Unlike that, the 86th "Verona" Regiment was established from the temporary Infantry Regiment "Sabratha" from a battalion of each as well as their mortar, anti-tank, and infantry gun companies.
It also had the attached units: 340th Anti-Tank Company, 18th and 42nd Artillery Groups (unknown), spare 88/55 and 75/46 batteries, a MILMART battery, and of course the 3rd San Marco Marine Battalion and Giovani Fascisti Regiment.
Since in my world the Sirte Division follows this lineage, what units should I attach to it? I'm gonna work on just the battalions for now and not worry about the support. It has:
61st "Sirte" Infantry Division: (auto-transportable type AS)
    69th "Ancona" Training Volunteers Regiment
    70th "Ancona" Infantry Regiment
        Former Infantry Regiment "Sirte"
    Artillery Raggruppamento "Sirte" (Listed as 42nd Artillery Regiment but this was disbanding)
    61st Mixed Enginer Battalion
    Infantry Regiment "Giovani Fascisti" (Only Temporarily)
Not Added:
    3rd "San Marco" Battalion
    18th Artillery Group
    42nd Artillery Group (AA)
Companies:
    340th 65/17 Battery (Former G.a.F?)
    75/46 Battery (Unknown Number)
    German 88mm Battery (Unknown Number)
    14th (76/30) Regia Marina MILMART Battery
I don't have a clue where either of these groups appeared or what happened to them. Maybe they were corps artillery?

Back to the GaF companies and the phantom 31st battalion. By this point I don't see any realistic timeline where the battalion survives. It has no reason to exist and is never seen again.
But I think now it's time to maybe step back and see the ambiguity in a different light. I know for a fact that this battalion was gone. And I have no idea what replaced it.
But if I don't know after all this digging, only the archives of the XXI Corps GaF Command in Italy know. They might even not. And I'm not going there. Nobody is anymore.
By all means, I am now the trailblazer in this field. I might actually be at the forefront of it. There might be nobody in the world, at least on the internet, who is at this point. I... have won.
No more need to read forums. I have all the knowledge I need to write those answers myself. It's been a long journey since the von Barby incident.
So yes. I am going to make my own semi-arbitrary decision regarding these areas. And if anyone wants to dispute me, let them try! They'll find my evidence for me.
I can only gain from going out on a limb. Let's go!

Let's return to reason.
There were 3300 men in June and 2300 in December.
Initially, the strongpoints of the 31st Sector had 300 men on average and those of the 32nd Sector had 250 men on average.
1000 men left Tobruk. They must necessarily be equal or similar in strength to the 4 GaF companies that replaced the 150th CC.NN. battalion. So each company had 250 men.
The reasonable math says that each sector must have lost 2 strongpoints, leaving 8. This doesn't align with the map, but there's another option.
Why would Barberis be so stupid as to have no reserves? If he deployed all 4 of his reinforcement companies, no reserve exists until the El Adem position. That's the thing I've been missing.
The 32nd Sector must have had more men. The full 4 strongpoints in fact, covering the positions alongside the reinforcement infantry. Another 2 from the 31st sector backed them up.
The remaining 2 from the 31st sector secured Meduauar and Sidi Cheiralla. In effect, the 31st sector took over the entirety of subsector 32/B.
That does mean, though, that I have to fit 4 more company-sized units into my order of battle. Unless we allow ourselves a little suspension of disbelief and... disappear the 32nd sector's 4 companies.

So the final result of all this? Instead of the 31st Infantry Battalion, we have the 31st/A Subsector. Nothing consequential happened.

Let's try to figure out some directionality in the subsectors. Nothing is provided for those of Tobruk, so instead I took a look at those of Tripoli.
For the 35th Coverage Sector, the subsectors move clockwise around the fortress. But that's it. None of the others gave me anything of value.
I guess I'll just go with the clean assumption. They went clockwise in Tobruk as well, to ensure a clean move from GaF to XXII command, these sectors were kept (except for a new one getting created).
But that means the 31st/A sector with 4 strongpoints covered the Acroma direction, while the 31st/B sector covered the Derna road. This one was replaced by the VdL battalion, so it's gone, leaving just the 31st/A.

One final wrench in the works is Montanari's claim that the XXXII sector just... didn't show up? Like it was assigned to cover the east, yet it is never mentioned as actually defending that area in the battle.
Rather, the XXXI sector gets the credit even though it was meant to be on the other side of the defensive network. It would've made absolutely no sense for them to swap, so what happened?
Perhaps the XXXII sector had just vanished by this point and, with few units left to handle, it was decided to merge the 2 sectors into a single one. I'm gonna take this as my guess.
It's entirely projective, but if so, clears up a lot. The 31st sector had been fully reorganized by Pitassi Mannella to take on the new organization of the defense. Thus the XXXII sector was entirely forgotten.

Here's my final reading of events:

June:
XXXI Sector: 6 Strongpoints, 1500 men
XXXII Sector: 6 Strongpoints, 1800 men

(Strange that XXXII is larger, as it had a shorter front to hold. But it would have been facing the most likely direction of an attack so it might have been prioritized.)

December:
XXXI Sector: 4 Strongpoints, 1000 men
XXXII Sector: 4 Strongpoints, 1300 men

Early January:
XXXI Sector: 8 Strongpoints, 2300 men
    XXXI/A Subsector: 2 Strongpoints, 500 men
    XXXI/B Subsector: 2 Strongpoints, 500 men
    XXXII/A Subsector: 2 Strongpoints, 600 men
    XXXII/B Subsector: 2 Strongpoints, 600 men
    +About 100 Men Somewhere
(XXXII Sector is never mentioned again)

This, above, is what the geographic analysis would give. However, I do have another method of analysis that I think gives me more valuable information.
That comes in the form of the final subsectors. Subsectors A and B are marked in Montanari's map with nothing, while Subsectors C, D, and E are marked with the 1st/70th, 3rd/70th, and "Volontari della Libia" Battalion HQs.
This makes me think that in contradiction to the geographic approach, in reality these other subsectors that were of the former XXXII Sector had all been disbanded. Only A and B were left and they were part of the XXXI Sector.

After Pitassi Mannella's Reorganization:
XXXI Sector: 8 Strongpoints, 2300 men
    XXXI/A Subsector: 4 Strongpoints, 1100 men
    XXXI/B Subsector: 2 Strongpoints, 600 men
    Strongpoint Hagfet Umm Haleiga: 300 Men
    Strongpoint Ras El Meduauar: 300 Men
(XXXII Sector is never mentioned again)

Hooray for cleanliness!

Another interesting question, not related to research but something to relax moreover, is the deployments in this alternate state. I kinda half-assed them but how would Pitassi Mannella as overall commander deploy his forces?
I'll probably just add this to events.txt though. No need clogging up my effective research log with entirely hypothetical material.

Some claims have indicated that two battalions of blackshirts and a machine gun battalion were sent to Tobruk in the early phases (Summer 1940) to bolster the ineffective GaF.
I'm well aware of what these two blackshirt battalions are. It could also be a typo for the two companies of the 140th Battalion, or it could be a reference to two that were later moved.
But for this machine gun battalion, I have no idea. Maybe it was just the native machine gun battalion of one of the divisions? It wasn't the Sirte since that was in Tunisia, but could easily be the Catanzaro.

There are translated versions of several of Montanari's books that can be found on Archive, but most of them are quite poor.
It's quite obvious that the translator, while somewhat fluent in Italian, has little to no knowledge about the actual structure and units of the Italian Army and shows little consistency with them.
There is at least some good news though. The scanning on these is somewhat higher quality, so I can see the illustrations with more detail. We can finally read the weird Tobruk map from "Operations" book 1.
Unfortunately, it's barely a help. There are 2 units that before confused me, and now I can read them clearly as 5/26 and 1/35. Both are labelled as artillery groups/battalions.

But this solves nothing. Let's look at 1/35 first because it's more obvious. There are a couple units the "35" might be, but all are false and here's why:
1. 35th Artillery Regiment. This was part of the 20th "Friuli" Infantry Division. None of this division ever went to North Africa.
2. 35th Coastal Artillery Raggruppamento. This was in the dodecanese, along with the 36th and 55th Raggruppamenti of the same type.
But... they had the 18th, 23rd, 29th, 30th, 32nd, 34th, 35th, 36th, 39th, 40th, and 41st groups. None are the 1st, so none of them could have contributed 1/35.
This is quite apparently a typo and its the first crack in this map. And I think another is right there with it:

5/26 quite obviously can't exist either. The 26th Artillery Regiment was from the Pavia Division and was definitely involved in the fighting, but it had no 5th group.
There's also the 26th Corps Artillery Raggruppamento from Albania, but this had only the 18th, 32nd, 115th, and 116th Heavy Howitzer Groups, none of which are the 5th.
Again, yet another thing that cannot possibly be true. The reliability of this map is truly impressive in how poor it is.
In "The First British Offensive" a similar map shows that this unit is actually the 105th Battery of the 25th CAR. How 105 became 5 and 25 became 26 is anybody's guess. A truly immense mistranscription.

I've managed to identify where almost all of the Libyan Coastal battalions were located. Here they are:
30th Libyan Coastal Battalion: XXVIII Coverage Sector
31st Libyan Coastal Battalion: Tobruk, XXXII Coverage Sector
32nd Libyan Coastal Battalion: XXIX Coverage Sector
33rd Libyan Coastal Battalion: Spread Out From Agedabia to the Libyan Sahara
34th Libyan Coastal Battalion: Bengasi-Barce Area, Later Reformed With Remnants, Maletti Group
35th Libyan Coastal Battalion: XXXI Coverage Sector (Later Moved)

This is not perfect, as it relies on Montanari's unreliable maps and an unreliable forum post, but it's better than nothing and proves that most of these units did exist.

Just learned some more Italian. Apparently, the reason for the abbreviations GG.FF. and CC.NN. with the doubled letters is because they were plural. A single "young fascist" would just be a G.F.

A standard Blackshirt battalion in 1940 had just 3 companies, but this was no longer the case for the African divisions, each of which was formed with 4 companies.
So, while the African Blackshirt Legions were by no means strong, they at least had 12 infantry companies rather than just 6, totalling almost 3000 men like a normal regiment rather than having half strength like most legions.

The DAK had some corps units that are shrouded in a bit of mystery. Luckily Niehorster's OOB for 22 June 1941 handles this. It appears that at this time the Afrika Division had no assigned units yet.
Sümmermann is also shown in command, and at the time was still a colonel (oberst). 155th Infantry Regiment is also shown as a loose staff with no subordinate units (all 5 3rd Battalions are corps troops).
As for communications, some fixed radio stations that I have no information are there, plus some military geology sections and the 900th engineer battalion.
The 523rd, 528th, 529th, and 533rd coastal artillery batteries are under the command of a 221st coastal artillery regiment. All are armed with captured 15cm French guns.
Finally, there are communications units. Those are Nachrichten-Abteilung Libyen (later Panzerkorps-Nachrichten-Abteilung 475), and the 3rd company of Panzerkorps-Nachrichten-Abteilung 56.

I do not know why the 2/13th Australian Battalion is sometimes listed under the Polish Independent Carpathian Brigade. While I've seen this quite a lot, Andreas at Rommelsriposte claims this never occurred.
Rather, the battalion was the infantry support of 32nd Army Tank Brigade and later part of 16th and 23rd Infantry Brigades of 70th Infantry Division.
I am no expert on the later phases of the Siege of Tobruk so I cannot offer an independent opinion. But if I had to guess, they were temporarily under Polish command but this didn't remain so when they entered combat.

Also Niehorster's OOB for the ARMIR has an 14th Arresto Company. I don't know if this is different or the same from a "Combattimenti di Arresto" company but I'm going to assume they are different.
Google Translate isn't perfect, but it calls the latter a "delaying" company which implies a mixed or infantry unit while the former is shown as an engineer unit. Either me or Niehorster could be making a mistake here but I'll take the blame for now.

Apparently the batteries of the Australian anti-tank regiments were numbered, and there were four each. So that's a whole bunch of mistakes I've recently made.

While it's not quite apparent that the XXXI G.a.F. Infantry Battalion didn't survive and was likely dissolved between June 1940 and December 1940, the fate of its 6 companions deployed in Albania unknown.
I'm not very well read in the Albanian-Greek campaigns and even if I were, odds are the shadowy job theire G.a.F. performed there would be just as difficult to discern as their fate in North Africa.

It's time to return the favor. I've finally worked up the courage to return to Wikipedia (which I haven't been on for a while) and return all I've gained so that future researchers won't have to do the same.

On tracking Lomax's 16th Infantry Brigade, after joining in the destruction of the Libyan Divisions Group at Sidi Barrani, appears to have moved to take a peripheral role in the battle of Bardia.
After that however, it appears to have returned to base to join the rest of the 6th/70th Division in planned operations against Syria. It wouldn't got to Tobruk until late 1941.

The Maletti Group's commonly cited order of battle at Nibeiwa is not the correct one. While it is true that the Raggruppamento had a mixed tank battalion with two companies of different types on formation, this changed.
In December, once the group was stationary, its tanks returned to their original units, and another tank battalion, the 2nd Medium, was temporarliy attached. It was this one that was destroyed at Nibeiwa.

If every necessary, here's an OOB for the opposing forces at Bir el Gubi:

22nd Armored Brigade
    2nd Royal Gloucestershire Hussars
    3rd County of London Yeomanry
    4th County of London Yeomanry
    (158 Total Crusader Tanks)
    C Battery, 4th Royal Horse Artillery
    B Company, 1st King's Royal Rifle Corps
    3rd Battery, 1st Light Anti-Aircraft Regiment
    Small Detachments of AT and Engineers

132nd Armored Division "Ariete"
    132nd Medium Tank Regiment (7th, 8th, and 9th Battalions)
    8th Bersaglieri Regiment (3rd Mixed Support, 5th, and 12th Battalions)
    132nd Artillery Regiment (1st and 2nd Battalions of 75/27)
    3rd Group, 3rd Fast Artillery Regiment (75/27)
    1st Group, 24th Corps Artillery Raggruppamento (105/28)

1700 lines later (first mentioned on line 352), we finally have answers to the question of where 232 different HMGs managed to sneak into the 1940 AS Autotransportable Division TOE.
The counting is the following:
    24 HMGs per infantry regiment (48 Total)
    18 HMGs from artillery regiment
    92 HMGs from L3 Tanks
    72 HMGs from the MG Battalion (12 per company)
    5 in the Bersaglieri Motorcycle Company
    9 in the Replacement Battalion (8 in the A/A company and 1 from idk)
    2 in the 65/17 batteries (apparently)
    222 Total... I guess 10 disappeared
The math works out but this was rarely true in reality. The tank battalion often didn't show up and the MG battalion often had less companies.
Also, some TOEs straight up show the machine gun battalion with only three companies, which makes more sense in terms of size, so I don't know what the Italians were cooking with this one.
Not with standing the 10 lost guns, since the actual MG battalions rarely had 6 companies, in practice the AS 1940 divisions were much weaker in machine gun firepower than they should have, contributing to their poor infantry performance.

Also found the theoretical composition of a Raggruppamento Genio di Corpo d'Armata
    Special Combat Engineer Battalion
    Signal Battalion
    Mobile Dovecote
    Chemical Company
There's no confirmation but I can be decently sure that this was the structure of the 22nd Corps support elements. Unless they had been caught out side the pocket, which Rostagno suggests occurred.
We do see that signals were kind of a mess and the 55th Signal Company probably was brought into the garrison to deal with this, so maybe that's another sign that the existing corps engineers were gone.

At Derna with the Tonini parachute troops was the elusive "Raggruppamento Pancano". I was unable to find literally anything about this unit, but thankfully Jeff Leser identifies it as the reconnaissance force of the Babini Group.

The histories of the 10th, 22nd, and 25th CARs are all quite hard to decipher. Little to nothing is known about any of them and most of them had absolutely unimpressive careers prior to their destruction.
Here's what is known:
10th CAR: Formed originally for use in Albania. Reformed for use in Africa with the 7th, 17th, and 35th groups. Only 7th and 17th groups remained by January.
Nothing is known about the fate of the 35th group. I did find a mention of it in a blog post about 1943, but that's far ahead so it doesn't give me much information.
22nd CAR: Formed from the 1st, 42nd, and 43rd groups and sent to Africa (Acroma). Was part of the GaF Tobruk artillery on June 17.
Only the 1st group and a loose battery were left by January. Nothing is known about the XLII and XLIII groups, neither of which are ever mentioned again with this caliber
25th CAR: Formed in Naples from the 105th, 130th, 148th, and 150th groups and sailed into Tobruk. Destroyed nearly immediately.

Big news! I've identified the 42nd group as surviving Compass. It pops up again in an order of battle for the Sabratha Division after Crusader, so it must have been detached.
This also makes a bit of sense since the 42nd group has the same number as the Sabratha's organic artillery regiment, which was mostly destroyed during Compass, but I guess this spare group survived.
But... this is incorrect. Because this XLII group was not a regular artillery group. It was an anti-aircraft group with 75/50 guns. Back to square one.
It later turns up at El Alamein, where the one battery of this group is identified as the 5th. Can't be sure if this is the same battery that was with the Sabratha Division, but either way my hopes are dashed.
In a rare moment of insight, Wikipedia has details about this group. There were four such groups, the XLI, XLII, XLIII, and XLIV, with what seems to be both the XLII and XLIII groups getting sent to Africa.
It appears each group had two batteries. They were armed with captured Czechoslovak 75/50 guns, which proved a decent gun almost comparable to the 88mm.

There's also an XLIII pack group that turns up in East Africa, but that's neither here nor there. Just an interesting tidbit that the numbers refused to be distinct.

I don't know what the establishment structure of the artillery specialist detachments were, since they were very unique units only assigned to corps artillery regiments.
However, I can now begin to take a guess that they were not artillerymen themselves. Rather, from similar "specialist" units in anti-tank roles, they were fire controllers and signalmen, and had no assigned firepower.

Also, the WW2 channel / Eastory screwed up again. In their maps of the Sicily landings, they showed only the Hermann Göring Division attacking the bridgehead at Gela, when in reality the Italian Livorno Division joined in.
This is one instance where the Italians really did perform well, and I would argue their troops showed much more courage given how outgunned they were.
It really is dangerous to hide such events from the public eye. The Italian Army was not useless as is often said and while they made many of mistakes there were numerous flashes of brilliance.
The refusal to display those may be an unintentional mistake or it may be the case of the animator deciding that the Italian contribution "wasn't important", but either way, it's a massive error that ruins my enjoyment of the video.

Niehorster claims that none of the divisional motorcycle Bersaglieri companies were ever formed. This was true as of June 1940 but was no longer true by December, when at least some companies had been received.
Certainly the 60th, 61st, and 63rd were formed and were with or near their divisions, but I can't really say the same for the other Type 1940 AS divisions which mostly had no such companies.

One of the strangest units I've ever seen was the 9th independent infantry battalion that shows up as part of the independent Giarabub-Siwa sector, along with the 4th Libyan Battalion.
But now, both of these units have been decently identified, so here they are:
IX Independent Infantry Battalion: A mixed support battalion formed for the 16th "Pistoia" Infantry Division, which was undergoing reorganization to Type 1942 AS structure.
Swapped names and places with the previous infantry battalion. Now as a regular infantry battalion, sent to Africa where it was probably destroyed in the retreat from El Alamein.
4th Libyan Battalion: The former 18th Libyan Battalion which had started Compass in Cyrenaica but had managed to get out prior to the annihilative battle of Beda Fomm.

There were also other independent infantry battalions from 1-10, all of which were sent to North Africa, as well as the 156-163, 185, 186, and 190, none of which went to Africa.
All were formed as AS 42 battalions. Some were absorbed and vanished, while others like the 9th were absorbed but took over the previous battalion's old elements.

The structure of the GaF is underexplained by all the sources, so let's go over it again:
Comando di Corpo d'Armata > Settore > Sottosettore > Caposaldo (fort) > Centro (1 position) > Nucleo (fireteam)
The Caposaldo was a company-sized formation, in rough terms, while the Centro was roughly platoon-sized unit. Larger units varied highly in size and establishment structure.

I've also found out why there's no static machine gun companies in Tobruk, except for the ambiguous 31st Infantry Battalion and its 3 machine gun companies.
This is because the static machine gun companies were interval units, meant to defend areas between fortifications. In Tobruk, where the forts were in an unbroken belt among the desert's flat terrain, this was not necessary.
Also, while Libyan static machine gun companies did exist in decent numbers in Africa, not only were none present in Tobruk, but they weren't part of the G.a.F in the first place and were functionally regular army units.
Also, some people are disagreeing about whether the XXXI and XXXII sectors were in the west and east, respectively, or the other way around. How this is even a disagreement, I cannot understand.

One more thing. I'm actually going to go insane. While the 5th, 6th, and 7th battalions of the G.a.F. were subject to the 31st Raggruppamento, this was not how they were formed.
Rather, they were formed as the 31st Groups of the 5th, 6th, and 8th G.a.F. Regiments.
I was entirely wrong about this, but it won't end up having an effect on the final orders of battle since having all the groups numbered as the 31st would be confusing as hell.

There is another mention of the 105/28 XLII group, but it's quite dubious. Wikipedia claims that such a group was previously attached to the Pavia Division.
But... there's a problem. In the original source this lists, "The Royal Army to 2000", the caliber of this group is never listed. And given context there's a good chance this was actually an anti-aircraft group.
It also lists a II/24th Group with 75/27 guns, but the 24th "Piemonte" Artillery Regiment's records never show any of its units moving to North Africa. This is likely another mistake, with the actual unit being from the 24th CAR.

Back to the 22nd CAR, I can't confidently say that not both of its 105/28 groups remained in Tobruk. 105/28s of roughly group or larger size appear at the frontier in December, but they might not even be real.
Originally that area had 2 total groups of 105/28 (1 group and 3 batteries in the sources) but it later had 2 groups and 2 spare batteries. That said, maybe these were the same units with a more confusing organization.
Not helping is another mention of "2 groups of 105/28" in a slightly earlier and less detailed report. These were probably the 2 105/28 groups (I and II) from the 21st CAR.
The 21st CAR does reappear with these 2 groups in Bardia so it's somewhat unlikely they survived, however. A decently reasonable possibility is that these two groups came instead from the 10th and 22nd CARs.
Another final possibility is that these were part of the 5th or 10th Army's Artillery Raggruppamento, neither of which I am sure had 105/28 guns but either of which could've recieved them from elsewhere.
The truth is not yet discernable from the fragmentary evidence above.

Given the confusing orders of battle, let's try to count the 105/28 guns that actually show up in the individual battles based on Montanari's narratives.
Raggruppamento Maletti:
    Nibeiwa: 1 105/28 Battery (from II/21st CAR)
    (Later information contradicts this and says it was from the XLIII/22nd CAR)
2nd Libyan Division:
    Tummar East: 1 105/28 Battery (from II/21st CAR)
    Ras el Dai: 1 105/28 Battery (from II/21st CAR)
1st Libyan Division + 4th CC.NN. Division:
    Sidi Barrani: 1 105/28 Group (Identity unknown. Maybe it was a GaF unit?)
63rd Division "Cyrene":
    Alam el Rabia: 1 105/28 Battery (from I/21st CAR)
    Point 236: 1 105/28 Group (-1 Battery) (from I/21st CAR)

Later: 3 Groups of 105/28 Destroyed
The 3rd of the groups was probably from the Cirene Division which had the I/21st CAR group. But actually now this is confirmed true.
So this confirms roughly that the 2 105/28 groups from the 21st CAR were lost. But there is a 3rd one that we cannot identify. Maybe the process of elimination will help here.

It's now known, by the way, that there wasn't a 25th group with the 10th CAR. Rather, the 25th group was formed by the 12th CAR with 149/35 guns. Classic typo.
10th CAR (2 (17, 35)): 17th destroyed at Tobruk, 35th destroyed at Bardia
20th CAR (2): Both destroyed at Beda Fomm
21st CAR (2): Both destroyed at Sidi Barrani
22nd CAR (2 (42, 43)):
23rd CAR: Didn't Exist Lol

Let's now return to the chart that Montanari provided on Page 258 of "Operations" and see if we can decipher it.

With all the above research, here's the changelist:
Composite Battery of the 22nd CAR renamed to a Mixed Battery (42nd and 43rd Groups weren't there)
(This was already a change, just now it's locked in and has actual historical evidence behind it)
...And that might be it. I've learned a lot, but it's mostly confirmations of what I'd already approximated using pure assumption.

A couple claims I've seen say that the Free French 1st Marine Infantry Battalion participated at Tobruk and Sidi Barrani.
That said, these claims are on internet forums by people who really don't sound like the most trustworthy and show little respect for the Italians, which rubs me the wrong way.
With no decent evidence that this actually happened and had a major impact on the battle, I have no reason to execute on this potential change.
The Italians do seem to agree that some units of Free French were on the western direction, at least according to Montanari. Their identity remains unknown but it lends credence to French claims.

With more research having been done, this decision will be reversed. I now have much more information on this unit and know it was at Tobruk.
It was formed from a total of 500 personnel, defectors who escaped in two groups, one of 150 men from Lebanon and the other of 350 men from Cyprus.
The resulting battalion, the 1st Marine Infantry Battalion (1er Bataillon d'Infanterie de Marine), had two companies and was commanded by a Captain Folliot.

On another note, it appears the Royal Northumberland Fusiliers (1st MG Battalion) were transferred immediately from 4th Indian to 6th Australian Division when the swap occurred.
On the other hand, the Cheshire Regiment's 1st Battalion, another MG Battalion, appears to have been attached to the same division only in mid-January.

Also, I've heard many people and sources call the structure of the Blackshirt divisions "poor". While undoubtably these units performed badly I think this was due to the quality of the men, not the structure.
In reality, I think the structure was fairly reasonable. It was just like the Type 1940 AS Autotransportable Division but with 1/3 less artillery and similarly reduced infantry.
People have also said that the armament of the blackshirt battalions was poor. Again I have to disagree. They have a similar number of machine guns and slightly less mortars, but those were the outdated 45mm mortars.
Other than that, the only major differences are the loss of the replacement and light tank battalions, neither of which I consider to be all that damaging of a change.

One other interesting thing to think about, though it's not much use for wargames, is how the "effective" GaF was reconstituted in Cyrenaica after its conquest by Rommel.
Niehorster is the only source I have about this, but I haven't searched very hard. It seems a brigade-sized unit had become responsible for Tobruk's Garrison as part of a larger Tobruk\Cyrenaica fortress command.
It contained the 3rd Battalion, 350th GaF Infantry Regiment, 7th Bersaglieri Battalion, 5th Libyan Battalion, 18th Carabinieri Battalion, 345th Coastal Artillery Group (8 120/25), and the 90th Group, 2nd Anti-Aircraft Regiment (12 75/46).
The 8 120/25 guns that the 345th Group contained could've been the guns from the 230th Bis and 231st Bis batteries of the G.a.F. that were part of the XXXI/6th Group and were destroyed in the same town.

Attemping to count every 120/25 gun challenge: There were 74 operational total according to some claims.
By December 1941, 5 batteries (847, 849, 850, 851, and 859) still used this gun. These were all from the former Fortress Tripoli.
That's not it, though only 4 batteries, two with just three guns, are listed back in June. I can't tell where the bonus battery appeared from.
4 batteries of unknown number were assigned to the 30th Raggruppamento at Bardia. All were destroyed in that battle.
Tobruk originally had 4 batteries of 120/25. One was sent to Bardia and it seems the other three stayed there and were destroyed in that battle.
This is slightly messed up as Montanari only mentions 7 such batteries between Tobruk and Bardia, which seems to be the better number.
Finally, 7 batteries of unknown number were in the garrison of Addis Ababa and were destroyed in that battle.

The total thus reaches between 18 batteries and 20 batteries. That's not far from 74 if we assume that some of the batteries had just 3 guns or had more.
I think the best answer to this is 19 batteries for a normal 76 guns, except for the 2 batteries that were down a gun.
Final Count:
Tripoli: 14
Tobruk: 16 (242, 243, 230bis, 231bis)
Bardia: 16
Addis Ababa: 28

Another blessing. Looking through the archives of Comando Supremo, I've found another post by Jeff Leser that proves my guesses about Bardia right.
The XLII and XLIII groups are now confirmably there, having been used to form the previously nonexistent 23rd CAR.
So, I guess I was wrong about that, since the 23rd CAR was never established at a depot but managed to form in the field with these two groups at full strength.
Unless... this was a mistake by Maurizio. It's certainly a possibility. I'll make sure never to include this 23rd CAR in any of my Orders of Battle.
In addition, he notes two 149/35 batteries (238 GaF, 239 GaF) and a 105/28 battery (232 bis GaF), and the 15th and 16th 20mm batteries, also from the 22nd CAR.

Somebody has mentioned a 502nd 20/65 Battery under the command of the Brescia Division. This probably was part of the replacement for its old artillery lost at Tobruk.
Nobody in the archives responded to him, so I have nothing else to go on. I couldn't find anything on the internet either.
The numbering is also quite weird. This unit has nothing in common with any of the units in the Brescia Division, so its very existence if so would be an abberation.
I have now solved this, as it appears that this battery was in fact in the same series as the 503rd and 504th. It was subordinated to the 1st Fast Artillery Regiment.

I haven't mentioned this previously, I believe, but I just thought about how thin the defenders actually were. It's a surprise they even tried.
On a frontline of 54,000 meters, they had 24,000 men, 340 guns, and 23,000 mines. And only about half of those men would have actually been frontline troops, and many of the mines were defective.
Thus, for a frontline of 5 meters, a single man with 2 possibly defective mines would be present. That's almost nothing against even a single squad of attackers. It's no surprise they lost the battle.
Also, the rear line was a shorter 25,000 meters, but the need to split the already-thin combat infantry between two lines definitely further weakened each one individually.

Another thing that I think I've discovered is that the anti-aircraft battery of the tank infantry regiments was typically the 7th, counting after 2 battalions of c companies each.
This is only confirmed for the Ariete Division's 132nd Regiment which transferred it to the Artillery Regiment, so this is quite hard to prove, but it's the best I have so far.

An opposing source notes the existence of a 33rd and 133rd 20/65 Companies, which could only be from the Littorio Division. If this was the case, you'd expect the 4th to have a 4th as well.
Still, a 4th battery does appear, but only as part of Raggruppamento Maletti, and this may have been a Libyan unit of some kind if not from the 4th Tank Infantry Regiment.
But if this unit was with Maletti, there's no reason to think it made it out alive. Rather, I think there's one more option that I just haven't considered so far yet makes sense.
This battery might not have been organic. What if it had been transferred from the Sirte Division, say, the 261st battery? That would solve another question at the same time.
Of course I'll still have to search for better, but for now, this is legitimately the best option I can imagine and is the one I will be using in future OOBs.

An updated counting of guns is thus probably in order by this point, and may in fact be used to establish my planned final set.

An interesting thing I found was that the 256th GaF Battery, which consisted of 65/17 guns, managed to make it to Africa to defend Derna from its previous deployment in the Alps.
A truly unlucky unit, given that none of its compatriots from up there met the same fate until 1943 or later.
Actually no, because the 252nd GaF Battery actually did meet the same fate. Imagine being one of these gunners, sent to another continent just to be captured in the conclusion to a great disaster.

The entire 12th GaF artillery regiment from which these batteries (252-257) came from had been sent to North Africa near the end of 1940 and deployed forward to the 10th Army.
It was on the French front at the beginning of the war and reached Naples by the 24th of June. Their ship left on December 26 and they joined combat on the 5th of January with the Sabratha Division.
Only two batteries escaped Beda Fomm, one of the 257th. This battery would ultimately be destroyed along with the rest of the Savona Division when it ran out of supplies at Halfaya.

To this day, I still can't figure out what the artillery specialist companies actually did. I can take a guess, but without much information about that, I'd probably do well to not add it to any listings.

The 65/17 batteries of the Sirte Division were sent to support the Cirene Division in June. As we know, by the later battle two such batteries still existed, which raises osme questions.
The two possiblities are that the batteries managed to retreat safely or were transferred back (which seems unlikely but possible) or that two new batteries were formed (of numbers I cannot know).
In fact, this source has enough detail that I can make an attempt to count up where all the 65/17 batteries of the various divisions were.
19,20 "Pavia" (actually Brescia) - 1st Libyan Division
69,70 "Sirte" - 63rd "Cirene" Infantry Division
27,28 "Brescia" (actually Pavia) - 62nd "Marmarica" Infantry Division
15,16 "Savona" - 1st CC.NN Division "23 Marzo"
So, the batteries that survived are those of the Bologna and Sabratha Divisions. Lemme see if any records of the Sabratha's are around.
My check indicates that the two batteries of the Sabratha Division stuck around and were between Derna and Mechili. So it could only be the Bologna now.
One of them was part of the 1st "nucleo motorizzato" of the Sabratha Division's deployment, while the other doesn't appear in OOBs but does appear in maps and was presumably transferred to Babini's control.

Also, Maurizio, the above source, admits himself that his information is erroneous in some parts. And I'm gonna have to agree.
It makes much more sense for the 2 batteries sent to the Cirene Division to be the ones from the Bologna Division. Why rob a fellow division in the theater when you could rob the 5th Army instead?

Based on similar circumstances from Bardia, the 4 companies deployed to support the GaF in Barberis's sector were probably not actually deployed in the strongpoints.
At Bardia, the 63rd Machine Gun Battalion had its 4 companies split into company strongpoints behind the lines. It seems fairly reasonable for the Tobruk Garrison to make the same decision.

One battery of the 105th/25th CAR was numbered the 10th. I did a bit of research and apparently the groups were individually numbered with 105/28 guns getting numbers 1-50.
This is cool, but I have absolutely no means of obtaining the numbers for any other similar units so unfortunately I won't be able to do much with this and won't execute on it.
Also this source baited me, since I initially searched for batteries and I instead got information about battalions/groups. Not much help here...
On a similar note, one battery of the 2nd/21st CAR was numbered the 4th. I have nothing else on this matter.

I found a book about Italian artillery's entire history. Only 1 book, Volume 16, deals with WW2 and it's fragmentary and shares information with Montanari, but it's much, much better than nothing.
Here's the main things I've found from it:

Tobruk:
I (and Montanari) was right that 1 battery from the CL group was tranferred to the CXLVIII group.
The XXXI/6 group had at least 1 120/25 battery. That's something I got temporarily wrong, and can only be blamed on Rostagno.
But... it's possible that this was actually the 242nd Battery, though that also creates more questions than answers.
A group "Tramontin" (the 55th Artillery Regiment's commander) was formed as a sub-group of the central artillery group.
Here's my guess as to its order of battle:
    II/43
    III/55
    CV/25 Corps
    (Maybe naval units)
Pitassi Mannella was an attaché with the Ecuadorian Army in the early 1920s. His title of "King of Artillerymen" was quite well-earned.
The commander of the I Group, 12th "Sila" Artillery Regiment was a Lt-Col. Nicolò.

Wider Compass:
At the start of the Libyan campaign, there were 72 105/28 guns and 24 149/13 guns. Other numbers are provided but they are too large for me to reasonably count up.
There are also numbers for the Aegean and for metroplitan Italy, but these numbers are also not all that helpful for me. Still, it's nice to have this in my back pocket.
Also, this data is backdated to December 1939. It's not too far back, but it also could have some serious deficiencies since I know major changes occurred in early 1940.

At least some kind of artillery command for the 23rd CAR existed, under the command of a Colonel Luigi Podio.
The XLII group was also certainly there. It used vehicles from the 17th/10th group to reach Sidi Barrani.
It was also under the command, at least temporarily, of a raggruppamento led by the 12th "Sila" Artillery Regiment's commander, Grande.

One 47/32 group each from the Bologna, Pavia, and Sabratha Divisions were transferred to the North African high command.
I was wrong about the 69th and 70th 65/17 groups. They were given to the 10th Army, though maybe they made it back.
The Savona Division's infantry gun batteries also later reappear despite having been transferred, so maybe they also made it back or were reformed with fresh guns (or former GaF guns).

GaF artillery commander identities have been found (mostly Lt-Col.s):
31st GaFAR - Guido Baggini
32nd GaFAR - Gaetano Mesiano
(There's also data in this book for the rest of the GaFARs, but I'm not going to transcribe them here as they are irrelevant.)

The commander of the 17th Battery of the VI Libyan Artillery Group (from the 1st Libyan Division) was an Amadio. The battery was destroyed in June just as the war began.
The VI Libyan Group's commander was a Paolo Sabbataini. He did not survive the attack on the Tummars and his command was wiped out.
And the commander of the 18th Battery was a Captain Fascietti. The book has a pretty expansive section about the last stand of these men and their units.
The 2nd Libyan Division had the I and II groups, while the 1st had the VI and VII groups, and anti-aircraft (20/65) batteries with the same numbers for each group.

Other than its 100/17 and 105/28 groups, the 22nd Corps Artillery Raggruppamento had the following 20/65 batteries:
13th and 14th, organic. 10th and 11th, from the 2nd CC.NN. Division. 27th, from the Brescia Division.
The 22nd CAR also had only 22 guns in its 2 (XLII, XLIII) groups of 105/28, rather than the normal 24.

Funny story, I got trolled by google translate because the Italian "salmerie", which means the pack train or vehicles, was translated as "charcuterie".

Changes to make:
Commanders for the 31st and 32nd GaF Artillery Raggruppamenti

Another theory that I've come to believe. I believe Montanari screwed up when he transcribed Rostagno's OOB, specifically dropping just a single line.
There are two instances of the "XXXI settore di copertura (meno 2 capisaldi)" line. There's no reason for this, in fact one of them is just out of place. This second one must be a mistake.
This second line is, in fact, in the exact spot where detail about the makeup of the XXXI/6 group would be mentioned. And that's the exact one with two 120/25 batteries.
While obviously I can't know the battery numbers (despite my best efforts so far), I realize now that this solution really is a highly likely one that I'd just previously discounted.
This is what he said if you don't apply my changes, and it makes no sense. You can see how the 2nd mention of the 31st Coverage Sector could be a typo:

31st Coverage Sector (Minus 2 Strongpoints)
Elements of the 32nd Coverage Sector
-242nd Battery / XXXI/5 (4 120/25)
31st Coverage Sector (Minus 2 Strongpoints)
-244th and 245th Batteries / XXXI/7 (4 149/35 Each)
-249th Battery / XXXII/1 (4 75/06) (75/27 Mod. 06)
-246th Battery / XXXII/2 (4 77/28)
-247th Battery / XXXII/3 (4 77/28)
(More elements)

I looked but I don't think Rostagno's document is anywhere. Only in the archives in Rome can they be found, and I doubt anybody has that around. I searched all over the internet but found nothing.
With that, there's no way to find if this is his mistake or if it is Montanari's. It's a very strange mistake too, so I really cannot understand how this could've possibly happened.

Given the 243rd Battery was part of XXXI/5 and never appears, the best solution is to finally accept that the 230th and 231st bis batteries were there.
Maybe they had been renamed, but there's nothing I can do about that. No sources say anything about the GaF bis batteries, but at least until 1943 this designation existed, so I see no reason to think this happened.
In fact, even by early 1942 some bis units still appeared, though not in large numbers. The designation was certainly still in use, however, and I don't think any mass renaming occurred.
After so long... this might be it.

There is no updated version of "The First Offensive". I painstakingly went back to the Issuu version but it seems to be entirely identical.
I also looked through "La Guardia alla Frontiera" but didn't find much.

The 251st and 254th GaF batteries weren't at Tobruk but appear to have been part of the defense of either Derna or Benghazi since they reappaer at Berta with the Sabratha Division.
Actually there were a whole bunch of these, lol.

Part of the Sirte Division's transport to from Tripoli to Benghazi took place aboard the transport "Calitea" in mid-July 1940.
Tomasso Mosetti, a prisoner from the Sirte Division, was a member of the 2nd Battery, 1st Group, 22nd Corps Artillery Raggruppamento (100/17).

I found a collection of postcards from the Sirte Division's prisoners of war. Given most of the division was captured these should reflect the makeup of the division. Let's see what we get:
I later also found some mail that was sent by families of the prisoners to the office of the Vatican, which gives similarly fragmentary information.
