The one thing I gain from this is to know that the 70th Infantry Regiment had two of its rifle companies, the 6th and 7th (haha). I don't know where its 5th is but there's now no reason to believe it was elsewhere.

It's not exact, but it's noted that the 140th Blackshirt Battalion had "2 rifle companies". The 4th company was a support weapons company, so this implies (which I agree with) that the 2 companies were the 1st and 2nd.

The 2/1st Anti-Tank regiment did not participate in Operation Crusader as it had only just arrived. That's a mistake on my part to assume it was in action.

Other than the 55th Artillery Regiment, the Brescia Division also gave up its 2 infantry gun companies and its anti-tank company to the 10th Army, all of which would be slowly replaced.
The new anti-tank companies (yes, there were multiple now) were numbered the 5th, 71st, and 227th, all with 47/32 guns.
The infantry gun companies were also replaced, respectively, with the 34th "Livorno" and 38th "Ravenna", both of 65/17 (as normal).
The 55th Artillery Regiment in Rostagno's report has at least one 65/17 company, but of unknown number. If I find more about this in the future I could assign that company a number.
If so, however, it would have escaped destruction at Sidi Barrani and Bardia under either the 1st Libyan or the 62nd "Marmarica" Divisions, which seems like a tall order but not an impossible task.

The Savona Division also had just a single infantry gun company that was subordinated to the 16th Infantry Regiment. I couldn't find anything about its number.

On reformation with the 283rd and 284th groups, the Artillery Raggruppamento "Sabratha" had just 5 batteries, with 3 and 2 respectively in the groups.
All were of 75/27. There were some spare anti-tank, anti-air, and infantry guns but no more regular field guns or howitzers. But it's fair for a division with just 4 infantry battalions (Regio Esercito describes them as companies but that's unlikely).
By May 1942, though, the division might have returned mostly to normalcy. Its artillery regiment had once again a full headquarters and number as the 42nd, and there were now three groups with the new 3rd having 100/17 guns.

On the 17th of August, 1939, so quite far off, the 31st Mixed Engineer Detachment (part of the GaF, and subordinated to the 31st Coverage Sector) had a strength of 4 officers, 14 NCOs, and 97 enlisted men. (115 total)
These groups do see to vary in size. The one for Tripoli had a total of 177 men, while the one for Nalut (the middle of nowhere) had just 61. Overall, though, they all seem to be at least on part with company size.
Honestly, 115 men doesn't even seem like a company after later thought. It might make sense to call it a platoon, in fact, since it doens't seem to have operated independently if at all.
Actually, no, this was in fact company size. At times even I can completely and utterfly forget the basics and it makes me look stupid. Sorry about that, everyone!

I never realized for while that the numbers given to "points" by the Germans and British are not arbitrary. They simply refer to the altitude of the position, which simplifies decisions for commanders.
The Italians do similar, although they use the term "quota", abbreviated "q.", but it means the same thing and is used for the exact same purpose.

Other than providing unit histories, though, this source is quite terrible. It doesn't have much from the British point of view and engages in quite a bit of glorification.
While bringing nothing new to the Tobruk table, it repeats the insane claim that the British had five divisions and the Free French a brigade in the attack force.
The accompanying map is also entirely irrelevant as it's Montanari's map from almost a year later during Operation Crusader. Safe to say it's not very helpful.

Other than the 52nd Medium Tank Battalion of RECAM, there was also 51st Medium Tank Battalion that was part of the Littorio Division and was commanded by Salvatore Zappalà.
This division was highly incomplete upon it joining the battle of June 19, 1942. Many of its elements had been taken to replenish the Ariete Division, so it would be missing many elements until they were rebuilt by Second El Alamein.
Those units that were left were the 12th Bersaglieri Regiment, the 51st Medium Tank Battalion, and a single battery of 75/27 guns. The 12th Bersaglieri were also down their mixed support battalion.
Some elements had also been lost on transport or had failed to be transported in the first place, further weakening what was left of this division, down to barely brigade strength.
Thus, it's order of battle was this sorry jumble of incomplete units:
133rd "Littorio" Armored Division (Gervasio Bitossi)
    12th Bersaglieri Regiment
        23rd Motorized Bersaglieri Battalion
        36th Motorized Bersaglieri Battalion (1/3 Strength in 1 Company)
        12th Bersaglieri Motorcycle Company
    51st Medium Tank Battalion (2 Companies)
    1 Battery / 321st Artillery Group (4 75/27)
Total: 7 Companies. That's about the same strength as the Division z.b.V. "Afrika" was on deployment.
A 12th Medium Tank Battalion had also been arrived and unlike the 10th and 11th, weren't robbed by the Ariete and Trieste Divisions, but had its own deficiency: all its tanks had been lost en route.

The 4th Tank Infantry Regiment was outlasted by its child formation, as it was responsible for the formation of the headquarters of the 132nd Tank Infantry Regiment.
It did get reconstituted and again meet its end in Operation Achse, but until then it functioned only as training formation and did not see any combat of real proportions.
The 1st Medium Tank Battalion had gotten into an engagement of November 19, 1940, where it saved some motorized men from ambush at huge losses to itself. I now see why many of the its tanks were in a state of utter disrepair, with only 7 operational.
While the tanks that were mobile, all 7 of them, were organized into a company, the 3 tanks that were semi-mobile were only enough to form a platoon. So at least there was a realistic appreciation of their strengths.
Remaining elements of the 4th Tank Infantry Regiment that were to begin rebuilding it were lost on the troopship "Conte Rosso" (Red Count) that was sunk on May 24, 1941.

The third Tobruk line, or green line, according to the British, was the one built around the old forts of Perrone, Airente, Solaro, and Marcucci.
Neither the Italians nor the British were very successful with defending this line, and the forts were barely positions in the first place. The whole third line was honestly pretty useless.
The organization of the second, or blue, line also varies somewhat between the Italian and British plans. The British/Australians probably realized most of the old strongpoints weren't very useful and didn't defend them.

I recently did a small, shallow bit of research on the operations around Zara during World War II, which didn't amount to much. Both sides planned for operations but not much of scale.
The Yugoslav 12th "Jadranska" Infantry Division was scheduled to attack from its base at Knin (yes, that Knin) but failed to do anything. This allowed the Italians to advance from Zara and link up with others elements coming from Slovenia.
That was it. The rest of the Yugoslav Army had completed its disintegration by then and the 9000 men of the Zara garrison commanded by BG Emilio Gilioli achieved a complete success.

A while ago, I discovered an order of battle for the "di Bardi" task force that has no context behind it in the very unreliable Nafziger Collection. Let's try to make sense of it.
The unit is titled "Command Piazza di Bardi". This is very likely a mistranslation, as all my Italian sources call this organization the "Piazza di Bardia".
It's probably the predecessor to Schmitt's "Division Bardia", and in fact Montanari's "The Second British Offensive" indicates that Schmitt was in command of something in the area.
I think it's fair to put him in command, making this a German command with no German units. Moving on to the rest of the Nafziger document, let's look at the order of battle for this "task force" (it wasn't one).

Task Force HQ (probably not real)
    475th (German) Communications Platoon (probaby real but I couldn't find it, it's too small)

Piazza di Bardia Command:
    2nd Battalion, 15th "Savona" Infantry Regiment (4)
    2nd Battalion, 16th "Savona" Infantry Regiment (4)
    3rd Battalion, 40th "Bologna" Infantry Regiment (4)
        9th 20/35 AA Company
        10th 47/32 AT Company
        11th 81/14 Mortar Company
        12th Machine Gun Company
    4th Machine Gun Squadrons Group, "Genova Cavalleria" Regiment (1 Company Strength)
    HQ and 3 Mixed Companies from the 28th G.a.F. Coverage Sector (I finally know what this means)
    282nd Group, 280th G.a.F. Artillery Regiment (75/27)
    342nd Group, 340th G.a.F. Artillery Regiment (75/27)

General Staff (Savona Division HQ)

One benefit of this document, though, is that it has a clear order for one of the elusive AA/AT battalions at this time. Somewhat helpful, although way too late.
In a stroke of insane luck, I already got it right. It's just that my numbers are all one off, since they don't account for the AA company which my game often ignores out of convenience.

Also, I was wrong about the strength of the 3rd Battalion, 347th Infantry Regiment. A standard German rifle company in 1941 had 12 LMGs, 3 light mortars, and 3 anti-tank rifles.
A smaller variant of this rifle company, the type e, had 9 less men and just a single mortar with no anti-tank rifles.
The standard machine gun company had 12 HMGs and 6 mortars, with a variant, the type f, having the full 12 HMGs but no mortars, though it did save 52 men worth of manpower.
Depending on whether it used normal type c of everything or if it was using the variant types with weaker armament, here are the ranges for firepower of a single infantry battalion:
LMGs: 36
HMGs: 12
Mortars: 3-15
Anti-Tank Rifles: 0-9
The 3rd Battalion, 347th Infantry Regiment had the following strength:
LMGs: 45 (Some Captured)
HMGs: 12
Mortars: 12
Anti-Tank Rifles: 18
So, it's strenght was around normal or even above standard in all respects. It perhaps could be given a sort of "buffed" status as early as Crusader, but at the same time its parent Division was lacking in support.
For all the company-level and battalion-level firepower they could leverage, communications were poor, the engineers were Italians subbing in for the 2nd Company/39th Engineer Battalion which was in Bardia, and there wasn't any artillery.
As a siege formation working in tandem with Italian units and corps-level forces (not represented in my game), it's not bad, but were it and the rest of its division to fight a battle on their own it would be completely over.

I started doing this at some point and it's really confusing. Ad'A is an abbreviation for "Armi d'Accompagnamento" or support/heavy weapons.

We've tracked artillery, we've tracked mortars, we've tracked personnel. I wanted to track machine guns next, but I'm sure the splitting up of the 61st Machine Gun Battalion will greatly complicate things.
Expected Count:
48 HMGs from the 2 infantry regiments (8 for each battalion's Ad'A company)
40 HMGs from the artillery regiment (2 per battery) (I don't know how this works for the corps units, but I'll ignore these anyway)
36 HMGs from the MG battalion
10 HMGs from the 2 bersaglieri motorcycle companies
18 HMGs from the 2 replacement battalions
Infantry-adjacent total: 102
Total: 152
The specific model of HMG is the Fiat-Revelli Model.35, or just the Fiat 35
Other smaller machine guns varied in caliber and build, and the calibers that Montanari gives don't match up with models I could find, so we won't worry about them

Anyway, here's the counts Montanari gives:
18 reinforcing the MG battalion (the MG battalion itself had 2 companies) (probably from 2 of the Ad'A companies, perhaps 4/70 and 12/70)
Ras Beiad: 8 (probably from an Ad'A company, likely 8/69)
Actually, this is probably the Ad'A company from the 64th Replacement Battalion
3rd Battalion, 69th Infantry Regiment: 8 (probably from a spare Ad'A company or its organic company, i'd prefer 12/69)
Pilastrino: 8 (same, likely from 8/70)
Actually, these were probably from 12/70, since that's... exactly what Montanari says in his book. He doesn't mince any words about it.
Gabr el Abd: 8 (same, likely from 1/61MG)
Actually, this is probably the Ad'A company from the 61st Replacement Battalion
Point. 71: 8 (same, likely from 1/61MG)
(Not much option left, so I'm giving these to 8/69)
Mobile Reserve: 2 platoons = 8 (same, likely from 4/69)
We have 5 of what are almost certianly Ad'A companies. So that tells us where most of them went. The rest is a little wishy-washy but the numbers don't seem completely off.
The missing machine guns are probably where Montanari puts many of them - inside the defense pits. I can't come up with any numbers, so here the train of thought ends.
Those are probably all from the MG battalion, though, so those could probably be subtracted from the calculations.
What I can say confidently, though, is that while the Ad'A companies of many of these infantry battalions are not placed anywhere on Montanari's map, I am nearly certain that they were still within the Tobruk Fortress.

Niehorster's site lacks an OOB for the 1941 AS Autotransportable Division, a mostly-transient structure that was rendered impossible by the severity of losses during Crusader.

Divisional HQ, divisional services, and motorized transport

    2 Infantry Battalions, each:
        3 Rifle Companies

German reconaissance units did come to the support of the Savona Division's HQ at Bir Ghirba, according to Montanari.
For me, that gives another excuse to keep the 580th Reconaissance Company near the frontier even though it wasn't there in reality.

Also, Montanari's book on Crusader gives some orders of battle that shine light on the mixed and severely cursed state of many axis units in the preparatory stages of the battle.
In fact, his orders of battle are the origin for Nafziger's "di Bardi" task force. What a mess. An order of battle for this German unit became corrupted to a nearly unrecognizable name.
And yes, there are still no German units in the division except for the communications platoon. But Montanari does add something we didn't have before: information about Bardia's defense.
Regia Marina Garrison: 1 120/45 battery, 1 76/40 battery, 2 13mm machine guns
Regio Esercito Garrison: 1 105/28 battery, a single 75/27 gun with a G.a.F crew
3 mixed detachments with assorted recovered and captured weapons, and the infantry of the 3 battalions present and the Genova Cavalleria squadrons group

It's worth noting that Montanari refers to the heavy companies of German units as "compagnia d'arresto" which may be relatted to the "compagnia combattimenti d'arresto" we've seen previously.
The rest of what he has aren't groundbreaking. Here are the takeaways:
The Brescia Division had the normal 2 infantry gun companies, and as previously known they had been taken from 2 divisions in metropolitan Italy. It also had a cadre of a divisional motorcycle company (not bersaglieri).
Funny that the Italian word for radio operators is "marconisti". They truly are proud of their (fascist) countryman.
155th Artillery Regiment had two normal 105mm howitzer groups and 1 mixed group with 1 100mm gun battery and 2 105mm howitzer batteries.
Meanwhile, the 33rd Artillery Regiment was entirely normal with 2 105mm howitzer groups and a 155mm howitzer group (abteilungen in German).
On November 1, the 361st Infantry Regiment wasn't assigned to the Divison z.b.V. "Afrika". That, or Montanari just missed it.
The Trieste Division's formation on November 1 shows only the 65th and not the 66th Infantry Regiment with a 3rd support weapons battalion.
Also, its Bersaglieri battalions (all 3 non-support ones), formerly with just 2 motorized companies or 3 motorcycle companies, had begun receiving or reorganizing into a 3rd support weapons company.
The Bersaglieri support weapons battalion also had the exact same structure as those of the regular infantry regiments, with four companies.

There are no more axis orders of battle other than this one, though there are some mentions of British dispositions in the documents attached to the book, and a full OOB for them later.

So... I might have screwed up ever single replacement unit. I still have to this date not found any proof that any of them had four companies. Here's what I have:
The 55th Replacement Battalion had 2 companies, but both were in Naples anyway.
A random Alpini Replacement Battalion, the 7th, had 3 companies with high ordinal numbers.
An Alpini Replacement Regiment during the campaign in the Soviet Union had an unknown number of companies, but did have a support weapons company.
Some replacement unit that I can't identify had 4 total companies, three rifle and one support weapons, just like a regular infantry battalion.
It seems to me that when in doubt, I should go with the replacement battalion having the same strength as a regular battalion of the same type, so 4 companies for infantry and 3 for alpini (except +1 for the ARMIR).
The Savona's case is probably an anomaly and that order of battle is on the suspicious side anyway. Perhaps the replacement battalion, as it wasn't overseas yet, wasn't yet fully established.

Some say replacement battalions were meant to handle replacement companies. They themselves never replaced anything and were chiefly administrative, and were also entirely permanent and stood in for depots (which were not overseas).
In contrast, march companies were formed at the replacement battalion from personnel sent from Italy. There, they would be trained and equipped in preparation to join the unit they were replacing.

The Savona Division appears to have a single infantry gun company with 5 guns during Crusader, at least according to a German Source. Other places indicate that its artillery regiment had two batteries of 65/17 after rebuilding.
I believe at least one of the batteries was numbered the 267th, which certainly was a replacement unit and probably hadn't come from any existing infantry regiment.
Apparently, some sources indicate a 266th battery as well, which would complete the set. I can't find any confirmation of its existence but I think it's not unreasonable to assume it did.

Previously, I had thought the 2nd Battalion, Bedfordshire and Hertfordshire Regiment was commanded by a Lt-Col. Eason. I had no proof of this but I had seen his name and rank somewhere and that was the best I could find.
I'd like to complian briefly that this was an awful experience to research. While it was a few months, British sources suck and it doesn't help that there's a similarly named Hertfordshire Regiment elsewhere.
The good news is that Rommel's Riposte has solved this problem by confirming the commanding officer was a Lt-Col. Hassell. Maybe this was the guy who later commanded 69th Infantry Brigade later, though his name is spelled different.
Also, this unit is not in the Crusader game, but the commander of the 3rd County of London Yeomanry was a Major Dickens. Major's a low rank, so maybe he only took command after the previous man became a casualty.

From tacticalorganization.com, there are a ton of documents that have so far seemed on the reliable side. These have now been reached on the reading list, so let's get to them. Things of note:
Off-topic, but between June 10 and July 1940, the East African Southern Zone formed a series of colonial divisions to handle its numerous independent brigades, numbered the 21st-24th.
The Eritrea HQ later did the same, forming the 1st, 2nd, and 4th Colonial Divisions, as well as some additional brigades from reserve elements.

The XXXI Libyan Coastal Battalion was formed by the mobilization center at Derna, the same as the XI Libyan Battalion, 7th AA Battery, 14th and 15th Static Machine Gun Companies, and 22nd/7th Artillery Battery.
While most of the Libyan Battalions had nicknames, mostly after places, I couldn't find any such name for the XXXI or the other coastal battalions. They are truly obscure.
Finally news about this unit's structure though: it had a 44 man hq with no weapons, and 3 companies of 151 men each, containing two rifle platoons and a machine gun platoon.
So I was at least right about the 3-company structure. But with a total strength of just 497 men, these were much, much weaker than a regular divisional battalion of 662 men.
But at least it's stronger than some blackshirt units, with their support weapons battalions sometimes having as few as 313 personnel.
Also, the 32nd-35th Libyan Battalions were all formed initially as coastal untis as well, probably, but were then reorganized into regular field battalions.

That said, while the Libyan Coastal Battalions were stronger than the blackshirt support battalions, they were weaker than blackshirt garrison battalions of 517 men (also with no weapons).
A slightly more mobile version of those garrison battalions with just 499 men but at least they had a few heavier machine guns (6) plus some SMGs.

About the Libyan Machine Gun companies, 19 existed. 13 were placed on the Tunisian border, while 14, 15, 18, and 19 were sent to Giarabub, and 16 was deployed to Kufra. 17's doesn't seem to have existed, for some reason.
There were also Static Libyan Machine Gun companies, 13 numbered 50-62. Most survived into 1942, except for 59 and 69 which were lost at Kufra. They had no fixed organization.
The "Croatian Legion" that appears in some returns for the Italian 8th Army was not the same one that went to Stalingrad. Instead it was a militia force formed by the Italians.

The "depot" legions such as the 140th Salerno one were called that because their active battalions were elsewhere (in this case North Africa), hence they were to act as depots and not as combat HQs.
So, a single field legion such as the 238th "Aurelio Padovani" with three battalions would have three depot legions back in Italy supporting it.
The "Assault" legions were the ones that were given to the army divisions, a change that occurred into October 1940, though it resulted in no actual combat difference.
The Roman size designations such as "Centuria" and "Coorte" were discontinued for most of the blackshirt field units, except for a minorly cursed Raggruppamento formed from the forestry militia.

The Bersaglieri Motorized Battalions with just 2 companies apiece had a rather small strength of just 463 men but made it for it in eliteness.
The motorcycle battalions were stronger with 731 men, while I have nothing on the strength of individual companies or the ones given to the Type AS Divisions.
The AS 1941 Bersaglieri organization that appeared during Crusader had 2 motorized battalions with 3 companies each (2 rifle and one heavy) along with a support weapons battalion (missing its anti-tank company) and motorcycle company.

While my mixed bersaglieri battalion was a bit of a cursed unit, a similar unit did exist. As part of the Babini Group, a motorcycle bersaglieri battalion was formed from the 10th and 60th companies.
It didn't have a number, as an ad-hoc headquarters, and was instead termed "provisional". Its organization is also in doubt as some say the 201st and 202nd companeis were with it as well.
A third motorcycle company, the 17th, was part of the Babini Group but was put under the command of the tanks rather than the bersaglieri headquarters. Neither was part of the 10th Bersaglieri Regiment (which hadn't shown up yet).
The 4th Tank Infantry Regiment headquarters had served as the basis for the Babini Group's 1st Tank Battle Group (probably termed a Raggruppamento) when it went to its destruction with its 1st Battalion.
And yes, the 20/65 battery that I KNOW THE REGIMENT HAD is listed but predictably without a number. It might be the 13th from the Savona Division that disappears but that disappearance may be a typo.
It's like this is some kind of intentional coverup operation. For none of these regiments with a 20/65 battery can I find the number. It's actually immensely miserable.

I genuinely don't think this could possibly be the case. There's no way a single number could be collectively forgotten by every source I have. The only answer... was that there was no number.
In fact, Leland Ness includes an OOB for the Babini Group with every single company-sized or larger unit numbered, except this one 20/65 battery. If it did have a number this would be a real travesty.

The Carabinieri Parachute Battalion was the third such battalion formed, as only after the two Libyan Battalions and it were formed were any metropolitan battalions formed.
This battalion was destroyed in its rearguard role in the days following Crusader, but the pieces of it that survived were able to rejoin the Folgore Division as a single company.

It's strange that Montanari at one point mentions that the 100/28 guns were "the best". On paper, in terms of range and firepower, maybe.
tacticalorganization.com's Leland Ness disagrees, arguing that its outdated design and poor mobility and versatility made it near use-less and necessary to replace with the more modern 105/40.
The sometimes-menationed 105/32 is not a mistake, but a rare redesigned Austro-Hungarian 104/32 field gun. I feel as if I've seen this gun somewhere but I don't remember where.
The 149/12 was also distinct from the 149/13 and was a German gun licensed by the Italians. It had absolutely abysmal range, worse than even the 149/13, so it's not surprising that the only one at Tobruk was for training.
The two models of CAR for North Africa were as follows:
Type 1 (2 100/17, 2 75/27): 20th CAR, 21st CAR
Type 2 (2 105/28, 1 100/17): 10th CAR, 22nd CAR
(These numbers weren't the ones used, I am just arbitrarily numbering them. More variants from Types A-G were used for metropolitan CARs).
The Type 1 was the original North African design that were formed prior to the war, while the Type 2 ones were later reinforcements sent directly from Italy.

A typical corps artillery group in europe had 546 personnel, while even with its support elements, those in Africa had a total of just 426 men handling its 12 guns.
Despite a pretty unimpressive record as commander of the Sabratha Division, though that wasn't completely his fault, Guido Della Bona survived to command XXVI Army Corps occupying Epirus.

I've also finally received information about the "reparto specialisti di artiglieria di corpo d'armata", or artillery specialist units. These were observation and target acquisition elements attached to each CAR (depot).
The strength of such a unit was 254 men, who served in, you guessed it, a series of specialized roles such topographics, sound ranging, or even meteorology.

Compared to the abysmal quality of most Italian artillery units, the 20/65 and 75/46, the anti-air guns at Tobruk, were both 30s designs that were high quality but couldn't be produced in quantity in time.
The XV Group of 75/46 which would be destroyed at Tobruk was in fact not originally deployed there but was instead shipped to Derna. That does put into doubt that it is the same one later mentioned at Cyrene, however.

Overall, it's a truly impressive amount of stuff that Ness has been able to collect. You can tell he actually went to the archives in Rome and NARA, which certainly means I won't need to do the same.
Still nothing on the replacement units, though. I'll have to read through his information on the infantry again, spend some more hours digging, and probably end up making my own judgement anyway.

The 3rd Battalion of the 20th "Brescia" Infantry Regiment was commanded during Crusader by a Major Bocchino and managed to survive some rearguard actions.
Knight of the 1st Battalion, Worcestershire Regiment had the full name "John Oldham Knight". His second in command was a Major Robert Tuckey.

More information on the deployment of the Savona Division. From west to east, its elements were as follows:
Cirener: 1/15
D'Avanzo: ?
Cova: 3/15, 13 Oasis, 257th GaF 65/17
Frongia (Omar Nuovo): 3/16, HQ + 2 MG Companies, 2 75/27 Batteries, 503rd 20/65 Battery, 1/18 FLAK
Sidi (Libyan) Omar: HQ 16 IR, 1/16, 3 MG Companies, 2 105/28 Batteries, 267th 65/17 Company, 12 Oasis
The 266th 65/17 Company was probably at Bardia, if at existed at all.
The cause of much confusion is that Sidi Omar on the Libyan side is called Libyan Omar by the British and Sidi Omar by the Axis.
Meanwhile, Sidi Omar on the Egyptian side is called Sidi Omar by the British and Omar Nuovo by the Italians. There were only two such Omars, not three like the names might suggest.
Faltembacher (subordinated to Schmitt): 6 Oasis

I've also found an older series of conversations from Comando Supremo. For some reason it seems the site was wiped and restarted later, so a lot was lost but is now archived in the waybackmachine.

The 10th AAR (not CAR) appears to have been originally deployed in France and was disbanded after the French campaign, reappearing in Tobruk on July 1940.

The anti-air battery of the artillery regiments often appears numbered in line with the regiment's batteries rather than in total, like using 10th/43rd for the Sirte Division's rather than 61st.
This took effect only in North Africa, however. Divisions that were used in Europe did not have this, and the North African divisions also began life without this change yet having been made.
By July, though, the battery appears to have been given away and replaced with the 77th battery, from the Pavia Division.
This is a bit of a shady thing, since no sources clearly indicate the number of this battery. My only source for this is page 219 of "History of the Artillery", by Carlo Montù.
Prior to becoming the 136th Regiment, the Giovani Fascisti seem to have been organized only as a Raggruppamento Battaglioni "Giovani Fascisti", so I severely backdated that one. My mistake.
The 9th Independent Infantry Battalion might have actually been a former G.a.F. unit and was under the command of a Major Gelli who would be killed in combat during the Tunisian Campaign.
It appears the number change that resulted in all G.a.F. artillery groups getting multiplied by 10 occurred in Spring 1941. Units lost during Compass were unaffected.

One source about 3rd Battalion, 255th Infantry Regiment and 3rd Battalion, 347th Infantry Regiment notes they were full of "older men and young recruits".
This is probably just referring to the fact that the divisions these battalions and regiments came from were formed in later deployment waves and thus used lower quality manpower, not an intentional weakening of these two battalions.
Prior to joining the 361st "Afrika" Infantry Regiment, the 361st "Afrika" Artillery Battalion had been the 1st Battalion, 190th Motorized Artillery Regiment.
The two heavy infantry gun companies, the 707th and 708th, landed in early 1942, the 708th in February and the 707th in April, and each was equipped with 6 self-propelled guns.

About the battle that resulted in Mickl's capture, he was enclosed in a pocket along with other elements of Kampfgruppe Böttcher, including the 361st "Afrika" Infantry Regiment.
Nearby, Belhamed was held by the 900th Pioneer Battalion and elements of the 9th Bersaglieri Regiment. 4th New Zealand Brigade with its 18th and 20th Battalions cleared that feature on the 25th-26th of November.
On the 28th, Mickl's pocket was finally overrun by the 44th Royal Tank Regiment of 1st Army Tank Brigade, resulting in the capture of 637 unwounded men including Mickl, most of whom later broke free with him.

There is a discussion on the Replacement Battalions, although even the experts and buffs there are really struggling to find much of value. But there's not entirely nothing to say about this.
Noted are replacement battalions for ever division in North Africa, and it seems that this was standard procedure. A special fortress replacement battalion also existed in Tripoli in 1941.
I used the Issuu reload tactic to read the organization tables of each division type from the book about the Italian army between the two world wars, but it didn't include the number of companies in each battalion.

I wanted a video by Kings and Generals about the Italian Invasion of Egypt. This video was pretty terrible, failing to understand which Italian weapons were outdated and which were honestly pretty good.
They also made some other mistakes about the status of units and their movements:
8:24 The 11th Hussars were a battalion-sized unit. The British army still used old regiment designations for some of its battalions. Same for the 7th Hussars at 8:37.
12:43 The Italian "motor-transportable" divisions like the 60th, 61st, 62nd, 63rd, and 64th were not actually motorized. The artillery and other heavy weapons were motorized, but most of the regular troops were on foot.
18:22 It's true that the Italian command had made significant mistakes, but encircling a much faster enemy in the desert was functionally impossible for an army that was almost entirely on foot. 

I'm gonna be honest, I think Regio Esercito's source book that claims the existence of 310th and 320th Artillery Regiments with new structures and groupings are just straight up wrong.
No proof exists that the reorganizations that would have resulted in these units existing occurred prior to their destruction in January, and if they were ordered to reorganize there was absolutely no chance that it was carried out.

Bonus information regarding the Romolo Gessi Battalion: it wasn't actually disbanded, just removed from the front. Rather than becoming a company, it split into 2 companies with one taking a different name.
Both were employed in rear area guard duties in Africa at least until 1943, with records showing that it in fact was reinforced rather than being consumed for reinforcements to Rommel.

I've found some more information about the labor battalions. Certainly the 8th, 9th, and 4th existed in various positions at times.
These battalions were army assets and were simply formed from random manpower. It had no combat ability and the personnel were considered unfit for service on the frontline.
Two raggruppamenti (named as if they contained copmanies, for some reason) were formed from these labor batatlions: the 1st with the 5th-7th battalions and the 2nd with 1st-4th battalions.
The 8th of Tobruk fame arrived in May 1940 along with the 9th. Nothing else is known of the things of value it did other than maybe some ineffective maintanance on the Tobruk defenses.

It's possible to find a mention to GaF replacement elements, which were roughly the size of a weak company or 80 men
One has been located for the X Coverage Sector and I think it makes sense for similar sectors to have their own. But this doesn't tell us much about army replacements or the GaF in Africa.

It seems that while machine gun companies often had irregular number of companies, the 4 numbered from the 60th-63rd fro the original XX and XXI Corps all had 3 companies in their structure.

I'm going to attempt to ascertain the personnel of the replacement battalion in the AS 1940 Type by just counting all of them. Here's the attempt:

From Montanari:
Total 10978 Men

From Ness:
Infantry Regiment:
    HQ: 134 Men
    Infantry Battalion:
        HQ: 115 Men
        Rifle Company: 156 Men
        Heavy Company: 181 Men
        Total: 764 Men
    Infantry Gun Battery: 113 Men
    Mortar Company: 199 Men
    Total: 2738 Men
x 2 = 5476 Men
Artillery Regiment:
    HQ: 113 Men
    Artillery Group:
        HQ: 84 Men
        Battery: 87 Men
        Total: 345 Men
    Anti-Aircraft Battery: 100 Men
    Service Battery: 55 Men
    Total: 1403 Men
Machine Gun Battalion:
    HQ: 58 Men
    MG Company: 200 Men
    Total: 658 Men
Anti-Tank Company: 134 Men
Total Listed: 7671 Men

Approximate:
Division HQ: 358 Men
Light Tank Battalion: 190 Men
Motorcycle Bersaglieri Company: 100 Men (Leser gives 133 Men)
Engineer Battalion: 527 Men
Services: 235 Men
Total Approximate: 1410 Men (1443 Men)

Total Exclusion: 9081 Men (9114 Men)

Left:
Replacement Battalion: 1897 Men?
That's much too high. Certainly many of the assorted units would've been expanded significantly between the 1939 and 1940 TOEs, but there's no clear information.
Let's try this again, starting with the AS 1941 design rather than the AS 1939 design that might have diverged more. Not going to do the math here, but the sum is:
Rest of the Division: 10208 Men
Infantry Battalion: 886 Men
Replacement Battalion: 770 Men
With this information, I think it's fair to say that the replacement battalion had a fairly standard 4-company structure, not one of the much weaker 3-company battalions which fielded 500-600 men apiece.
Also, 770 men is barely different from the infantry battalion standard of 764 men. It's safe to say that the difference is within the error bars and is negligible.
For that reason, I think I'm going to assume from now on that the structure of the replacement battalion was identical or nearly identical to that of a regular infantry battalion.

Also, if we assume that the rifle company strength was 156 men like that of a regular infantry battalion, we realize that when Montanari noted that 160 men were avilable to defend the port, I misinterpreted his statement.
Those 160 men were a company-strength force, but with 156 men in the 3rd Company, 62nd Replacement Battalion, the only reasonable solution is that the replacement were not counted in the 160.
Thus, the 160 men are exclusively from the 3 Carabinieri sections (~65 men each) and the two depots (116th Infantry Regiment, G.a.F. Tobruk Unified).
On the other hand, there's no reason to 100% believe that the Carabinieri sections were at full strength and entirely committed. The replacement company may also have been weak.
So there's still a way for the total to add up to 160 men even if both the replacements and the carabinieri are there, plus a couple extra loose men from the depots who likely contributed little.

The Sirte Division did NOT have the 61st Engineer (artieri) Company as this was part of the 24th "Pinerolo" Infantry Division in Greece. The actual number is unknown.
Below, I'll start tracking some of the engineer companies and see if I can find a gap that will represent the location of the companies of the North African divisions (60th-63rd).

Some more information on tracking which 2 120/25 batteries formed the 6th Group that Rostagno missed. I'm still leaning on 230bis and 231bis, but it's not certain.
I thought that maybe the solution would be to fill in the gaps in the existing numbers of 242, 244, 245, 246, 247, and 249, leaving 243 and 248.
However, a 248th Battery appears in Albania on 6 April, and then in Montenegro, and I don't think it would've been formed so quickly (just 2 months after Tobruk fell to form it).
The original 248th Battery is also a 77/28 battery and this timeline requires it to be retooled into a 120/25 battery, destroyed, and then reformed as a 149/12 battery in Albania.
Alternately, if the battery was disbanded sometime in late 1940, it would have several months to be reformed into a 149/12 battery and the personnel would be available for this change.
But maybe this matters not, as the 242nd and 243rd Batteries appear in the alps and then in Africa within 7 days of each other (June 1, 1940 - June 8, 1940), which must be impossible.
I can only guess that this means the transfer of batteries could in fact be very quick, or that the numbers in these two theaters had no need to be distinct from one another.

And like an idiot I forgot to just go back to the base source about all this. Montanari doesn't actually leave much ambiguity with the structures of these two battalions.
The LXI Replacement Battalion is noted as having 3 rifle companies and 1 support weapons company, split up between Point. 71 (2 rifle) and the strongpoint of Gabr el Abd (1 rifle, support weapons).
The LXIV Replacement Battalion is noted as having 2 rifle companies, with some additional elements (likely its other 2 companies) part of the coastal defense command.

Other than its two battalions, each formed from 3 rifle companies, the Giovani Fascisti battalion regrouping also had a machine gun company and platoons of infantry guns and mortars.
This machine gun company, however, appears to have been disbanded with the wider AS 42 decentralizations of weapons and was gone by the 24th of May 1942, in time for Gazala.

Just reading through stuff, I found some more information about the positioning of German and Italian defensive positions during Battleaxe.
At Qalala, other than the 6th Oasis Company, there was one battery of Italian guns from the 1st Group, 2nd Fast Artillery Regiment.

It's known the 1st Battalion, Royal Northumberland Fusiliers, a machine gun battalion, definitely took part in the attack on Tobruk. Whether the 1st Battalion, Chester Regiment did the same is unknown.

And with that, we have found all the information about the units that I need. It is time to construct a final order of battle for the British Capture of Tobruk so that this problem is never anybody else's.
All the numbers have finally been found. There is no more confusion. Everything is done. And what a satisfying journey it has been, despite all the misery!

But for the units I haven't kept track of, maybe there's a bit of value in attempting to bring more information to the collective world.
For that reason, I'll do a bit of work in tracking the companies of artieri (builders) who unlike the companies of signals didn't share the number of their mixed engineer battalions.

Not Included:
40th "Cacciatori d'Africa" Infantry Division (African Engineer Company)
Artieri Battalions - they had their own internal companies
Blackshirt Divisions - unknown, if I were to guess they were numbered 200+
For example the 4th Artieri Battalion had 1st, 2nd, and 3rd Companies, and the 15th Artieri Battalion had the 1st, 2nd, and 104th Companies. The Alpini Corps also had its own specialized battalion.

This is for the companies only, which were assigned to the divisions. Each (most) corps had a raggruppamento of engineers including a battalion of artieri.

The commander of the 3rd Company, 28th "Pavia" Infantry Regiment was a Francesco Coco. He was killed during the siege of Tobruk on 30 November 1941.
The commander of the 203rd Artillery Regiment which was part of the 64th "Catanzaro" Infantry Division was an Ermenegildo Farfaneti, killed on 11 December 1940.
The commander of an unknown group of the 44th "Marmarica" Artillery Regiment was a Giuseppe Ghione, killed on an unknown day in January 1940.
The commander of the 1st Medium (M11/39) Tank Company, 4th Tank Infantry Regiment was a Giuseppe Locatelli, killed on 19 November 1940.
(I thought this wouldn't be relevant, but I remembered that many of the base maps, including Operation Compass, are out of date, so that's no longer the case.)
The chief of staff of Bergonzoli's XXIII Army Corps was a Achille Meneghini, killed on an unknown day in January 1940.
The commander of the 157th Infantry Regiment, 63rd "Cirene" Infantry Division was a Alfredo Marone. He survived the war.
The commander of one group of 105/28 guns in the 24th Corps Artillery Raggruppamento was a Giuseppe Pasquali. He died on 26 December 1941.
The commander of the 3rd Battalion, 157th "Cirene" Infantry Regiment was a Michele Purrello. He was killed on 3 January 1941.
Three members of the 61st Light Tank Battalion (for the 61st "Sirte" Infantry Division but ended up with the Babini Group) were Adelio Ponce de Leon, Aldo Bruni, and Claudio d'Amico.

The VIII Battaglione Lavoratori, or 8th Labor Battalion, was initially subordinated to the XX Army Corps Engineer Raggruppamento. Its commander was Vincenzo Fusco, who was killed 9 Decmeber 1940.
Rostagno notes that this battalion was in Tobruk. If it was, it was certainly not at full strength and didn't have its original commander with it. I don't know much about its actions in either battle.
One company was noted as located at Capuzzo. Given the fates of other units that ended up in that area I wouldn't be surprised if this company was destroyed and little left of the battalion made it to Tobruk.

About the mixed engineer battalions in North Africa, I don't have all that much information, but I believe these are the most likely realities:
60th "Sabratha" Infantry Division: 72nd Engineer Company, Mobilized by the 20th Engineer Regiment
61st "Sirte" Infantry Division: 73rd Engineer Company, Mobilized by the 20th Engineer Regiment
62nd "Marmarica" Infantry Division: 74th Engineer Company, Mobilized by the 21st Engineer Regiment
63rd "Cirene" Infantry Division: 75th Engineer Company, Mobilized by the 21st Engineer Regiment
64th "Catanzaro" Infantry Division: Former blackshirt engineer company
(This has since been discredited)

The official Italian General Staff Historical Office book about the engineers adds a bit more but doesn't include concrete numbers. But here's what it includes:
The LXI Mixed Engineer Battalion was initially formed in Bir Ghinea and then transferred to Misurata by 30 April 1938. Until its formation, the Sirte Division had no engineer units.
Still no detail about the component elements of the LXI Mixed Engineer Battalion, but I stand by my number of 73 for the artieri company and the normal 61 for the signals company.

Another big mistake by me that I should probably own up to here: I mistranslated "reparto" as "detachment" many times. That's not what it means. It just means "unit". Like, any unit.
Also, "aliquota", while the translation is kinda screwy, just means "part" or "portion". It can also be abbreviated "aliq.", meaning that a unit is incomplete in its deployment.

Of the 6th Australian Division's 3 field regiments (2/1st, 2/2nd, and 2/3rd), only the first 2 were available at Bardia but the 2/3rd managed to join the division at Tobruk.
Among the 3 Infantry Brigades of the Division, there was a total of just 11 anti-tank guns, the strength of a single battery from an anti-tank regiment. (The actual 2/1st Anti-Tank Regiment had not arrived.)

The structure of the 7th Support Group isn't detailed to the smallest subunit in any of my sources, but we can learn a bit from the similar 1st Support Group of 1st Armoured Division, on September 1939.
The unit's headquarters was termed a battery rather than a company. The 1st Support Group's infantrymen were the 1st Rifle Brigade and 2nd King's Royal Rifle Corps, each with 4 motorized companies.
Its two field regiments, the 1st and 2nd RHA, had 2 batteries of 2 troops of 4 guns apiece. Each battery also had 6 anti-aircraft machine guns and 4 light anti-tank rifles.
The engineer component consisted of little: just a single engineer company and field park troop (platoon level). The Commander, Royal Engineers was also subordinated to the Support Group for some reason.
Worst of all, the divisional headquarters had a divisional signals "battalion" but it consisted of just a single company, the 1st Company, Royal Corps of Signals.

By the May 1940 reforms, most of the idiosynchracies of the above establishment had been fixed. The engineers were no longer part of the support group.
The signals unit had been expanded massively. Each of the two armored brigades got its own company, unlikely the single one split among all brigades of an infantry division. The total thus came to 4 companies.
Honestly this was a rare moment of insight in the organization of British Armoured Divisions during this period of the war. They at least understood the value of good communications.

I also read the original text of Playfair's "The Mediterranean and Middle East: Volume 1" but in all honesty he adds very little. Few orders of battle and unit details are included.

The 1st Battalion, South Staffordshire Regiment had the most miniscule possible role to play in the North African Campaign.
Just the A Comapny of this battalion joined Selby Force and struck Sidi Barrani. Afterward, the battalion just escorted prisoners and did rear-line duties until being sent to India, leaving the theater.
The 67th (Suffolk) and 68th (4th West Lancashire) Medium Regiments, Royal Artillery were both sent to reinforce Klopper's force defending Tobruk and both were captured in the subsequent defeat.

3rd Regiment, Royal Horse Artillery had its P and J companies detached to 4th Indian Division in October 1940. I don't know if the batteries were eventually returned but it seems unlikely that they left for East Africa.

The 2nd Battalion, Rifle Brigade was a unique battalion that followed an axis-style structure with a heavy company (in British terminology, this was called the "S" company).
It seems that some regiments and their battalions decided to conduct this reform at some point and that it wasn't followed by most of the army which stuck with companies A-D. All of them also had companies lettered HQ.

According to Montanari, only a portion of the G.a.F. personnel and anti-tank guns were committed to the first line of the Eastern Sector. I'm going to not worry about this, though, as I can't track it.
Instead, the four rifle companies attached to the sector's frontline will be the interval troops. And yes, they are necessary, as the 500 meter intervals were seriously in need of defense.
I was thinking that this might mean the strength of the G.a.F. in this sector was less than 6 companies, maybe because of Rostagno's report claiming that it was down 2 strongpoitns, but there's no justification.
If this did happen, I would have to find where these personnel went, and there's nowhere. We know only about 4 companies of strength were sent to the frontier and that was it in terms of drawdowns.
Those 1000 men were never followed by another force. So there's no chance for the defense of Tobruk to decrease even further.

Out of interest, I've attempted to transcribe the order of battle for Ras el Dai, a position held by Pescatori's 2nd Libyan Division:
2nd "Orfella" Libyan Battalion, 16th "Azizia" Libyan Battalion, 2 77/28 Batteries (divisional), 1 105/28 Battery (from II/21st CAR), 1 20/65 Battery with 6 Guns, 1 47/32 Company With 6 Guns

I think by now there's no more appetite for changes to the G.a.F. infantry, so let's just recap everything and make sure it's all consistent and going well.
There were initially 3300 personnel, 1500 in the XXXI sector and 1800 in the XXXII sector. Each had 6 strongpoitns, so the XXXI sector had 250 each and the XXXII sector had 300 each.
Montanari notes 3 positions were reconstituted, each with a "heavily reinforced company". It's like the same happened to Giarabub, resulting in a fourth company.
These personnel replaced the troops of the 150th Blackshirt Battalion which were tied down acting as the garrison, freeing them up to get destroyed deeper into Egypt.
These 4 companies were taken evenly, with two taken from the XXXI sector and two from the XXXII sector. This decreased the strength of the Tobruk garrison by 1000 men.
With all of these changes, the XXXII sector's command had been moved forward to handle the reconstituting troops, or had been disbanded entirely, leaving behind its four strongpoints.
At this point, Rostagno made his report. He noted that two strongpoints had been taken from the XXXI sector but lacked any information on the composition of the XXII sector.

When the 2nd "Volontari della Libia" Blackshirt Battalion arrived, it was slotted into subsector C/E. The existing two companies/strongpoints of G.a.F. there were sent to subsector A.
This resulted in a final defense of six strongpoints for subsector XXXI/A, two for subsector XXXI/B, and two loose remnant strongpoints that were probably directly subordinate to the XXXI sector.
While this doesn't align with Rostagno's report as changes had been made, it's safe to assume that Pitassi Mannella made the above changes, as they certainly do improve the defense dispositions.
Montanari also doesn't give anything of value regarding the "Volontari della Libia" battalion and in fact manages to show it with just one company. I'm gonna disagree on that one. Unless...

I suspect from the cursed counting that the naval battery "Vittoria", armed with three 76/40 (misspelled by Montaari as 76/30) guns, was actually detached from one of the existing naval batteries.
I have no other idea where its origin would be. There's no ship "Vittoria" that went anywhere near Tobruk and there's no record anywhere else of such a battery.

I'm going to put together a sort of cursed mixed battery to deal with the loose anti-tank gun elements that were lying around the fortress.
This battery is composed of the 2 149/35s of the "Buffolotto" section, 1 76/40 found in the naval stores, and 1 47/32 from the G.a.F., totaling 4 disparate guns.
Since the only named element in its composition is the "Buffolotto" section, I'm going to use that name for the mixed battery in total.
Also, the term Montanari uses to refer to the Tobruk reserve is "Unità Celere", or "Rapid Unit". This unit was split into two halves, each of which was roughly a battalion in size. The state of its headquarters is unknown.

Admiral Vietina's command isn't exactly clear and I lack an Italian name for it, but the one Niehorster gives for the local area is "Tobruk Coastal Sector" or "Settore Costiero di Tobruch" (translated by me).
I was able to find some additional information about him. In February 1937 he was the commander of the Cruiser "Eugenio di Savoia". He died according to this information in 1967.

A loose signal company is listed alongside the various units of the Babini group (many tanks and artillery, Bersaglieri battalion, 2 random machine guns lying around).
The 55th Signal Company is never mentioned anywhere else in the book except in Rostagno's document. I think it's then reasonable to say that this is where the company was at previously.

While the headquarters company of the 140th Blackshirt Battalion was at Tobruk, one rifle company and the support weapons company were at Bardia where they were destroyed.
In his sketches, Montanari even shows the position of the battalion as equivalent to the others, though we know that it was only a half-sized detachment there.

There's a cursed possible interpretation of what Montanari gives in his sketch 18 on Tobruk. He shows only a single company from the "Volontari della Libia" battalion at the Ras es Sehel strongpoint.
If this is to be taken at face value (which I certainly don't), this implies that the XXXII Coverage Sector maintained all four of its strongpoints in the west and none were sent to the east.
Also, this seems a little cooked, since Montanari refers to the XXXI Coverage Sector as "reinforced" first, then "reinforced with four companies" implying it was reinforced first with personnel from the XXII Coverage Sector.
He also does give subsector lengths which I think are to be believed. He gives 12km for A, 8km for B, 8km for C, 12km for D, 8km for E.

John Muirhead is typically known as the commander of the 4th Anti-Aircraft Brigade thanks to the unreliable Australian sources. But he wasn't always in command.
Until September, this brigade was commanded by a different brigadier, J. Slater. I really do think that Australian sources regarding the North African Campaign tend to be poor quality.

Immediately after Sonnenblume, when attacks against Tobruk were underway, Gott's force, then called the "Mobile Force" (no translation, duh), consisted of the following elements:
22nd Guards Brigade Group (1st DLI, 2nd SG, 3rd CG, 4th RTR)
4 Jock Columns (1 Battery + 1 Company Each)
1 Company of the Free French Motor Battalion (Playfair doesn't identify which one or what the battalion was)

Upon being trapped in Tobruk, the 3rd Armoured Brigade had the following composition:
1 Regiment of Armoured Cars, 1 Composite Light Tank Regiment, 1 Composite Cruiser Tank Regiment, 1 Infantry Tank Troop
Playfair also includes an OOB for Gott's forces during Brevity, but all it adds is that 2nd RTR and 4th each had a strength of 2 squadrons.
I also found a page that includes the full list of embarkations for the 6th Australian Division. This would be useful if it included any combat record whatsoever.

The 7th Motor Brigade was reformed from the 7th Support Group on 9 February 1942. Most of its artillery elements had been taken away the day before to be given to other units.

It's worth saying that I have no idea how the 1st Medium Tank Battalion summoned tanks from nowhere, as on 21 September, they only had 9 mobile and 23 immobile tanks.
Some tanks must have arrived in Tobruk to reinforce them, then, as they would soon have as many as 39 total tanks. Either these were from the 2nd Medium Tank Battalion or they were entirely new.
I'm leaning more to the second. It makes sense for the 32 existing tanks to be mostly intact but ineffective while the 7 extra tanks were brought in from elsewhere, likely in a fresh shipment from Italy.

Even now I still struggle to find an adequate translation for the word "autotransportabile". After all, auto-transportable isn't a term most English speakers were readily recognize.
"Truck-movable" is what Niehorster uses, but I think it's not clear. While the unit could be moved with trucks, it often lacked much organic transport to a crippling degree.
I prefer "semi-motorized". While this is vague, it's at least true, as parts of the division were motorized and others weren't. But it does somewhat give the illusion that motorization was even, which was no true.
"Partially-motorized" has the same problem and "mixed motorization" sounds awful to the ears. There's not really a good option I can think of so far.

Niehorster shows two strange CC.NN. units in Turin. These are neither legions nor are they independent cohorts, but rather, entirely unique units.
The first is the "Lavoratori Fiat", composed of Fiat factory workers. The second is the Coorte Autnoma Mutilati, composed of soldiers wounded in WW1.
It's a shame neither of these were formed in different versions elsewhere or saw any combat. Such unique units, entirely wasted by history.

Montanari claims that the 4th Tank Infantry Reigment was reinforced with "long-range and curved-range support weapons". In all honestly I have no idea what these are.
In his other book a bit of clarification is added but doesn't justify the previous statement. All it includes in the set are 4 81/14 mortars and 12 MGs.
Actually, when I wrote this I must've sounded pretty stupid. It's pretty apparent that the mortars in question fit this description of long-range and curved-fire weapons (all heavy weapons are "support" to the Italians).

Also, the "Buffolotto" section was entirely a G.a.F. unit. Going back to read through, I couldn't find a single mention of this unit having any actual naval identity.
But it gets worse. The later book changes its name to "Buffolotti". And neither of these is an actual word in Italian. I'm not joking, they both translate to nothing.
I think that this is ultimately a pluralization (of a nonexistent word, though) and thus it makes more sense in light of other battery names to use to singular "Buffolotto".
At the very least, though, its composition is not in doubt. It was a section (in my game upgraded to mixed battery) with 2 149/35 guns. A platoon, or firing battery, consisted of two of these (4 guns).
That was the attacking portion of a battery (company-sized unit). The other elements of the battery other than the platoon/firing battery were all support components.

After many changes, I think it's time to release my updated version of the previous OOB.

An interesting debate exists around whether to model "San Giorgio" as multiple batteries or just one. Here are some arguments on both sides:
Multiple:
They can shoot independently
They have different fields of fire (not modeled in game)
Number of guns (invalidated by British battery numbers and naval branch)
One:
They can move independently (invalidated because they can't move)
They take up too much space
(This debate would have resulted in the relacement of the "Buffolotto" battery that is also partially fictional)
They can reorganize separately (invalidated because I intend to disable reorganizing on ships altogether)

One of the anti-aircraft groups responsible for defending the Dodecanese islands had a total of 21 subordinate batteries yet was still a battalion-level unit.
So I think it wouldn't be a stretch to call the Tobruk coastal sector, consisting of 11 batteries and a ship, a battalion-level unit as well.

By my estimations, the engineers at Tobruk did little. The British and Australian ones had to contend with a minefield that barely existed at all and no anti-tank ditch.
For the Italians, things were not much better. Not once are the engineers mentioned outside of an order of battle. They were probably little more than laborers or maintenance workers.

To my horror, I am now once again forced to count artillery pieces.
The reason for this is that the 337 vs 343 controversy has now been resolved. The answer was that the Wikipedians screwed up.
Rather than 123 guns in each of the two eastern artillery groups, there were 123 TOTAL between them. Thus 123+97 results in the total of 220 field guns.
Missing (Still): 3 76/30, 1 149/35, 3 100/17. I think at this point we might as well give most of these to the "Vittoria" battery and then try to jank everything else into place.
Also, I think the only real way to make this all fit with both the numbers and Montanari's sketch is to say that the 105/28 battery of the 22nd CAR was actually split.
That way, half of it can be with the mixed group of the G.a.F. without there being too many 105/28 guns in total. I can't think of any other way for this to work.

I'm gonna go back to the WDF map of Tobruk, which graciously includes the armnament of each individual strongpoint and see what I can gather about the dispositions.

The issue is that I'm not sure how 49 became 42, especially given that this map is from January 12, far too late for the garrison to have made any further reorganizations. The G.a.F. frontier move had already happened.
