Yet somehow, the WDF/XIII Corps map managed to find a total of 123 guns, way more than this. They couldn't have been taken by the G.a.F., so where are they?
Honestly, I'm gonna go ahead and just disregard this map. It seems outdated and a lot of stuff just doesn't make sense.
For example, there are 6 or more 120/25 batteries shown, as well as some coastal 120/25 batteries, and also 6 or more 105/28 batteries. None of that matches up.
One solution to this problem is to not have it. I'll go for this for now by downright ignoring all the anti-tank guns and integrating them into the strongpoint units.

The civilian population of Tobruk in 1939 was just 5032 people, compared to the more than a hundred thousand in Tripoli. Marmarica in general was poor and utterly barren of useful resources.
Worse, we can expect that after the three battles, this population was further decreased. We know that the British evacuated many "useless" personnel during their siege so maybe this is where they came from.

The source of the Carabinieri that appear defending the port were probably not the three sections that the 22nd Army Corps and Sirte Division had between them.
Rather, I think it's more likely that these personnel came from the local barracks (one was present in Tobruk) and were not an organized formation in any way.

The three main posts of the XXX Coverage Sector on the Egyptian border were at Amseat, Scegga, and Giarabub. I honestly couldn't tell you what the first two actually contributed to the battle.
This suggests that the organizations after Operation E probably resulted in the some of these posts becoming considered unnecessary and getting disbanded.
It's also weird that the Italians built a "Sheferzen" strongpoint when Bir Sheferzen is on the wrong side of the Frontier Wire. There must have been a "Libyan Sheferzen" or something that I don't know about.

One source claims that the "Assault Tanks" of the Italian army were never built, and instead they built the L3 Tank. This is quite funny given the L3 Tank is literally the "Assault Tank" discussed previously.
This guy also wrongly believes that the Blackshirt Divisions were organized in the triangular structure of the metropolitan legions. This was not the case as they were forced to adopt standard army organizations.
He also claims that the Libyan CC.NN. Legions were useless (true) but that the Volontari della Libia Battalion was "outstanding" and "fought well" which I would debate since it didn't do much of anything.
And in the most strange of all his claims, he states the existence of an Italian XIII Corps with the 1st Libyan Division. This corps had been in Sardinia the entire war and never made it to Libya.
Yet another mistake comes when he notes that non-motorized infantry were useless in the North African theatre. He seems to not realize that both the Division z.b.V. Afrika and Savona Division seem to have done quite well.

The worst crime of this guy, however, is that he ends up repeating the standard claim that Graziani blundered by advancing using a large infantry force rather than a smaller motorized one.
I want to dispell this myth because, while Graziani was a war criminal, he was absolutely not a complete idiot. He was well aware of the value of motorization and would've taken that choice if it was available.
But it was not available. There was a crushing lack of trucks or fuel in Libya to actually motorize anything. There's a reason that Raggruppamento Maletti, a non-divisional formation, got the few that were around.

The dumbest statement of them all, though not relevant to here, is that he argues that launching Operation Sealion in July 1940 made "good tactical sense". What?
The Kriegsmarine was hopeless and there wasn't a good chance of getting more than a single convoy across the channel. Why did he say this? It just makes no sense and I have no idea how this guy came to this conclusion.

After a relatively inconsequential engagement on 5 August 1940 in which the Italians got revenge for D'Avanzo's defeat and captured two British tanks, Colonel Aresca wrote a report.
While he thought the lack of optics and radio in the tanks continued to weaken them, he was satisfied with their performance and asked for more support equipment and personnel.

The 47/32 company and two 65/17 batteries given to the 1st Libyan Division in reinforcement both came from the G.a.F. as did the two 65/17 batteries given to the 2nd Libyan Division.
The 19th Libyan Infantry Battalion had its three companies split up, with two at Nibeiwa and the third at Alam el Iktufa. The Alam el Iktufa force also had a 47/32 company, 65/17 battery, and 20/65 section.
The 135th Blackshirt Battalion arrived at Sollum on the 9th of December to meet with the rest of the 2nd Blackshirt Division. The III Group of the 202nd Artillery Regiment (100/17) was also there.
This guy also claims that the III/43rd Artillery group had just 8 100/17 guns. If this was the case it was only temporary since otherwise there was no way for enough 100/17 guns to appear in Tobruk
Also, wasn't the group with 100/17 guns meant to be the I Group? I don't trust this source much anyway and this isn't really something that I can easily reconcile.

One company of the 1st KRRC was deployed to support the 3rd Coldstream Guards at Halfaya Pass at the start of Compass as well as one company from the 1st RNF MG Battalion.
The tank component of D'Avanzo's column was the 3rd Company, IX Light Tank Battalion (L3), commanded by a Captain Rizzi. Since it was destroyed the battalion reorganized itself with just two companies.
It appears the light tank battalions had just 40 tanks apiece, rather than the later 46, with just 13 in each company. It seems this is because of the lack of the two radio tanks per company that were later added.

I also read through a bit of Rodolfo Graziani's book, "A Life For Italy", which I thought was decently well-written but had absolutely nothing of value in relation to the Tobruk battle.

It seems some mixed engineer battalions were reinforced with bonus searchlight units in addition to the single section they typically have.
The 33rd "Acqui" Infantry Division's 33rd Mixed Engineer Battalion (the battalion may have been disbanded) had a 7th Searchlight Company and 4 independent sections split across the Ionian islands.

In fact, I've gone into something of a paper-reading phase. I'll just list all the major takeaways for this next set here as a couple giant blocks.
Each will also be added to sources, but might not result in much in terms of actual gains, since they are only adjacent to the Crusader and Tobruk topics.

The first is written from a South African perspective regarding their two defeats at Sidi Rezegh and Tobruk. The writing is pretty good and they criticize popular history, which I agree with.
I could only find one minor mistake, in that they claimed Tobruk fell to the British before Bardia did. But other than that everything has been rather excellent.
The British gun, the 3.7 inch, was basically a 88mm gun. The Italians also had the 90/53. It's mainly due to their aggressive tactics that the 88mm is much more famous than either of them.
Two days after becoming CiCME, Auckinleck told Churchill that "It is quite clear to me that infantry divisions... are no good". Yeah, he wasn't subtle about his poor knowledge.
Brink complained about the use of his division as it arrived in Egypt between May and June 1941, as it was employed as construction crews to build up the Matruh defenses.
And I'm very happy to see that South African historians do nothing to downplay the Italian victory at Bir el Gubi, and in fact are fast to come to the Italians' defense.
They also claim that Jan Smuts showed some actual military talent and in fact argued for the combined arms utilization of 1st South African Division, but his wishes fell on deaf ears and Brink didn't press the issue.
Brink made some more delusional reports after the defeat at Bir el Gubi, claiming that the Italian M13/40 tanks were actual partially crewed by Germans, which is just an insane thing to say.
TIK gives 30 tanks lost by 15th Panzer Division in the first engagement with the 4th Armoured Brigade but the South African historians consider this a gross overestimation.
The Ariete Division's tank strength only decreased by something like 30 after Bir el Gubi since repairs were made. Most of their losses instead attrition or minor battles (8 lost to South Africans on 21 November).
The Sidi Rezegh "box" position was supported not just by South African artillery but also by elements of the 2nd Scots Guards, 1st Light Anti-Aircraft Regiment, and the 3rd South African Armored Car Regiment.
The 5th South African Brigade had at that time 46 25-pounders, 2 18-pounders and 24 2-pounders for a total of 72 artillery pieces, 35 facing south.
Brink also took the wrong lessons from the destruction at Sidi Rezegh. He in fact thought that the British forces should become even more static ("box" defenses) and discouraged night moves.

Moving on, the second piece of this paper covers the defeat at Tobruk. So far, it has in fact offered a very modern and nuanced analysis of the last battle so I'll keep reading.
The board of inquiry set up to examine that battle largely exonerates Klopper, but even the paper admits that his handling of the battle was in many ways rather inept.
Klopper later published some memoirs of his own and made sure that Auckinleck's ones didn't point out his failures. I'm somewaht sympathetic to his position but not very.
There's also a very raw quote regarding the imprecision of personal accounts: "the individual being in the center of the densest clouds generated by the fog of war".
Klopper had in fact been responsible for the orders that resulted in the successful reduction of Schmitt's position in Bardia at minimal casualties (the second assault, as the hasty first had failed).
At Gazala, while the 1st South African Brigade was a veteran formation that had taken 138 prisoners during Crusader for 389 casualties (mostly due to air attack), Du Toit's 2nd was poorly trained.
The Gazala "box" defenses also had their transport sent away to avoid it being destroyed by artillery fire, leaving these positions immobile. It was the same mistake the Italians made in 1940.
Barnett and Carver disagree on Ritchie's defensive plans at Gazala, with Barnett arguing that only a thin screen (like that of O'Connor in 1940) should have faced the initial advance.
I side with the latter, however. O'Connor could only retreat because there was nothing of value up to Matruh. Giving up Tobruk for little of a fight would've been political and military suicide.
However, while this claim by Barnett is a little unreasonable, I still respect him as a historian for his reasonable appraisals of Ritchie, Auckinleck, and Montgomery as commanders.
The destruction of the 3rd Indian Motor Brigade was actually a massacre. The Italians suffered just 76 casualties and 25 tanks and inflicted 494 killed and wounded plus 600 prisoners.

As early as 10 May 1942, Ritchie and Auckinleck had agreed and given orders to their corps commanders that Tobruk was not to be held. ABC and Tedder agreed.
British communications were so poor by this point that Klopper only learned of the loss of El Adem when a reconaissance force from the Umvoti Mounted Rifles found the enemy there.
I agree with the paper that the Morshead's Australian Division had it easy and faced exclusively poor planned attacks by Rommel. Their pride over the South Africans is unjustified.
Most secondary sources are wrong about the poor state of the Tobruk defenses in the second siege, as many other engineer officers disagree with Gaussen's claims.
The need to deploy a portion of the 6th South African Brigade along the coast is often criticized but wasn't a horrible move given the lack of armored cars in the second siege.
TIK is also wrong that the German claim of the bombardment neutralizing the minefields is incorrect. Eyewitness accounts note the premature detonation of many of the mines.
Reeve of the 7th RTR might actually have been named Reeves with an s. I think this is a more normal name but there's no shortage of goofy British names.
Brigadiers Willison and Johnson was not blameless in the failed counterattack. Willison failed to show up entirely and Johnson didn't order the infantry units to take any initiative.
The overoptimistic claims of Klopper were not caused by him as much as they were caused by the lackluster state of communications in the 11th Indian Infantry Brigade's sector.
Hayton, whose idea it was to form a defensive base in his sector rather than conduct a breakout, later changed his decision, further confusing the fortress command.
Unsurprisingly, the Blake Group was commanded by a Lt.-Col. Blake. I don't know if he was from a headquarters or if he was a formal unit commmander.
Only a single general, Klopper, was captured. However, this is because the British don't consider brigadier to be a general rank, as seven of them were lost.

They conclude with an honestly very insightful point that South Africa, which technically won but in a highly qualified manner, is similar in its remembrance of the war to France or Italy.
Pienaar claimed that rather than masking the Ariete Division, he was in fact locked in a difficult battle with the 21st Panzer Division at Taieb el Esem, which was a complete lie.

Notes on commanders:
Lt.-Col W. Kirby was commander of the 3rd Transvaal Scottish Battalion and was killed at Sidi Rezegh (in the failed attack on Point 178, not the climactic destruction of 5th South African Infantry Brigade).
Armored car companies were numbered sequentially. The 1st was commanded during Crusader by Major Harry Klein.
A major Cochran became the acting commander of the 1st South African Irish Regiment after its previous commander was incapacitated by some means (I don't know which).
Colonel Bastin was the quartermaster general of XIII Corps and stayed in Tobruk to manage the extensive supply installations based there.
Brigadier C. de L. Gaussen was the chief engineer of XIII Corps, Brigadier F.H. Kisch was the chief engineer of 8th Army and Colonel Henderson was chief engineer of 2nd South African Division.
The commander of the Tobruk artillery (he may have been from XIII Corps or from 2nd South African Division) was a Colonel Richards (given his rank he was probably from the division).

In a kinda boneheaded move I didn't realize that the 4th "Genova Cavalleria" Machine Gun SQUADRONS GROUP was in fact made of squadrons and not companies.
I think I implemented this somewhere but it never occurred to me that this should also be translated into English as "Squadron", not as "Company".

A standard Italian squadrons group had a tiny HQ and two regular squadrons. A regiment of two squadrons groups would also bring a light machine gun squadron.
I don't know if this is Ness's research or the mitigation of an Italian archivist, but the artillery regiments of the Pavia and Brescia Divisions didn't "fail to be shipped". They were destroyed.
In February 1941, a Raggruppamento celere della Sicilia was formed out of fear caused by Compass and the rapidly arriving need for more units in Africa, although it never made it there.
This consisted of the 6th Bersaglieri Regiment (plus a motorcycle company), a light tank battalion, and an anti-tank battalion (a special newly formed unit with 3 motorized companies).
Funnily, the 3rd "Principe Amadeo Duca d'Aosta" Celere Division actually had no cavalry by 1942 since they had become their own unit. Their old division was now just full of Bersaglieri.

While I don't know how the 4th "Genova Cavalleria" Machine Gun Squadrons Group was organized the similar squadrons group units sent to East Africa had just 2 squadrons.
Never mind, I do. Montanari gives 4 squadrons, honestly a pretty high number. In his typical blundering Nafziger translated this to "4 platoons". How is he so bad at this?
At the very least, he does give sources which softens the blow and gives me a chance to prove him wrong. But this level of poor quality is honestly unacceptable and it seems the AHF people agree.
The 10th Bersaglieri Regiment was the only one originally in Africa and thus had a unique organization with just 1790 men (96 HQ, 3 x 463 Battalions,  155 AT Company, 150 Transport)

Of the four battalions that formed the 34th GaF Artillery Regiment (reorganized from the 34th Raggruppamento) during Crusader, one actually managed to survive to make it to Tunisia.
That battalion was the 343rd, and it contained two batteries of 105/28. While its survival is impressive, 8 outdated guns were of little to not consequence at this point in the campaign.

There was also a mysterious fourth battalion of the 12th "Sila" Artillery Regiment that appears at this time and was converted to the 357th GaF Battalion. It had 3 75/27 batteries.
This is weird given the Savona Division was destroyed by this time. My guess is that this was actually the replacement for the 1st/12th Battalion that had been lost on transport.
Upon arriving only to find that its parent division and regiment no longer existed, the battalion had no need to exist anymore and was reorganized into a GaF unit since only GaF depots were in Tripoli.

I don't know if this was the case for Tobruk, but the port defense commands in mainland Italy and Sicily were given the term "Comando di Difesa del Porto".
There were also coastal sections according to Niehorster. He mispells that for Tobruk as "coast section" but I don't think there's much of any real difference between these terms.
I think the harbor defense (port defense) and coastal defense sectors were distinct functions, though. But I can't tell what this means for Vietina, if he did in fact have a sector and not a harbor.
Or, if he had the harbor and the sector, I wouldn't be able to prove that just yet. I might look around to see if I can find anything on the matter, though.
Montanari actually gives his title as "command of the naval base" or "comandante della base navale" so I really can't disambiguate this. I guess we go with the Montanari answer?

The Trento Division appears to have been reinforced at some point with the 104th and 106th companies of 47/32 guns. They appear in a map of Montanari dated 14-17 April 1941.
However, this is entirely new to me since they haven't appeared anywhere else... yet. But maybe that's just because I haven't spent much of any time looking.
Yeah. I checked Wikipedia and they're right there. So I'm going to note that I somewhat badly mismanaged this and never really researched the Trento Divsiion as much as the Savona, Sabratha, or Sirte (3S).

Full list of additional units given to the Trento Division:
156th Bersaglieri Motorcycle Company (September 1941)
II Group of the 46th Artillery Regiment (existed in Spring 1941 but not in November)
412th and 414th AA Batteries of 20/65
102nd, 104th, and 106th Anti-Tank Companies of 47/32 (merged into the DLI Support Weapons Battalion in September 1941)
IV Anti-Tank Battalion "Granatieri di Sardegna" and the 254th and 355th GaF Artillery Groups of 77/28 (By Second El Alamein)

In preparation for the attack of the British on November 1941, the Italians had some interesting maps drawn that Montanari reproduces.
For one, they identify a bunch of stuff wrong. The 70th Division is now the 8th Division, it has a 6th Brigade, and the 2nd Cameron Highlanders are numbered the 11th.
Also, they have some interestingly named columns given for the British, other than the 11th Indian Infantry Brigade, 7th Support Group, and 70th Division. For example:

Colonna "Piccola Sorella" ("Little Sister" Column):
Elements of the 1st Battalion, King's Royal Rifle Corps
1 Battery From the 2nd Regiment, Royal Horse Artillery
1 Light Anti-Aircraft Section

Other columns: "Piccolo Fratello" ("Little Brother"), "Fratello" ("Brother"), "Sorella" ("Sister")
All of these columns were formed from elements of the 7th Support Group to guard the frontier while the 8th Army was still in formation.
In fact, they have pretty much the same structure as Jock Columns, at least in the case of this one. Maybe that was the point of their formation.
I don't know what the British called them, however, since they don't appear anywhere. Playfair is completely silent about any of them and the names were probably not the ones the British used.
But even though we don't know exactly, I think it would honestly be very funny to have these names on the columns, but it's whatever.

Montanari also gives different OOBs for the three columns that participated in Operation (Unternehmen) Sommernachstraum.
Left Column (Kampfgruppe Stephan): 5th Panzer Regiment, 1 MG Company, 1 Anti-Tank Company, 1 Engineer Company
Center Column (Recovery Unit (Kampfgruppe Panzenhagen?)): 1 Anti-Tank Company, 1 Artillery Battery, Many Recovery Trucks
Right Column (Kampfgruppe Schütte): 33rd Reconaissance Battalion, 1 Artillery Battery
These are nothing like what TIK's sources give, which is worrying. I feel as if Montanari is the generally more reliable source so I'll take his side.
However, the Operation Sommernachtstraum mission is already done. So that means its time to go do some rebuilding.
I'm also going to guess that the center column did in fact still have the 3rd Battalion, 347th Infantry Regiment since it would be weird to have no infantry at all.
But honestly everything is becoming a mess again. I don't trust Montanari's information about this operation very much so I'm gonna side with not making any major changes.

But there is one cool result, though. The maps with the "family" columns also show the 11th Indian Infantry Brigade still forward, reinforcing my idea for the OOB of Sommernachtstraum.
I don't think elements from the 11th Indian Infantry Brigade actually ended up doing anything until Bir el Gubi, but it's nice to know that I was right when I made that decision.

Panzerjäger means "tank hunter" in German. For this reason, the Italian translation ends up being the kinda cool sounding "cacciatori carri" (meaning the same thing).
Other than that, the Italian translations of German OOBs are surprisingly high quality. One weird one I found was "XV Machine Gun Battalion", which is probably the 15th Motorcycle Battalion.
Also he calls Kampfgruppe Marcks "Raggruppamento Marks" and notes the existence of an XXI Indian Infantry Brigade (probably the 29th but they slightly screwed up the number).
When Daumiller's group from the 288th Sonderverband appears for the first time, it shows up defending the right flank at the Marada Oasis, along with the some Sahiariana troops and GaF units.
The larger 288th Sonderverband reappears as the "288° Reparto Esplorante Tedesca" or 288th German Reconaissance Unit still holding the right flank during the pursuit to the Gazala line.
A 500th Reconaissance Company appears just ahead of it. I have no idea what this unit is and I don't think it was from the 288th, so I honestly don't have any idea what this was.
Actually, now that I think about it, that's probably the 580th Reconaissance Company, which also reappears holding the western front against the Free French at Bir Hacheim.

On the 23rd October 1942, "Subsector Sirte" existed under the military command of Tripolitania's defense, but had no assigned units. Its subsectors were "Sirte" itself and "Nofilia".
This book ("The Third British Offensive") has a ton of maps for a very short period between Second El Alamein's beginning and the arrival and defensive preparations in Tunisia.
In Rommel's canceled November attack on Tobruk, not only did he envision the use of the nearly fully built Afrika Division, but also a battalion from the rebuilding Sabratha Division.
This battalion doesn't seem to have ever arrived, and instead only made it to Sirte where it was protecting the local airfields from potential LRDG raids.

The destroyed garrison force at Gialo was meant to reconsistute the 8th Bersaglieri Battalion. It consisted of the following:
2 Bersaglieri Companies (-2 platoons), a 47/32 Bersaglieri Company (-3 pieces, so it was very weak), a mixed machine gun and anti-tank company (1 platoon of MGs, 1 platoon of 37mm and 47mm guns).
There were also some loose units, including a 47/32 platoon from the Sabratha Division, a training section with 2 75/27 guns, and 20/35 battery with 8 pieces.
The 2 detached platoons and 3 detached 47/32 guns were defending the adjacent Oases of Augila and Gicherra.
I find this actually very weird, since it doesn't seem to be a mixed support battalion at all. Rather, its strength is very similar to that of a regular Bersaglieri battalion (2 rifle and 1 support companies).

Supposedly, on the 8th of August 1941, the Trento Division was not part of the XXI Corps and instead part of the Sabratha Division was there. This may be a classic Montanari mistake though.
He also gives a somewhat unique-seeming OOB for the Afrika Division dated to the 1st of November. Let's break down the somewhat challenging translation here. Numbers in brackets added by me.
[1] Divisional Command With 259th Mapping Detachment (Kartenstelle)
[4] III/347th Infantry Regiment
[4] III/255th Infantry Regiment (in reconstitution) - this was probably caused by its losses fighting inside the Ras el Medauuar salient
[12] 155th Infantry Regiment (3 Battalions)
[9] 2nd Celere Artillery Regiment
    1st Group (3 Batteries of 100/17)
    2nd Group (2 Batteries of 75/27)
    3rd Group (2 Batteries of 75/27)
    402nd Battery of 20/65
    405th Battery of 20/65
Total: ~30 Companies, 8 Battalions
Arguably, this organization is very interesting and could be used for a future variant of the Crusader OOB. A sort of "Crusader but cursed" now sounds like an interesting prospect to me.

He also notes the non-existence of the 4th Company, 1st Battalion, 8th Panzer Regiment at this time, while the 2nd Battalion does have all four companies as do both battalions in 5th Panzer Regiment.

Disaster! I've found evidence that the artieri company of the Sabratha Division was numbered the 27th. The translation is a little cursed since it calls this a battalion, but the number 27 is unused.
This puts into doubt everything I've previously gained about the Sirte Division's engineer company number. Now it could be literally anything. If I find time I'll do some more research on this topic.
On the other hand, it's not conclusive evidence. In fact, the artieri company is shown as a battalion with two companies. Maybe this was a company with two platoons or maybe such a battalion actually existed.

The 559th Anti-Tank Battalion was meant to be part of the the 5th Light Division on its first formation but never actually made it to Africa. Instead it went to the USSR.
It had three companies each consisting of 3.7cm guns. It had originally been motorized but I don't think it was still by the time it was intended to be sent to Africa, hence why it didn't go.

During the Crusader period, Gioda's 10th Army Corps was responsible for the defense of the area east of Sirte, merging his command with the military command of Cyrenaica.
The Bologna Division's 10th Machine Gun Battalion remains an elusive unit of unclear existence. I was unable to find much on the web about it and its only mentioned once in shady circumstances.

The Sirte Division's single section of Carabinieri was numbered the 261st. Or at least I think it was based on the confusing writing "PM 261. 1a Sez. CC.RR."
It appears based on the information about other divisions that this means the Carabinieri command was numbered the 261st, and the subordinate section was just the 1st.
For some cursed reason, posts R27 and R29 in the Tobruk defenses is labeled on Google Maps. These post was in the Sidi Cheiralla Strongpoint. I don't know which battle made them famous.
Based on some information I'm read, it may be because the Germans chose to attack in this area in some of the early attacks against Tobruk as it lacked a deep anti-tank ditch.

Something that annoys me to no end is the fact that the Eastern Artillery Raggruppamento corresponds to Subsector A but the Central Artillery Raggruppamento fails to exactly correspond to Subsector B.
Instead, its 148th Group, 25th CAR manages to poke out into the area of Subsector C. No other unit in the Raggruppamento does this, as even the "Buffolotto" section just manages to not cross the border.
Thus, the Western Artillery Raggruppamento, which should be the only one in the Western Sector, actually had part of its jurisdiction robbed from its grasp and given to the Eastern Sector.
That said, though, the responsibilities of the sectors outside of the forward fortifications is pretty irrelevant. We could just say the line between them gave a little more space to the Eastern Sector.
It still annoys me, though, because these artillery positions were very close behind the strongpoint of Hagfet Umm Haleiga, so a redrawing of borders would look quite ugly.

Sources disagree on the number of Matilda II tanks possessed by the 7th Royal Tank Regiment at Tobruk. Some say 16 (I'd guess 2 companies of 8) while others say 18 (I'd guess 2 companies of 9).
I think the sources for 18 are generally more reliable so that's the number I'm gonna go with. Time to go reorganize all the existing establishments of this unit in game.
A later source states that the 16 number was from after the battle. They lost 2 or more during the battle and any difference were repaired so that the net loss from this battle was 2, which isn't much.

A poster on AHF has posited a potential alternate history scenario where the Italians defended Tobruk successfully. His point of divergence is Marshal Balbo surviving.
I disagree. Balbo was an inspiring man but he couldn't save that trainwreck. I think the better point of divergence is the British failing to draw up exploitation plans early.
This gives enough time for the slippery Bergonzoli to reach Tobruk rather than being holed up in Bardia. With 60,000 men of the 10th Army in Tobruk, they weren't going to lose the fortress.

I've still not made any significant progress on the question of the artieri ever since I got informed of the non-consecutive number for the Sabratha Division's company.
I did try to track the date of formation both of the divisions and the brigades that preceded them, but I obtained no results and it seems the numbering of the companies was unrelated.
I also went and spent some more time doing a bunch of research but obtained little to no results. I don't think anywhere on the internet such information is mentioned, and I doubt the archives would either.

I'm now pretty confident that the 76/30 guns Montanari mentions were actually 76/30 and not 76/40, and they were part of the "Vittoria" battery, which was probably a motorized one.
These were 1915 Armstrong designs, not 1916 designs like the 76/40 guns were. Otherwise they weren't too different. But at least I know they didn't come from another naval battery.
The location of the Tordo battery is also sometimes referred to as Point 98. The other batteries were probably also built on height positions but their numbers aren't mentioned anywhere.

The Tobruk map from the XIII Corps Staff was made by the 512th Survey Company, Royal Engineers, and was reproduced from an Italian map from 1936, making it somewhat badly outdated.
14 of the 47/32 guns there are known to have been moved to Giarabub based on the information regarding dispositions provided by Castagna. The fate of the rest isn't well known.

I've got a better option for the whole AT guns question though. Instead of utter confusion, what if the GaF batteries and loose guns were active in the east but everything was organized in the west.
Sector East - 42 AT Guns
    10 20/65
        All G.a.F.
    8 77/28
        246th G.a.F. Battery
        247th G.a.F. Battery
    8 75/27
        249th G.a.F. Battery
        G.a.F. Training Battery "Derna"
    16 47/32
        All G.a.F.
Sector West - 50 AT Guns
    16 20/65
        10th/20th CAR
        13th/22nd CAR
    13 65/17
        69th Battery
        70th Battery
        Provisional Battery
    21 47/32
        25th Battery
        61st Battery
        Provisional Battery
        G.a.F. Spare Gun
The benefit is this solution is that we get to completely ignore the outdated map and we get to accept the unified philosophy of each sector: G.a.F. in the east, organized army units in the west.

Sources disagree on the status of the Uadi Hammar Guardia alla Frontiera formation. Ascoli calls it an independent strongpoint with 500 men while Regio Esercito calls it an independent subsector.
Ascoli also doesn't note the existence of a Southeast Tripoli Coverage Sector (sometimes numbered the XXXVI) and instead calls it just the "Southeast Front".
Given the supposed coverage sector's very strange organization (2 independent artillery groups and 2 cavalry machine gun squadrons) I think Ascoli's claim is much more convincing.

I've just made the Tobruk December map, which is now the standard for the British Capture of Tobruk scenario. It's a major departure from the old one but I'd call it an improvement.
Rather than Group Barberis, a name for the eastern sector, Barberis is still in command but of the garrison before any XXII Corps units arrived to reinforce. He had only G.a.F. units, though the navy provided backup.
The state of artillery at this early December time is hard to state since all sources I have come from after that or the Summer of 1940. It's not a long period that they were like this, so nothing was recorded.
I'm going to go with the minimalist Rostagno interpretation. There were no guns of any meaningful value except for those of the G.a.F. and the naval batteries, and the spare guns weren't yet active.
So, they have just 1 47/32 (spare gun), 8 75/27, 8 77/28, 4 105/28, 8 120/25, and 8 149/35 of the G.a.F., and all the various guns of the army. I'll also let them have the 2 77/28 and 1 149/13... or will I?
Honestly I don't like this unit. There's a total of 0 proof that it was even a G.a.F. unit or that it existed at this time. Similarly the naval "Nembo" and "Vittoria" batteries might not have been active.
The original set I made was a 20 vs 40. While based on testing this workes very well, I think that, historically, it's highly unsound. So I'm gonna do what I can to build instead a 18 vs 36 scenario.

And, I've also considered purging the naval units entirely and forming a 15 vs 30. But, honestly, that's just too few. It's what I'm gonna keep since it's known, but it ain't great to look at.
I'm gonna add it below, though, with a comparison to the source Montanari/Rostagno text that I'm trying to emulate.

I feel a uniquely strange sense of catharsis after learning about the later fate of Robert Crisp. I don't have anything against this guy other than that his memoirs just sound annoying.
Either way, his disagreements with Montgomery led to several demotions and a transfer from the South African Army to the British one. His career was stagnant for the rest of the war.

I learned based on a British War Diary (C Battery RHA) that the commander of the 1st Medium Tank Battalion was a Major Ceva. But when I checked, I already had this in the game.
Which is weird, because nowhere else on this notes document is his name mentioned. So how this could've possibly happened is anyone's guess. Either way, he was probably Locatelli's successor at the battalion.
I don't have proof he was at Tobruk and in fact it seems like he might have been captured, or maybe only documents about him were captured and he was alive or was killed by some random cause anyway.
It may be that I found this out on Comando Supremo, where an old forum thread mentions this. But I could've gained it from some other place and just never knew. Strange.

C Battery RHA replaced F Battery RHA for the Tobruk battle and was placed under the command of Mackay. At this time, C Battery was commanded by a G.W. Goschen, with G.H. Thomas as his second in command.
Before Jock Campbell took control of 4th RHA, it was actually Wilson of 3rd RHA who commanded 4th RHA. The previous commander of 3rd RHA was Latham (probably the same one who lost 2nd Support Group).
A G.E. Lomas seems to have been commanding F Battery during the later stages (and probably the earlier stages) of Operation Compass. Unlike the rest his name carries no notoriety.
However, he was injured near Pilastrino and replaced by Currie. A rare case of actual damage that the British admit the Italians inflicted upon them. I guess that's why he never appears again.

It appears that the 116th Infantry Regiment from the Marmarica Division was unique in that it had its own anti-tank company rather than relying on a divisional one.
I haven't found anything similar for any other division during this period. It honestly doesn't make any sense and feels like a typo even though I have absolutely no evidence that it is.

So, here's a list of unresolved mysteries:
Sirte Division artieri company number
4th Tank Infantry Regiment anti-aircraft battery number
Organization of 76/40 guns in the naval batteries

The commanders of the squadrons of the 11th Hussars were as following. Note that when Combe left command to obtain a position at Cyrenaica command he was replaced by Leetham.
(Combe) A: Smail, B: Miller, C: Ward
(Leetham) A: Robarts, B: Wainman, C: Ward

By the way, I use to do all the gun counting in this notes file, but now that the counting has been completed inside the Listing/Tobruk/guns.txt file, this will no longer occur here.
Similarly, I'll try to minimize the amount of redundant OOBs that already exist somewhere in either the Listing folder or in the Listing Archive folder, but random incidental ones will still appear here.
Other than every Italian army corps having an artillery officer, they also had an engineer officer each, and of course a chief of staff. Unfortunately most of these staff officers are shady personalities.
However, it's worth noting that I don't know if this man was also the commander of the corresponding Engineer Raggruppamento that almost all corps had, with artieri and collegamenti battalions.

I thought that 20/35 and 20/65 guns might be different models, but this is incorrect. The competing gun to the Breda 20mm was the Scotti 20mm, but that gun had a designation of 20/77.
Rather, the "35" refers to the year, 1935, when this design was first created. The Scotti model wasn't built in any meaningful numbers until 1941 and thus wouldn't show up in Operation Compass.

The XV Anti-Aircraft Group (of 12 75/46) has also been tracked down thanks to tacticalorganization.com. It was one of three groups deployed in September 1940 to the North African coast.
The VII was sent to Benghazi, the XV to Derna, and the XVII to Bardia. This unit from Derna making it to Tobruk mirrored the movement of the XXXI Libyan Coastal Battalion which also formed in Derna.
I think these groups had only been formed recently. It's known that production on the 75/46 was pretty slow and there were constant shortages, and the XV Group doesn't appear in June OOBs.

In a kinda poetic moment, the depots of the Sirte and Catanzaro Divisions were actually both in Catanzaro, which was in the 21st Military Zone of the territorial army.
It's funny, since both of these depots formed field replacement battalions to replenish their overseas divisions. And both of those, the LXI and LXIV, were destroyed in the same battle almost side by side.
Aside from those two Type AS Division depots, the city of Catanzaro's venue was also shared by some other units, those being:
The depot of the 48th "Taro" Infantry Division, a territorial carabinieri legion (couldn't find a designation), the 564th Blackshirt Battalion, and a battery of the 21st Anti-Aircraft Militia Legion.
It's also cool to know that this was a rare case where a divisional depot was actually in the city it was named for, as the Catanzaro Division's depot was in Catanzaro.
In contrast, the Savona Division was based out of Salerno and the Cirene Division was based out of Savona. The Sabratha Division had its depot based in Naples, and the Marmarica Division was based in Chieti.
That said, the Trento Division's depot was actually based out of Trento, though the Trieste Division didn't do the same, being based out of Piacenza.

I'm still not sure what the term for a G.a.F. corps command was. Did it include the words "army corps" or just a number? I think it sounds better with just a number but I don't exactly know.
I think that the need for the words "army corps" was never real and was an invention by my head. Which does make me look kinda goofy to be honest to have made this mistake.
But at least in my case it's already a solved problem, as I've been using the term "Easter Libya G.a.F. Command" and ignoring the number and parent army corps entirely.

With how much searching I'm done, I think this is probably the time to put to bed a very long obsession of mine: https://www.mercari.com/us/item/m53680487151/
It may not be a definitive answer. It sure isn't visually confirmable. But if there's one thing I learned from researching Crusader, it's that often times that's what you have to settle for.

Niehorster.org is down right now. If it doesn't intend to come back up, that's probably fine, since much of it is still preserved in the Waybackmachine, which I'm using for the time being.
However, it's not all that great. A lot of the deeper pages are just completely inaccessible since while the page contents are archived, the image that includes the actual OOB is unavailable.
Curse this guy for his style of site-making. While I think visually it does look great, the fact that making edits in this system must be abysmal was never apparent to him.

The famous Pasta Rule from The Campaign For North Africa (a game I consider generally historically suspect and outdated in its research) was probably a lie and added entirely as a joke by the developer.
There's little to no historical basis that this would ever be true. Water shortages in the Italian military in North Africa were already severe and there wouldn't have been spare water for pasta-making.
Also, pre-cooked pasta was often included in the rations they received, and many times they would have to do entirely with dry foods of varying quality.

Crusader uses a free-movement system. As the game is entirely digitized, there is no need to establish exact positions for units or literally anything since the code handles this problem itself.
On the other hand, there's the interesting case of Movebuild/26. I read some information on redbloblgames/com.grids/hexagons and apparently the system I used is called "axial coordinates".
The alternatives are offest coordinates (I think this system sucks), cube coordinates (axial with slightly more redundancy), and doubled coordinates (useful but very unintuitive).
I think based on this information that I managed to get the best option. Three years ago me who began Movebuild's development was an idiot, but at least he got this right.
While Movebuild/26 is long past the point where this is necessary, a lot of the things it offers, particularly in algorithms, are actually very helpful and worth keeping in the back of my mind.

The 10th Corps Artillery Raggruppamento was just outside Tobruk on the 1st of December 1940. While the unit was probably within the perimeter it was under Pitassi Manella's command, not Barberis's.
So that does mean there are elements of confusion regarding the actual state of units INSIDE Tobruk at the start of December, since some in the area weren't officially part of the garrison.
This wasn't true for all of it, however. Some parts of the raggruppamento are labelled as located at Soluch moving throguh El Abiar and Barce toward Tobruk.
It was able to complete this journey by the end of the month. The 25th Corps Artillery Raggruppamento with a couple attached I Groups was also on the way at Berta.

Let's clarify some things about the concept of the "static machine gun company". These were interval units, holding between fortifications, not the fortifications themselves.
Thus it is wrong to call the strongpoints of the G.a.F. static machine gun companies since they were not that. They were static, and had machine guns, but were organized differently and had artillery as well.
So, none of these companies showed up in the major fortifications of Tripoli, Tobruk, and Bardia. Instead they were positioned along the extending borders with Egypt and Tunisia.
Also, the static ones were numbered 50 or more. Those numbered less than 50 were not static though they defintely still were lacking in terms of mobility.
I won't bother transcribing the location of all of these companies since some aren't known anyway, but Montanari's "The First British Offensive" has sketches with many of their locations.

The Volontari della Libia CC.NN. Battalion and unknown elements of the Sirte Division were together at Martuba on the 1st of December 1940.
At the same time, the XV Anti-Aircraft Group was at Apollonia and a labor battalion of unknown designation was at d'Annunzio, and the 1st Libyan Complement Battalion (a unique unit) was at Benghazi.

If I ever need information about the combat in Yugoslavia, note that the Italians made use of numerous auxiliary elements formed from the local population.
For details about these units, they're at the end of Ness's document about Italian foreign and colonial units on tacticalorganization.com, in a chart for each sponsoring division.

I missed this previously, but on page 31 of "The First British Offensive", Montanari, relaying a Graziani message, notes that 25th CAR (4 149/13 groups) was meant to be used against the Mersa Matruh defenses.
Only two of the groups, the 105th and 150th, which were complete except for a few missing trucks, were able to land in Behgazi on the 27th of October.
The other two had landed their personnel in Benghazi, but all of their equipment was still stuck in Tripoli aboard the ships there. It seems like the delay that kept them there probably cleared up quickly.

This (the gun breakdown above) sucks though, because the fact that the I/22 Group had just 11 100mm guns implies that an extra 100/17 battery existed somewhere. I don't know where it might be.
Type AS 1940 Divisions had a platoon of 45/5 mortars in each battalion (3 mortars) and a company of 81/14 mortars in each regiment (3 platoons, each 2 squads, each 1 mortar).

In many of my later theories, the 105/28 guns placed as part of the so-called "Mixed" G.a.F. group in the sketches is in fact a section detached from the 22nd CAR.
If this was true, though, the guns were not part of the G.a.F. It's apparent based on another sketch that these guns, part of "4 pezzi", were not part of the G.a.F or of the 22nd CAR at the time.

The mixed engineer units of the G.a.F. were in fact either companies or independent platoons. The companies would have a platoon for each subsector assigned to its parent sector.
In the case of the XXXI Mixed Engineer Unit, this implies that it had just two platoons, one for XXXI/A and one for XXXI/B. They were probably of different sizes, but how can you know.
These platoons were also named according to this system, so their names would've been "A" and "B". It is important to remember that this structure was administrative only and did not apply in combat.

I hate my life. It's spelled "Caposaldo" not "Capisaldo". However, the plural has both of the "o"s replaced by "i"s which creates confusion. I'm still not that familiar with Italian.

Training Heavy Mortar Squad (81/14)
Also, Niehorster is back up. That's good news that link rot hasn't worsened by too much.
The Libyan Coastal Battalions had absolutely zero heavy weapons. They did have a few machine guns in their rifle platoons, but they had no mortars or anti-tank guns.

I've been trying to track the origin of the Mixed Battery composed of 2 77/28 guns and a 149/12 gun. This may have been the "Mixed Battery" supposedly part of the 22nd CAR.
At the same time, both the 77/28 and 149/12 were guns used by the G.a.F., as no other 149/12 guns appear anywhere in Africa as part of the regular army.
The G.a.F. in Tripoli was noted having several 149/12 units by Montanari while there was also a battery in Tobruk. While this "battery" managed to only have one gun, it still existed.
This makes it seem like these guns had been in the G.a.F. depots from previously disbanded batteries. Thus in the "december" scenario it's fair to assume the guns could still be found.
But so was the entirety of the 22nd CAR. And I haven't deployed any of those units because they weren't part of the G.a.F. And this opens the door to a thousand more units that I can't all track.
This gives the 22nd CAR the total OOB of:
1st Group (11 100/17)
Composite Battery (4 105/28)
Mixed Training Battery (2 77/28, 1 149/12)
13th Anti-Aircraft Battery (8 20/65)
Provisional Anti-Tank Battery (6 47/32)
This totals 32 guns, a cool number I guess. They are from 7 batteries, a significantly less cool number. In designing the "december" scenario I can't really add these.

I've some information (possibly because of mistakes by me) that shows 120/40 guns in Africa. I don't thinks ever happened, least of all in the "Nembo" battery.
The "Nembo" itself was built not with 120/40 guns but with 120/45. This was a naval model and was effectively never seen on land. The 120/40 itself was generally a pretty normal artillery gun.
Worse, it would also force me into adding in all the cooked random gun elements, like the 3 75/27 with Point 144, the 2 77/28 and 2 47/32 with Point 71, and others.
I can almost certainly be sure, though, that the 77/28 section that was acting in an anti-tank role at Point 71 was probably of the G.a.F., since the regular army used these only in the Libyan units.
Even then, though, it could be a strange semi-organic unit to the 31st Libyan Coastal Battalion, which would honestly be really cool if true but it probably isn't.

I originally thought the extra 3 100/17 guns above the 60 that would be expected (as Montanari lists 63) would've just been random guns assigned to random batteries.
However, I later learned that the G.a.F. had 11 100/17s in early January. This would've been the I/22nd CAR Group, meaning that the +3 guns is not +4 since the I/22nd would be missing one.
I thus have no choice but to accept that there probably was a bonus battery lying around that I've never accounted for anywhere else. I looked around for any groups that seem to be missing a battery.
I found the missing battery in the I Group of the 12th "Sila" Artillery Regiment. I can't know its number but we'll go with the 3rd battery being the one that ended up with the I/26th Group.

The old 340-gun number is a little cursed and in current versions I've often ignored it. But there is at least some historical basis for its existence.
That is, if we ignore the 39 AT guns inside the tanks and add instead the 42 20/65 (or 20mm in general) guns, the total moves from 337 artillery and anti-tank guns to 340.
While Niehorster doesn't include this information, the standard Italian artillery battery consisted of a firing battery (platoon level), along with a headquarters and train.
The firing battery represented about 50% of the strength of the battery as a whole, with 71 out of 128 men. I don't know if the Guardia alla Frontiera followed the same rules.
Ultimately, though, I elected not to downgrade the 105/28 battery of the 22nd CAR down to a firing battery alone. While it's noted as "4 pezzi" in one sketch, Rostagno calls it a full battery.

The 63rd Replacement Battalion managed to last long enough to be destroyed at the Derna position. A "training battalion" of the 115th Infantry Regiment's Depot (maybe the 62nd Replacement Battalion).
In general, the addition of this extra battery has been catalyst for an increase from 144/72 to 145/72. I haven't found any reasonable way to reduce this except by blowing up the "Piave" company.

The 2 77/28 guns at Point 71 are not only mentioned in Montanari's map. Their existence is confirmed by a message from Pitassi Manella calling for them to fire on the troops attacking his headquarters.
The Free French troops besieging Tobruk, were, according to Montanari, placed under the command of the 7th Support Group. No 7th Armoured Division information yet supports this.

While some Bersaglieri units might have had a decent amount of machine guns or maybe even some extra, there were no units of a special MG TOE. I was wrong about this when I thought the 11th Battalion had this.

The 65/17 was used as a standard mountain gun in World War I. By the second war, the 75/13, an Austro-Hungarian weapon, had replaced it in the mountain infantry support role.
This is why the 65/17 became an infantry gun, replacing the 37/10 (of M11/39 fame) which had evidently become two weak and obsolete for use in a modern war, though the 65/17 was near obsolete as well.

According to Montanari's maps of Crusader, Amseat was not a single place, but rather, a geographical feature, probably a ridge. This may be why I could never find its precise location.
The Giovani Fascisti regiment had a 47/32 anti-tank company and 81/41 mortar company formed for it prior to Crusader. I think these units would've been with the regiment/battalion group at Bir el Gubi.
In my (reduced) world, the Raggruppamento Artiglieria "Sirte" was formed from reforming the remnants of the G.a.F. artillery groups, specifically the 6th and 7th Groups.
Since these groups would've lost all of their guns in Compass, they would need to be replenished with three fresh artillery batteries each. These would probably be 75/27 or 77/28 groups left by other G.a.F.
Since in my world these would've been from the Tobruk Fortress and not from the G.a.F. on the Tunisian border (Nalut-Zuara sector), so this would be 77/28 guns (there were no 75/27 in Tripoli).

Based on this information, I can't really know anything about the 283rd and 284th Groups that were sent with the Sabratha Division.
Two groups with these numbers do show up with the 280th Raggruppamento on the 23rd of December (Ascoli page 361), but both of them are given 77/28 guns so they probably aren't the same.

The 580th Reconaissance Company (sometimes incorrectly called a battalion) had a pioneer platoon, armored car platoon, and anti-tank platoon. This was a pretty abnormal organization.
Other than the 200th and 361st Light Infantry Regiments, Menny during Gazala had the 533rd Coastal Artillery Battalion, the 3rd Company of the 523rd Coastal Artillery Battalion, and the 612th Flak Battalion.
These two coastal artillery battalions were two of four that Rommel's corps had received. All were armed with captured 15cm French guns and rarely if ever show up in the narrative.

Some claim that the two (200th and 361st) infantry regiments also had engineer companies, but I find this hard to believe. There were no such spare engineer companies lying around in the Afrika Korps.
The panzergrenadier regiments of the 15th and 21st Panzer Divsiions did have a 14th engineer company, but they had three battalions and were the only infantry regiments in their divisions.
On the other hand, the 90th Light Division was already short of engineers with only the weak 900th Pioneer Battalion and no loose ones that could be grabbed up.

The 3rd Indian Motor Brigade was supported during Sonnenblume by a company from the 2/3rd Australian Anti-Tank Regiment and the 35th Field Squadron, Royal Engineers.

For the Polish Independent Carpathian Brigade, unsurprisingly, Polish Wikipedia has a lot more detail about its sub-elements and their commanders than English Wikipedia does.
It had the following exact structure, all taken from Polish Wikipedia:
Brigade Headquarters
Note: the "Carpathian" designation wasn't just a name, the units did actually have a light mountain disposition
    Brigade Infantry Command - Walenty Peszek (Dowództwo Piechoty Brygady)
        1st Carpathian Rifle Battalion (1st-4th Companies)
        2nd Carpathian Rifle Battalion (1st-4th Companies)
        3rd Carpathian Rifle Battalion (1st-4th Companies)
            4th Company - Czesław Hanus
    Independent Heavy Machine Gun Company - Malik Somchjanc (Samodzielna Kompania Ciężkich Karabinów Maszynowych)
    Carpathian Artillery Regiment - Stanisław Gliwicz
        1st Light Artillery Squadron - Juliusz Możdżeń (I Dywizjon Artylerii Lekkiej) (3 Batteries of 4 Guns Each)
        2nd Heavy Artillery Squadron - Franciszek Kikal (II Dywizjon Artylerii Ciężkiej) (3 Batteries of 4 Guns Each)
        3rd Horse Artillery Squadron - Emil Sikorski (III Dywizjon Artylerii Konnej) (3 Batteries of 4 Guns Each)
        Note: these different squadron names didn't actually mean anything
        Note: the batteries were numbered 1-9
        Note: under Polish systems, this would've been considered a regiment-level unit, and while the British would say the same, in their terminology that's a battalion
    Carpathian Anti-Tank Artillery Squadron - Cieszkowski (Karpacki Dywizjon Artylerii Przeciwpancernej)
        Note: this actually had subordinate batteries numbered 1-4, so it was a regiment-level unit by Polish standards and company-level by British standards
        Note: each of those batteries was armed with 4 guns, so they were troop-level by British standards, and this unit was formed in June 1941
    Carpathian Uhlans Regiment (Motorized acting as reconaissance)
        1st Squadron - Stanisław Kałusowski
        2nd Squadron - Antoni Smodlibowski
        3rd Squadron - Antoni Kawecki
    Sapper Squad (Oddział Saperów) - Władysław Rakowski (Company Size)
    Total: 27 Companies

The 56th Independent Anti-Air Group was probably the largest battalion-level unit I've seen in the Italian Army, with 7 mobile and 14 static batteries.
Similarly, the G.a.F. Coverage Sector "Dodecaneso" had 18 static machine gun companies, each of which was functionally independent and guarded various islands.

According to the "History of the Artillery", the reason for the confusing and inconsistent number of naval batteries at Tobruk is that different authors and categorizations are counting differently.
Only 7 batteries were of an anti-ship nature, of the total 11 that were to defend the harbor and its surroundins. Sometimes the San Giorgio is counted in these numbers as well.
I would assume the batteries that couldn't fire on ships were the ones that were inland: Tordo, Toro, Pilastrino, and Sidi Mahmud, since how would they hit a ship anyway?
I don't think any of the fortifications surrounding the town ever even had more than one anti-tank gun. That may have been a mistake on the part of the British/Italian mapper.
The ones facing the Wadi es Sehel also supposedly lacked anti-tank guns, since that area wasn't considered passable to tanks in the first place. The British map also got this wrong.
The map from "The Operations in North Africa" also supports this, as no anti-tank ditch or mines are marked in that area and that would probably imply a lack of anti-tank weaponry as a whole.
Each fortification initially also had two machine guns. We know this was still the case by the time the actual attack occurred since the six positions that fell first had 12 MGs between them.
This book also makes a different mistake, however, in taking Montanari's maps (or the sources he used) literally. When half a group's area is marked as lost, that doesn't mean exactly half of it was lost.
More likely, either all of it was lost as it was completely overrun, or the entire group managed to survive, for now at least. It wouldn't make sense for the Australians to just leave the rest alone.

He also notes that 22 47/32 guns out of the 43 total were taken from the "artillery magazine" which may refer to the depots of the G.a.F. or from the strongpoints.
I'll go over this some more two paragraphs later once I finish reading it. But this does annoy me since it certainly isn't the same number I had previously.
The 160 men that Vietina had to defend the port did not include the 3rd Battalion, 62nd Replacement Battalion. The grammar Montanari uses in his two books are slightly different on this.

Also, whichever guy on Wikipedia wrote that the 3rd Battalion, 70th Infantry Regiment defended the second-line positions misread the book.
In reality, this was the 2nd Battalion, 70th Infantry Regiment and it was only responsible for defending one position, that of Pilastrino, alongside the 61st Machine Gun Battalion.
The 3rd Battalion, 69th Infantry Regiment faced toward Bardia as part of the El Adem Crossroads position, while the elements of the 4th Tank Infantry Regiment mostly faced southward toward El Adem.
The Bejad position with the 64th Replacement Battalion is noted as containing only a single platoon of machine guns and no mortars, rather than the entire support weapons company.
The 3rd Battalion, 69th Infantry Regiment was also the one that had the 76/40 gun with it. I guess since the dug-in tanks had weak 37/10 guns of their own, they didn't need extra anit-tank support.

Unlike many other nations, the divisions of the Italian Army did not have any kind of brigade headquarters inside them (such as an infantry, tank, or artillery command).
To be fair, these divisions were almost brigade-sized anyway, so them having an additional brigade headquarters wouldn't have resulted in much of a benefit to the command structure anyway.
On page 293 of "The Operations", which discusses the distribution of spare guns found in the Tobruk stores, Montanari links to note 165. It was actually quite difficult to find this.

254 MGs (Fiat 35), 41 45/5 Mortars, 12 81/14 Mortars

20 77mm MGs, 30 127mm MGs (taken from inoperable aircraft, ineffective in the ground role but used anyway)

The Sirte Division's commander in early 1939 was Eduardo Quarra Sito. He then became commander of the Tripoli Fortress which he stayed iin until November 1941.
At that point, Tripoli's command was instead given to Alberto Mannerini, and Quarra Sito would go on to command some assorted infantry divisions before retiring in 1943.

Somewhere I've seen claims that the San Giorgio had 147mm guns rather than 254mm guns. I have no idea where this claim originated but it's almost certainly a mistake and can be ignored.

Some things to say about the strength of blackshirt units:
Volontari della Libia: About 658 men (21 officers, 36 NCOs)
Libyan Legionary Battalion: 734 men (29 officers, 27 NCOs)

Also, still no information on the 65/17 battery that the 55th Artillery Regiment had. Even its numbers don't help, since at least 1 of its 5 guns came from the Tobruk depot rather than being organic.
Thus the actual quantity of guns that this battery has is unknown but is probably just 4, and thus there's no real way to know where it came from.
But I did find something of value. On page 242 of "History of the Artillery", the "23 Marzo" Division and the XXIII Corps each left behind a 65/17 battery. Maybe it's one of these that stayed in Tobruk?
The "23 Marzo" Division had the 15th and 16th batteries from the Savona Division (to be replaced with the 266th and 267th by Crusader). It could be one of these, but can I find anything to differentiate them?
It was probably the 15th, though, since the other battery, numerically at least, would have a high chance of being deployed to the 2nd Blackshirt Division's artillery rather than the 1st "23 Marzo"'s.
There was also a weird 270th 65/17 battery that was either almost entirely or truly entirely lost in combat at Sidi Barrani. Maybe this was a G.a.F. unit?
Further, the Cirene Division initially had four batteries of 65/17, two organic and two reinforcement, the 69th and 70th coming from the Sirte Division. But once it reached Sofafi, it had only three.

It seems that by the Second Battle of El Alamein, the 15th Panzer Division's 33rd Artillery Regiment's 3rd Battalion no longer had 3 batteries of heavy guns. Rather only two of its batteries were heavy.
I can't implement this, though, since I don't know when this change occurred and it's so minor that researching this would be fairly difficult, even though the Germans are better documented.

The Sirte Division surrendered with about 200 trucks lying around. These were probably enough to motorize the mobile reserve of the Tobruk fortress but not much else.
Interestingly, the division had many more trucks earlier, hence why it was able to make the journey from the Tunisian border to Tobruk. There, most of the trucks were stripped and given to the 1st Blackshirt Battalion.

Let's talk about the infantry gun units that ended up with the Sabratha Division when it defended the Derna line. It should've had the 85th and 86th batteries at normal formation.
Montanari's maps show the following companies: 251st, 254th, 255th, 85th (Motorized). Note the curious lack of an 86th company, perhaps it had made it all the way to Tobruk?

Also finally I'm pretty sure that "di Formazione" doesn't necessarily mean a training unit. It can also just mean an ad-hoc unit in general.
Thus the force at the "Piave" strongpoint probably did not come from training at the depots. Instead, it was just a jumble of platoons and squads of assorted support weapons elements.

Several times I've had doubts about the amount of G.a.F. infantry or strongpoints that were taken forward to replenish the 30th/B Coverage Sector (likely taking the 32nd Coverage Sector HQ with them).
But this shouldn't be the case. Castagna says both that the original garrisons consisted of four companies of the 150th Blackshirt Battalion and alter has four G.a.F. companies to add to his Libyan units.
He later reorganized the defensive formation, as by this time many of his Libyan units had shrunk considerably from losses and desertions. Now there were just four strongpoints, each of which had a G.a.F. company.

Based on forum discussion about the tanks in Operation Compass, I've gained some information but nothing that I conclusively need. Here's what I have:
Earlier, the I Medium Tank Battalion had 40 tanks in three companies. Two of the companies were in Tobruk.
It moved to Tobruk by orad on the 13th of July 1940 and by the 17th of December, it had just 5 effective tanks at Gazala with the rest, immobilized, left in Tobruk.
The 39 immobilized tanks and the personnel that hadn't been sent to Bengasi that formed it at Tobruk came from both the 1st and 2nd Medium Tank Battalions.
In this case, it may make sense for me to apply the composite company formation to these just like I did for the 63rd.
The parts of the 63rd Light Tank Battalion that weren't at Tobruk were almost entirely destroyed at Tishdida which presumably is near Buq Buq.

While the 75/46 gun used a pretty common (75mm) caliber and was attempting to be produced, and in Tobruk it was used in a sort of anti-tank role, anti-tank ammunition for them was not produced until 1942.
I do find this a bit weird, though, since many 75/27 or 77/28 guns were also being used in an anti-tank role. It seems possible but very stupid if none of these had any anti-tank ammo either.

Unlike "The First British Offensive", the later published "The Operations in North Africa" series does actually have indexes (indices?) for units rather than just random stuff, though by now they are obsolete due to ctrl+F.
Worse, even if they were at least somewhat useful in finding stuff, all they tell me is that everything the book has on the topics I care about I have already seen, and that it has nothing new to offer.

I'm now reading through the diaries of a Phil who was in the 2/7th Australian Battalion and was at Tobruk. Here's some things that he notes (though they're probably not the most reliable):
The artillery at Tobruk was not only greater in quantity than in Bardia but was more accurate and aggressive, maybe due to Pitassi Manella's leadership.
The Italians built several large rock or concrete structures that were left around in the desert. It was thought these were used for rangefinding and the Australians couldn't destroy them.
His company was only given 5 posts to capture and then it was placed in reserve. Those were B1-B5, which aren't mentioned according to Italian maps so are probably Australian-given designations.
The last post they went to (probably not one they were assigned to capture) was very impressively built into the side of a wadi with deeply concealed weapons emplacements.
Each Italian anti-aircraft gun (20/6) used differently-colored tracer ammunition to track its shots, making them quite pretty against the night sky.

When Tobruk fell, to the Axis this time, the 25th Field Regiment had the 12th/25th, 31st/58th, and 287th Batteries (note that 12/25 and 31/58 are misrepresented by TIK) deployed on the 2nd line around King's Cross.
Note that only the first 2 batteries, both composite in nature, were from the regiment. The 287th Battery came from the 124th (Northumbrian) Field Regiment from the 50th Division.

Doing a bit of reading from Montanari's writing on the later stages of the desert war, more specifically "The Second Italo-German Counteroffensive" which covers the time between Crusader's end and September 1942.
Here's what I've gained from that:
By this point, the Trieste Division had just 4732 men left, plus 1198 drivers. No wonder that its regiments were reduced to a strength of four companies each, in two battalions.
The original AS 42 infantry division was meant to do the same with its infantry, having a standard strength of either 3373 or 3704 men (depending on source) and 8 infantry companies.
It was hoped that eventually reinforcements would be fed into the divisions to bring their battalions back up to four companies each and these replacement companies began forming in Italy.
The Trento Division in the spring of 1942 was badly understrength in artillery, with its four groups having just two batteries apiece.
The other divisions varied significantly and none met the target strength of 931 men manning 2 75/27 and 1 100/17 groups of two batteries. In June the third battery was readded as a goal.

It also includes a rare OOB for Panzerarmee Afrika on the 20th of January 1942. Here it is:

Changes to be made:
AS 1942 division reorganizations
III/33rd Artillery Regiment was mixed
317th Group of 75/27 had just 2 batteries

Unrelated, but Gotti commanded the Trento Division into February 1942 so he should be named in the Rommel's Second Offensive scenario.
The XXI Corps during Gazala, responsible for defending the sector from the coast to Sidi Breghisc, was reinforced by a German Tank Battalion.

The Afrika Division was not actually completedly inserted into the Tobruk siege lines at the start of Crusader. Rommel made this decision while literally under attack.
It just goes to show how poorly he handled the battle at its outset, and resulted in the loss of II./IR 255 since it wasn't fully prepared for its defense. Good thing the British were just as bad!

I think it's notable that the general defensive structure that the Italians used to defend Tobruk was almost entirely replicated by the British in the 2nd siege. Here's how similar they were:

I also intend to rebuild the Siege of Tobruk event since it's now severley outdated. Here's the changes to make:
Source 1: this ugly map https://cove.army.gov.au/article/80-years-remembering-rats-tobruk
Source 2: Montanari, "The First Italo-German Counteroffensive in North Africa"
Restructure the artillery. In the historical siege there were 3 RHA Field Regiments and 1 RA Field Regiment that were spread mostly evenly across the fortress.
3rd Armoured Brigade had: 1 Regiment of Armoured Cars, 1 Composite Light Tank Regiment, 1 Composite Cruiser Tank Regiment, 1 Infantry Tank Troop

Here's a bonus number provided by Montanari - 52 guns were facing south (Group Tramontin?)
    2nd Group, 43rd Artillery Regiment (12 75/27)
    105th Group, 25th Corps Artillery Raggruppamento (12 149/13)
    3rd Group, 55th Artillery Regiment (12 75/27)
    130th Group, 25th Corps Artillery Raggruppamento (12 149/13)
    2nd Battery, 15th Anti-Aircraft Group (4 75/46)

Elements of the 14th Air Brigade "Rex" were stuck in Tobruk and lost, but the headquarters were not there, and in fact it doesn't seem any administrative elements were actually in the base.
Instead, by the 1st of April 1941, the brigade was still active, now based in Tripoli, with four groups of squadrons assigned to it, with a total of 123 aircraft.
Also on the 1st of April 1941, Fortress Tripoli had a total strength of 7936 men, 361 officers, and 474 NCOs for 8771 personnel total. I believe by this point much of it had already been reorganized out.
Back on the 20th of February, it was slightly weaker, actually, with 7652 men, 343 officers, and 421 NCOs for 8416 personnel total. I don't really get how this happened, where did the 355 men come from?

Also, yes, the carabinieri sections were platoon-sized. The Ariete Division had two when it arrived in Africa and both of them had a strength of 66 men (each).

Changes to be made/takeaways:
The DICAT groups (except maybe the 201st) were not destroyed in Tobruk
(Some records actually say that they were taken out of North Africa entirely and redeployed later in March 1941)
The 8th Labor Battalion, if any of its units were at Tobruk, had companies of number 31 or higher (31st and 32nd, maybe)
551st Machine Gun Battalion was designated "Motorized"
The Pavia Division had the replacement 36th and 90th 65/17 infantry gun companies
The Bologna Division had the replacement 29th and 91th 65/17 infantry gun companies
The 27th, 503rd, and 504th Independent Batteries were actually 20mm companies, so it's time to redo the Savona Division in all existing scenarios
The 8th is never mentioned, but since it only had 2 guns it was probably larger caliber than 20mm but still an anti-aircraft unit (75/34?)
Still, whiel the German information is kinda useless in terms of unit types, the numbers of guns may still be useful information
Mario de Meo was originally from the Zuara Sector before he became commander of RECAM
The 2nd Group, 26th Artillery Regiment "Pavia" was not destroyed in Compass but stayed behind
Garrison companies were regular army units that could be formed by regular infantry divisions
The 16th Infantry Regiment didn't have a mortar company for some reason

Highly unrelated, but the word for "ships" in Italian, while I never thought about this beforehand, it now makes sense that it's "navi". Kinda funny and interesting for me.

There's also a listing of commanders back on February 20 1941. This includes the following interesting ones:
Giuseppe Cresmascoli and Mario Marghinotti - Brescia and Savona Divisions (Marghinotti is never mentioned by Wikipedia, perhaps he was temporary)
Parii Dino - Colonel, commander of some element of the G.a.F.
Leo Michele - Commander of the Libyan Sahara

Also, the 8th Bersaglieri Battalion of the 7th Bersaglieri Regiment, which was undergoing reformation prior to Crusader, had the following strength of the 27th of October 1941:
1 Motorized Battalion With 24 Submachine Guns, 10 Anti-Tank Rifles, 6 HMGs, and 8 47/32 Anti-Tank Guns
It was at Gialo, along with another platoon of 47/32, 2 machine gun platoons of the 18th Libyan Battalion, 1 section of 20/65, and 3 12.7mm machine guns.

Note that on the 1st of April 1941, the Brescia Division had the following gun units:

5th Anti-Tank Company (47/32)
71st Anti-Tank Company (47/32)
227th Anti-Tank Company (47/32) (in Preparation)
1st Fast Artillery Regiment
    1st Group (2 Batteries 75/27)
    2nd Group (2 Batteries 75/27)
    7th 20mm Battery
    8th 20mm Battery
    502nd 20mm Battery

But by the 1st of November, it had been reduced to:

1 Anti-Tank Company (47/32) (Number Unknown)
1st Fast Artillery Regiment
    1st Group (2 Batteries 100/17) (1 Missing)
    2nd Group (3 Batteries 100/17)
    3rd Group (2 Batteries 75/27)
    4th Group (2 Batteries 75/27)
    1 20mm Battery (Number Unknown)

The 63rd Garrison Company has reappeared as part of the ARMIR of all places. Here's how it can be found:
8th Army/ARMIR
    Directly Dependent Units
        Intendancy
            Rear Area (Tappe)
                Rear Area Directorate
                    63rd Garrison Company
                    3 Major Rear Area Commands
                    6 Secondary Rear Area Commands
                    11 Special Rear Area Commands
                    4 Rear Area officers
                    4 Mobile Territorial Battalions (4 Companies Each)
                    3 Mobile Territorial Battalions (3 Companies Each)
                    8 Dismounted Artillery Groups (4 Batteries Each)
One man from this company was a Sergeant L.J. (only initials can be located) who was interrogated by the CSIR for reasons I will probably never know.
Back in the days of the CSIR, I don't beleive the 63rd was around, and instead there was a 22nd Garrison Company that was operating in the rear in the Soviet Union.

In general, though the 70th Garrison Company of the Bologna Division and a separate garrison company from the Sabratha Division have appeared, I've haven't gained much about them.
In fact, almost all information I can find about these units comes almost exclusively from World War I sources which aren't too useful.
This is partially because all infantry divisions under the standard organization during that war had a garrison company assigned to them. Not sure why it was necessary.
Like, too many of them. It's nearly impossible to find anything from World War II. It's almost as if Italian writers, who are the only ones who cover this, aren't too proud of their nation's performance.
Also, the 70th Garrison Company even existing is weird given there was never a 70th Division in the first place. But maybe this was actually formed by the 70th Infantry Regiment of the Sirte Division.
But if that was the case, the 63rd Garrison Company would've been formed not by the 63rd Cirene Division, but by the 59th Cagliari Division's 63rd Infantry Regiment, which was in Greece.

Alternately, it could be the depots of the regiments that formed the companies rather than the regiments themselves. In fact, this possibility seems more likely.
The Cagliari Division did not exist during the East African Campaign, but the 63rd Infantry Regiment did, and it passed through Cyrenaica to get to Eritrea.
The depot was also based in Salerno, which isn't too far from North Africa and is where several other units that ended up in Cyrenaica came from.
Now that this seems quite plausible, I'm gonna make this change. It might not be 100% confirmable as true but it's the best I have for now.
This isn't a great number, but in World War I, garrison companies had both numbers and letters. Those with numbers 1-94 were from infantry regiment depots. Larger numbers came from special regiments.

When it deployed to Rommel's command on the 22nd and 23rd of January, the Sabratha Division had 7 battalions, no anti-tank armament, and a single 75/27 group.

From "The Second Italo-German Counteroffensive", we get confirmation that the Sabratha Division had just 2 battalions in each of its regiments, with no infantry guns (except for the 340th battery, divisional).
There's also a slightly cooked OOB for Gazala. It doesn't include specifics for any divisions, so I'll go with just the things I find interesting:

Series on AA Batteries:
0-99 - Divisional Artillery Regiments
100-299 - Divisional Artillery Regiment Reinforcements
300-399 - ? (Tank Units) (These have never appeared a single time)
