I made the above tentative chart because pages 431-434 of Montanari's "The Third British Offensive" have a ton of references to the 400-series units.
This makes me think the 4th Tank Infantry Regiment would've had the 304th, since these are both number series that I can't otherwise find anything about.
Another 400-series battery, the 437th, can be found with the 205th Artillery Regiment that acted as a replacement for the Bologna Division.

On the 17th of November a major storm hit the Mediterranean from Egypt to Libya. This seemed to me like something of little relevance initially until I read some quotes about its effects.
German troops of the 15th Panzer Division, then deployed near the entrance to Halfaya Pass, were drowned in mud from the first rains in 60 years. The defenders of Tobruk suffered a similar fate.

A Squadron, 7th Hussars was commanded by a Seymour-Evans, while B Squadron, 7th Hussars was commanded by a Younger, at least according to a Crusader report by Brigadier Davy.
I'm not sure because this information is slightly less clear but I think a Major Congreve commanded C Squadron of this same unit.
Seymour-Evans was later injured and was replaced by a Captain R.c. Watson, but he was also injured and command in the squadron collapsed temporarily.
Another injured commander during this phase was Freddie Byass who commanded the regiment, replaced by a Major Fosdick whose origin is yet unknown to me.

Neumann-Silkow's chief of staff in the 15th Panzer Division was Colonel Rainer Kriebel.
A major Godson commanded B Squadron, 4th County of London Yeomanry and was a casualty at Bir el Gubi.
Colonel Bonner Fellers, the "Good Source" was actually at 4th Armoured Brigade HQ early in Crusader. They were unlucky that he wasn't captured.
One of the artillery batteries subordinated to the 70th Infantry Division in Crusader was commanded by a Lieutenant Hester-Hewitt.
The company commanders of the 21st New Zealand Battalion were Major Trousdale, Captain Yeoman, Captain Tongue, and the unknown commander of its D Company.

According to Greene and Massignani, Arditi units in North Africa (and possibly in general) were picked men from existing units and were not formed on their own individually.
So the 2 Arditi platoons of the 2nd Battalion, 69th Infantry Regiment were taken from the best men of that unit (interval troops of the eastern sector) and this wasn't the case for most battalions.

Another thing I was wrong about was the structure of the Australian and New Zealand Artillery.
Australian regiments had 2 batteries each. Each battery included two 4-gun troops of 18-pounders and a single 4-gun troop of 4.5 inch howitzers. The numbers seem to just count up.
Batteries were sequentially numbered from the 1st, in the 2/1st Field Regiment. This makes the numbers quite easy to understand though they did become messier after they switched to three-battery regiments.
The same went for anti-tank regiments and the numbering of their batteries, although each anti-tank regiment had four rather than two batteries.
New Zealand field regiments all had 3 batteries apiece, and the anti-tank regiment had 4. There were also numerous specialized regiments and a huge amount that stayed in New Zealand.
However, the battery numbers are often irregular. Those that fought with the 2nd Division in North Africa are:
4th Field Regiment: 25,26,46
5th Field Regiment: 27,28,47
6th Field Regiment: 29,30,48
7th Anti-Tank Regiment: 31,32,33,34
14th Light Anti-Aircraft Regiment: 41,42,43
Some other units like the 12th Field Regiment and 39th Heavy Mortar Battery were at times part of the division but I haven't seen any reference to them in North Africa.

South African Field Regiments also had three batteries apiece with 8 guns each. However the batteries were often reorganized, such as at Tobruk in 1942 (2nd, 4th, 5th, 8th, 9th, and 12th) batteries present.
But at no point in the entire game currently do South African artillery units appear at the company level, so even though I was wrong about this previously, there's no consequences for it.
The Luftwaffe is often criticized for its use of ground divisions. Yet it's important to note that the British had Royal Artillery Infantry Brigades as well, the 301st-308th.

Based on this, plus other evidence from Carlo Montù's "History of the Artillery", I'm gonna attempt to track down where the 13 65/17 infantry guns that the Tobruk garrison had.
Firstly, the fortress itself had no infantry guns on the 25th of July. The only source of 5 was from the 1st Libyan Division, and there were 4s and 8s all over the place.
However, these 5 guns don't appear clearly anywhere else. It's hard for me to know where another 5 guns could've appeared, and at the same time at least one likely came from the Tobruk depot.
So ultimately I don't think there is anything I can do in this current position. There's no information anywhere about these batteries and I'm not anywhere close to piecing it together. Guess I'll sleep on it...

On the 16th of June ("The Operations in North Africa", page 67), a raggruppamento of 8 65/17 batteries from the XX Army Corps were given to the 10th Army.
These were likely the 15th and 16th "Savona", 19th and 20th "Brescia", 27th and 28th "Pavia", and 69th and 70th "Sirte". I don't know why it was decided not to move the ones from the Bologna Division as well.
Since the Bologna Division had its infantry gun batteries replaced by the 29th and 91st, they must've been lost at some point. I think it now makes sense to say they were the ones the Sirte Division got as replacement.
Its noted that the Libyan Divisions Group (Gallina) and XXI Corps (Spatocco) had a total of 12 65/17 accompanying batteries (so infantry guns, not artillery), and I think they were:

One thing I apparently just forgot to do was implement was the 12 81/14 mortars that were found in the depots of the Tobruk base. I implemented the guns of all types, but not the mortars or the MGs.
As the 61st Mortar Company was at full strength (6), these 12 would be evenly split between the 141st and 142nd. We can assume the quality of those two companies to be questionable but they were not missing any arms.

When the Trento Division's 2nd Battalion, 62nd Infantry Regiment was deployed to the Bardia-Halfaya-Sidi Omar area, it had a 47/32 company and 20/65 section with it of unknown numbers.
Annoyingly, this is mentioned in several different places in "The First Italo-German Counteroffensive" as well as the 2 47/32 companies investing Tobruk, but none of them ever include numbers.

Notes on the grammar surrounding "Caposaldo", with plural "Capisaldi":
Location / Point - "di" (e.g. caposaldo di q. 144)
Named - nothing (e.g. caposaldo "Piave")
Non-proper location - "del" (e.g. caposaldo del bivio di el Adem)

In late March, Rommel submitted requests for reinforcements that were intended to arrive for Gazala. These were mostly used to replace losses taken during Crusader and regularize the divisions.
For example, a tank company for the 5th Panzer Regiment arrived, as did the 707th Heavy Infantry Gun Company and the 11th (Infantry Gun) Company, 115th Rifle Regiment.
In addition, the 200th Pioneer Battalion received its 3rd Company, plus Hecker's 778th Landing Engineer Company. There were also many signals units sent.
Two final units sent over of interest were the headquarters and staff of the 104th Artillery Command and the 2nd Battalion, 115th Artillery Regiment, which may have lacked them previously.
This full document which also includes requests for reinforcements for the Italian units and other concerns is in "The Second Italo-German Counteroffensive", page 332-335.

Other documents from this book exist but I think I've gotten through most of the value. For instance there are some about British units but they tend to not be the most reliable in their information.
A good example of this is the Britsih 12th Division that was identified in the delta with the 36th and 37th Infantry Brigades and the 114th Field Regiment. In reality, this division had been disbanded in 1940.

It's worth noting that the Italian units that were operating near Matruh didn't attack it frontally until the encirclement was complete. Thus their losses were nearly nonexistent in this battle.
The X Corps spent the few days of battle making slow progress through the minefields, while the XXI Corps faced off against the enemy in an artillery duel with the infantry doing little to nothing.

Italian intelligence also made numerous failures prior to Gazala. We know that Rommel's German staff did no better and the whole plan for Operation Venice was poor but the Italians are not blameless.
For one, they had extremely outdated information regarding the British units, with many of the commanders who had changed in early 1942 not being accounted for. It was so bad that it's honestly comical.
Some of those mistakes include naming Godwin-Austen for XIII Corps, Brink for 1st South African Division, Lumsden for 7th Armoured Division, andGott for 1st Armoured Division.
They also wrong about the organization in many places. They thought the armoured divisions still had support groups and didn't realize the army tank brigades weren't just loose tank battalions/regiments.

The command organization between Umberto Barberis and the various 10th Army units is still kind of unclear. Barberis was meant to be commander of the frontier, but also appears as commander of Tobruk Fortress.
I think the most reasonable assumption for this is that he was initially in command of the frontier garrisons, but was redeployed to command Tobruk Fortress after the frontier was lost in the early war.

"The Italian Army Between the 1st and 2nd World Wars", another book from the Army Historical Office, has a lot of assorted data, charts, and maps from the 10th of June.
One interesting thing to note is that none of the guns in AFrica were rare. The 149/12 could be found in hundreds as could the 149/13 and 149/35, even though all of these guns were short in Africa.
On the other hand, the 149/19 was never found in Africa. This makes sense because apparently there was only a single one of these across the entire army. Why not just retire it?

The book also shows that the amount of artillery in the Italian divisions, while never a specialty, increased relative to the quantity of infantry battalions over time.
This is to challenge the idea that the binary division was a worse organization than the ternary or quaternary division. Artillery support is important and this shows a genuine trend toward better-built divisions.
Here are the numbers to prove it:
Quaternary Division (1918): 12 Infantry Battalions, 8 Artillery Batteries (0.67)
Ternary Division (1926): 9 Infantry Battalions, 12 Artillery Batteries (1.33)
Binary Division (1938): 6 Infantry Battalions, 9 Artillery Batteries (1.5)

Here are some other nations' 1940 or 1941 (1939 for Poland) divisions for comparison:
German Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 9 Artillery Batteries (1)
Dutch Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 9 Artillery Batteries (1)
Hungarian Division: 6 Infantry Battalions, 7 Artillery Batteries (1.17)
Polish Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 10.5 Artillery Batteries (11 Batteries, but 2 have only 3 guns) (1.17)
American Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 12 Artillery Batteries (1.33)
Japanese Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 12 Artillery Batteries (1.33)
Belgian Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 12 Artillery Batteries (Reserve divisions had only half as much) (1.33)
Soviet Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 15 Artillery Batteries (1.67)
French Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 15 Artillery Batteries (1.67)
Yugoslav Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 16 Artillery Batteries (1.78)
British Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 18 Artillery Troops (same size as an Italian battery) (2)

And some random divisions I ended up reading about anyway even though they didn't participate in the war:
Latvian Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 3 Artillery Batteries (0.33)
Lithuanian Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 6 Artillery Batteries (0.67)
Turkish Division: 9 Infantry Battalions, 6 Artillery Batteries (0.67)
(Yeah, the nations that didn't participate in the war had goods reasons to. Their armies were often underequipped and the lack of aritllery is an example of that)

The binary structure also had other advantages. Each one had 24 guns of 47/32 or 65/17 compared to the 20 that a ternary division would've had. These guns were also hopefully (but not always) more standardized.
Furthermore, it better fit the intentions of the tactical use of the division. The Italians didn't see it as the base maneuver unit and wanted each division to function more in terms of shock.
Rather, the corps would act as the basis of maneuver. This also bypassed the issue of poor commanders since divisional commanders were not given the difficult task of running a maneuver division.
